=== PAGE 8 ===
Introduction by The Rt Hon
the Baroness Hallett DBE

=== PAGE 9 ===
                                        Introduction by The Rt Hon the Baroness Hallett DBE




An effective test, trace and isolate system is well recognised as a major tool in the armoury
of pandemic response. When Covid-19 reached the UK, had a robust test, trace and isolate
system been in place, it would have identified positive cases, traced their contacts and
ensured that all those affected were isolated from others as quickly as possible. In the
absence of a vaccine, this was the only way to stop the new and deadly virus taking hold
of the country.

Yet the UK entered the Covid-19 pandemic woefully ill prepared to implement effective,
nationwide test, trace and isolate systems. It had the scientific expertise – Public Health
England developed a reliable test within days of the identification of the genome of the
virus that causes Covid-19 – but it lacked the infrastructure and equipment required to test
and trace at scale.

Previous pandemic preparedness exercises had highlighted the need for a system that
could be scaled up to the level required to meet the demands of a pandemic. Yet, when
Covid-19 emerged, the existing test, trace and isolate system in the UK could only handle
a relatively small number of cases. There were no adequate plans to test people who might
have been infected, to trace their contacts or to isolate. As the number of cases began to
rise exponentially, capacity for testing, tracing and isolation was soon overwhelmed.

This meant that, just as cases began to spike in the UK, community testing had to be
paused in March 2020 and tests restricted to specific groups such as healthcare workers.
Without the surveillance of the population offered by community testing, decision-makers
had no idea of the extent of community transmission and any attempt at containment of the
virus was doomed to fail.

When they realised the scale of the crisis, the four governments of the UK invested
considerable time, effort and resources in developing a scaled-up system of test, trace and
isolate measures that could meet the demands of a pandemic. Once established, it
was extremely effective. However, their initial efforts were hindered by the lack of planning,
a lack of knowledge of the existing testing capabilities of the private and public sectors,
and a failure to work with them and local authorities with expertise in contact tracing.

Contact tracing can be either manual or digital. Manual contact tracing during the Covid-19
pandemic did not always make adequate use of local authorities or health boards. Digital
contact tracing (through mobile phone apps) failed to take adequate account of language
barriers or the challenges some people would face in having access to a phone or digital
device. At the same time, digital contact tracing was hindered by concerns about privacy
and mistrust in the effectiveness of the apps. Engagement was ultimately much lower than
expected.

For those advised to isolate, many felt forgotten and let down by the lack of practical
or financial support when trying to follow the rules. I heard distressing accounts from
individuals who were faced with the unenviable choice of self-isolating and losing income,
or going to work and risking spreading the virus.


                                                                                                 vii

=== PAGE 10 ===
       Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




       Unclear messaging left sections of the population uncertain about how to comply with the
       measures, including what symptoms to look for before taking a test, or how to access vital
       support during self-isolation. Many people found themselves unable to access the test,
       trace and isolate system designed to protect them from Covid-19. It is therefore unsurprising
       that levels of compliance with self-isolation measures were low during the pandemic.

       The response to Covid-19 marks an extraordinary moment in modern history:
       unprecedented restrictions and expectations were placed on the entire population.
       The UK’s effort to fight the virus demanded not only compliance, but a profound shift in
       daily life. Without high levels of compliance, the entire test, trace and isolate system risked
       breaking down.

       As discussed in earlier modules, the UK government and devolved administrations must
       be better prepared for the next pandemic, drawing on lessons from the past and from
       pandemic preparedness exercises. My overarching recommendation in this Report is that
       the UK government and the devolved administrations must work together now to formulate
       a comprehensive and adaptable test, trace and isolate system, capable of being deployed
       at the outset of any future pandemic.

       This Report and my nine recommendations are only possible thanks to those who provided
       the wealth of evidence I received during my investigation in Module 7. This included
       200 written statements, over 23,000 documents and two expert reports, as well as the
       oral evidence of 41 witnesses at the public hearing in May 2025. I am also grateful to the
       24 Core Participants and the Inquiry’s secretariat and legal team. I offer my sincere thanks
       to them all and especially to those who contributed to the Inquiry’s listening exercise,
       Every Story Matters, and to the impact film.




       The Rt Hon the Baroness Hallett DBE

       19 November 2026

Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      “I believe an effective TTIS [test, trace, isolate and support] programme could have
        played a crucial role in protecting my mother from contracting Covid-19. At the
        time, her domiciliary carers had inadequate access to testing and, even more
        concerningly, lacked the financial and practical support needed to isolate when
        necessary.”1

       Professor Naomi Fulop, Covid-19 Bereaved Families for Justice UK, speaking
       about her mother who died from Covid-19 in January 2021



      “My father was admitted for a gallbladder infection and was subsequently placed
        on a ward where a significant Covid-19 outbreak occurred. His discharge summary
        indicated that he had been ‘potentially exposed to Covid’, yet no testing was
        carried out before his release. This failure to test meant that his infection was
        not identified until his condition deteriorated rapidly at home, leading to his
        readmission and ultimately his death from nosocomial (hospital acquired)
        Covid‑19.”2

       Anna-Louise Marsh-Rees, Covid-19 Bereaved Families for Justice Cymru,
       speaking about her father who died from Covid-19



      “Our members believe that tracing infrastructure was complicated, with existing
        staff often redeployed to other roles. One of our members reported that in the
        Highlands, the lack of staff meant that contact tracing was delayed. In some cases,
        it was delayed for a minimum of one week. Our member believed this delay in
        tracing to be beyond the point of being useful in that it was past the stage that
        anyone could remember everyone that they had been in contact with, and/or
        beyond the point at which the person was still infectious.”3

       Nicola Boyle, Scottish Covid Bereaved, speaking about the delay in contact
       tracing experienced by a person living in the Scottish Highlands

=== PAGE 15 ===
Executive summary

=== PAGE 16 ===
    Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




    It has long been recognised that widespread testing, combined with the rapid isolation
    of infectious individuals, is one of the most effective means of limiting the transmission
    of infectious diseases. Over more than two decades, experience from public health
    emergencies, outbreak responses and pandemic preparedness exercises has consistently
    demonstrated that test, trace and isolate measures, when implemented quickly and at scale,
    can reduce transmission, support front-line services and save lives.

    The UK entered the Covid-19 pandemic underprepared to deliver test, trace and isolate
    measures at scale and at speed.

    The evidence presents a complex picture. While elements of the test, trace and isolate
    systems across the UK were developed quickly and worked effectively, they came too
    late and at a huge cost. The Inquiry has identified significant weaknesses in pandemic
    preparedness and delivery.

    The UK moved at speed to develop a reliable diagnostic test for Covid-19, testing
    the first symptomatic people and confirming clinical diagnoses of the virus as early as
    January 2020. This reflected the strength of the UK’s scientific and research capability.
    However, the development of an effective test for Covid-19 was only the first step.
    Delivering testing at population scale was a greater challenge and proved much harder
    to achieve. As community transmission increased dramatically, the UK’s existing testing
    infrastructure could not be expanded quickly enough to meet demand. The pause in
    widespread community testing in March 2020 came at a time when the virus was spreading
    rapidly, leaving decision-makers to manage the pandemic with limited information about
    cases, transmission and the range of symptoms.

    Future preparedness depends on sustaining and strengthening the expertise of the UK’s
    research and clinical capabilities and the networks that connect them. It is essential that
    the UK builds on the infrastructure developed during the pandemic to ensure that testing
    capacity can be scaled rapidly when needed, without the cost and delay associated with
    building mass testing systems from the ground up and in the middle of a crisis.

    Testing is only effective when coupled with measures to reduce onward transmission.
    Isolation of positive cases was a fundamental component of the UK’s response. However,
    when the UK public was first asked to self-isolate, people were provided with limited
    practical and financial support. Although support mechanisms for self-isolation emerged
    over time, this initial gap weakened adherence and exposed the limitations of relying solely
    on individual responsibility.

    The challenges associated with self-isolation were foreseeable. Planning for the financial
    and practical support required to help people overcome those challenges proved
    inadequate. In a future pandemic, individuals should not be expected to bear the burden
    of isolation without adequate support.




2

=== PAGE 17 ===
                                                                          Executive summary




Alongside testing and isolation, contact tracing played a critical role in efforts to break
chains of transmission. However, as case numbers surged during the first wave of the
pandemic, manual contact tracing infrastructure was unable to keep pace with demand.
The temporary suspension of community contact tracing removed an important mechanism
identifying infected people across the country.

Digital tools offered some mitigation. The UK eventually developed three digital contact
tracing apps, which enhanced the reach and scale achievable through manual contact
tracing alone, but this took valuable time and faced a number of challenges. The
effectiveness of test, trace and isolate systems depends not only on technical capability but
on the broader social, economic and operational context in which they operate. This was
not fully recognised. Certain groups – including older people, ethnic minority groups and
those experiencing digital exclusion – faced barriers to accessing the systems intended to
protect them.

Future systems should be designed with accessibility at their core, combining digital
capabilities with non-digital methods of engagement to ensure that all communities can
be effectively engaged, regardless of their access to technology or confidence in digital
communication.

The UK must learn from the experiences of those who were most affected during the
pandemic, in particular those who were socially and economically disadvantaged. It is
imperative that test, trace and isolate measures are designed to be accessible from the
outset. They must enable access for all communities – including those who may face
barriers – to ensure that future systems are equitable as well as effective. Clear, consistent
and accessible communication is also essential in giving people the best possible chance to
understand the importance of test, trace and isolate measures, why they are necessary and
what they are being asked to do.

Test, trace and isolate capabilities have an important role beyond domestic transmission
control. When applied proportionately and based on evidence, they can help identify and
manage risks associated with international travel, providing an additional layer of protection
against the introduction and spread of emerging infectious diseases. However, at the outset
of the pandemic, the UK lacked a strategy for the implementation of international border
controls. It is therefore vital that the UK considers the complexities in implementing and
enforcing international border control measures as part of its pandemic planning.

All four governments lacked robust mechanisms to monitor and evaluate the effectiveness
of the measures they implemented. Data on compliance, particularly with respect to self-
isolation, were often unreliable. This limited their value for informed decision-making.
Greater emphasis should have been placed on the systematic assessment of policies.
With more accurate and comprehensive data, decision-makers would have been better
equipped to adapt and refine policies to ensure that they were followed by as many people
as possible.



                                                                                                 3

=== PAGE 18 ===
    Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




    Many elements of the UK’s test, trace and isolate systems were developed and adapted in
    real time. By contrast, several countries and territories, including Singapore, Australia and
    Germany, were comparatively better prepared to respond to the emergence of Covid-19.
    These countries experienced markedly lower death rates than the UK during the first six
    months of the pandemic. The Inquiry recognises that their response overall may have had
    other social and economic consequences that are unacceptable to some. However, these
    examples show that detailed pandemic planning, together with significant investment in
    public health infrastructure and diagnostic capacity, can enable a government to implement
    scalable test, trace and isolate measures at an earlier stage of a pandemic.

    The UK’s test, trace and isolate response to Covid-19 came too late because it relied
    too heavily on plans and capabilities that had to be built from the ground up during
    the emergency itself. Testing infrastructure, contact tracing systems and self-isolation
    support schemes were constructed under pressure and scaled to levels that no plan
    had anticipated.

    Once established, the systems were highly successful and those involved deserve our
    thanks. Yet, their impact might have been greater had clearer strategic plans, stronger
    preparedness arrangements and scalable operational systems been in place before the
    pandemic began.

    The financial cost of building the UK’s test, trace and isolate systems was extraordinary.
    The UK government spent in excess of £25 billion on NHS Test and Trace in England
    between the start of the pandemic and June 2022. Better planning – including clearer
    requirements, proper evaluation and earlier testing – is required to avoid that level of
    expenditure next time.

    The UK must take steps now to ensure that it is equipped with a comprehensive system to
    test, trace, isolate and support individuals during a public health emergency.

    To ensure that the UK is prepared to identify and control transmission in a future public
    health emergency, this Report makes nine recommendations. In summary, these are:

      • monitoring the UK’s testing capabilities to ensure that the diagnostics sector is
         used effectively;
      • ensuring that plans are in place to deliver manual contact tracing on a large scale;
      • developing a UK-wide digital contact tracing tool;
      • ensuring that local plans are in place to deliver practical support to communities if
         self-isolation is required;
      • ensuring that plans are in place for financial support if self-isolation is required;
      • planning for the use of international border control measures;
      • developing aligned communications campaigns about testing, tracing and isolation,
         to avoid confusion or ambiguity;

4

=== PAGE 19 ===
                                                                          Executive summary




  • improving data collection and evaluation of testing, tracing and isolation so that
     the impact on the public and levels of adherence can be monitored effectively;
  • producing a single UK-wide system for testing, tracing and isolation.
A full list of the Inquiry’s recommendations is included in Appendix 3.

As noted in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report, it is not a question of ‘if’ another pandemic
will strike but ‘when’. The UK and devolved administrations should work together to
develop a UK-wide test, trace and isolate system that is scalable, adaptable, accessible and
trusted. By embedding the lessons of the Covid-19 pandemic into long-term preparedness
planning, the UK can move from a reactive approach to one that is resilient and capable of
responding quickly. The recommendations in this Report should be implemented without
delay to ensure that the UK is equipped with a system capable of testing, tracing, isolating
and supporting people from the earliest stages of the next pandemic to reduce widespread
transmission and, ultimately, to save lives.

=== PAGE 20 ===
Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness
to implement a test, trace and
isolate system

=== PAGE 21 ===
                         Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system




Introduction
1.1.      When the coronavirus (Covid-19) pandemic struck, there was nothing new about the
          concept of testing, tracing and isolation to prevent the spread of infectious disease.
          For centuries, the identification and isolation of people exposed to infection has
          been an important part of pandemic control. In the 14th century, Venetian laws
          mandated 40-day quarantine to prevent the spread of bubonic plague.1 In the
          16th century, English households infected with plague were required to shut
          themselves away from the rest of society.2 Cholera outbreaks in the 19th and 20th
          centuries led to quarantine laws around Europe.3 More recently, contact tracing was
          used during the 2009 to 2010 H1N1 influenza pandemic (‘swine flu’).4

1.2.      A system for testing, tracing and isolating cases of infectious disease is therefore
          an established and fundamental public health tool. If done well, it identifies sources
          of outbreaks, controls the spread of infection and ultimately saves lives. Without an
          effective system to do this, governments may have little choice but to impose more
          severe restrictions, which cause economic disruption and place significant restraints
          on people’s lives.5

1.3.      However, there was no plan to implement wide-scale testing, tracing and isolation
          in the UK in January 2020. As noted in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report, the UK was
          ill prepared for dealing with a catastrophic emergency, let alone the Covid-19
          pandemic.6 This chapter examines the UK’s readiness to control the spread of
          Covid-19 through testing, tracing and isolation. It explores the lessons about the
          importance of testing, tracing and isolation that could and should have been learned
          from previous epidemics and pandemics. It sets out examples of international good
          practice in terms of readiness to test, trace and isolate at scale, and how that
          contributed to effective pandemic response.

1.4.      The systems for testing, tracing and isolation adopted in the four nations of the
          UK were given different names: Test and Protect (in Scotland); NHS Test and Trace
          (in England); Test, Trace, Protect (in Wales); and Test, Trace and Protect (in Northern
          Ireland). This Report uses the overarching term ‘test, trace and isolate’ to refer
          generally to testing, tracing and isolation and makes clear when it is referring to
          a particular system in one of the four nations.

=== PAGE 22 ===
    Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




    The purpose of a test, trace and isolate system
    1.5.       The purpose of a test, trace and isolate system (ie a response to infection outbreak
               that incorporates testing, contact tracing and requirements to self-isolate) is to
               identify people who have been exposed to infection and to isolate them to reduce
               further spread of that infection.7 There are three elements that make up such a
               system (see Figure 1):

               • testing – identifying newly infected, or potentially infected, people as quickly
                   as possible;
               • tracing – finding the contacts of identified infected people as quickly as
                   possible; and
               • isolation – ensuring that as many infected people and their contacts as possible
                   are isolated to reduce further spread.8

               Such a system can also capture useful data on infected people and their contacts
               – improving understanding of the virus and the way it spreads.9 It may also reduce
               the need for other interventions (excluding vaccines, drugs or other medical
               interventions).10

    Figure 1: Key steps for a test, trace and isolate programme

=== PAGE 23 ===
                        Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system




1.6.      The public health response to an infectious outbreak – particularly a pandemic – will
          include a wide-scale system for testing, tracing and isolation.11 A Covid-19 modelling
          study conducted in May 2020 estimated that combining social distancing and
          household self-isolation substantially reduced virus transmission. Adding contact
          tracing to this package of interventions reduced subsequent Covid-19 infections by
          an additional 5 to 15%.12 Modelling has its limitations, but that study was one of a
          number of sources during the Covid-19 pandemic that recognised the value of a
          system to test, trace and isolate (as discussed in the Inquiry’s combined Report
          relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C).13 For example, a paper from the Independent
          Scientific Advisory Group for Emergencies (Independent SAGE; a group of UK
          scientists, independent of the UK government, set up to monitor and assess UK
          strategy in relation to the Covid-19 pandemic) in October 2020 concluded that the
          Covid-19 pandemic would “never be brought under control in the UK without every
          community having access to a … [test, trace and isolate] system that actually works”.14
          Similarly, a report from the Scientific Pandemic Insights Group on Behaviours (SPI-B)
          dated 22 April 2021 confirmed that identification and isolation of infectious people
          could have a significant impact on transmission of Covid-19 and that a test, trace
          and isolate system was key to achieving this.15

1.7.      Some pathogens may not require a response focused on all the elements of a
          test, trace and isolate system. For example, during the HIV/AIDS epidemic in the
          1980s and 1990s, key measures to prevent the spread of infection were avoiding
          unprotected sex and avoiding exposure to infected blood, rather than self-isolation.16
          However, for pathogens spread through contact transmission and airborne
          transmission, such as Covid-19, trying to stop contact through self-isolation
          is a main way to reduce the chance of exposure.17

1.8.      A test, trace and isolate system is most effective in containing the spread of infection
          when the number of people infected is relatively low, such as at the start of a
          pandemic.18 Studies suggested a notable difference between the effectiveness of a
          test, trace and isolate system in periods of low prevalence, compared with periods of
          high prevalence. One study of Covid-19 data from Switzerland estimated that contact

               tracing could identify up to 60% of infected contacts during periods of low
               prevalence, but less than 25% during epidemic peaks.19 This is because public
               health resources can be targeted to where they are most needed when there are
               low numbers of infected cases.20 Modelling conducted during the Covid-19 pandemic
               also estimated that a system of testing, tracing and isolation was most effective
               when there was a quick turnaround of testing and tracing, a high proportion of
               people following guidance to test, trace and isolate, and consistent data collection
               and population surveillance.21

     1.9.      However, even during periods of high prevalence, a test, trace and isolate system
               can reduce transmission, which is a key objective of any public health response to
               an infectious disease.22


     Testing, tracing and isolation in previous epidemics
     and pandemics
     1.10.     As noted in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report, the years leading up to the Covid-19
               pandemic presented numerous opportunities to learn lessons for pandemic
               preparedness. These included the vital role of a test, trace and isolate system
               in curbing the spread of infection.23

     1.11.     In 2003 and 2004, various pandemic preparedness exercises across the four nations
               noted the importance of contact tracing and quarantine, of preventing the initial
               spread of a virus rather than simply dealing with its impact, and of having a national
               response plan.24

     1.12.     Contact tracing was used during the swine flu pandemic, but only for three months.
               There were also significantly fewer cases of infection during swine flu than during
               the Covid-19 pandemic. Despite this, according to a 2023 Department of Health
               and Social Care report about contact tracing, pressures on existing systems rapidly
               became unsustainable during the swine flu pandemic.25

                       Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system




1.13.    In response to the 2013 to 2016 outbreak of Ebola virus disease in West Africa, the
         UK set up symptom screening at ports of entry, which included contact tracing for
         positive cases.26 Professor Julian Hiscox (Professor of Infection and Global Health at
         the University of Liverpool and Director of the UK International Coronavirus Network)
         said that an important lesson from the Ebola outbreak was:

              “that early diagnosis and rapid response was the most important factor in
                controlling and eliminating the outbreak. This relied on setting up isolation
                facilities to help prevent spread of the virus (and to treat infected individuals).”27

         Professor Hiscox told the Inquiry that this information was known by Public Health
         England and the UK government in late 2015.28

1.14.    In February 2016, the UK government conducted Exercise Alice, a simulation of an
         outbreak of Middle East respiratory syndrome (MERS).29 Not all recommendations
         arising from Exercise Alice had been implemented fully by the time of the Covid-19
         pandemic, including the development of a plan about quarantining and self-
         isolation.30

1.15.    In October 2016, Exercise Cygnus assessed the UK’s preparedness for a pandemic-
         scale influenza outbreak. It simulated the seventh week of a pandemic, with
         participants working where they usually would during a pandemic, communicating
         with each other and attending simulated COBR meetings.31 The Exercise Cygnus
         report concluded that the UK’s preparedness and response – in terms of its plans,
         policies and capability – were still not sufficient to cope with a severe pandemic with
         nationwide impact.32 It did not specifically consider testing, tracing or isolating but
         identified 22 lessons and 4 key ‘learning outcomes’ in relation to an influenza
         outbreak. The report identified a lack of capability and capacity to surge resources
         in a number of key areas, including healthcare, social care and the management
         of increased death rates.33

1.16.    In response to Exercise Cygnus, a Pandemic Flu Readiness Board was established
         in March 2017.34 However, it did not meet between November 2018 and November
         2019. Following that year-long hiatus, it was recognised that there was a need to
         reinvigorate the board and to prioritise workstreams. However, the UK government’s
         priority did not move back to pandemic preparedness because its focus shifted to

           Board did not meet again until 23 January 2020, by which time the Covid-19
           pandemic had probably already reached the UK.36

 1.17.     In summary, despite repeated warnings about the importance of testing, tracing and
           isolation, prior to the Covid-19 pandemic (as noted in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report):

           • None of the governments of the UK conducted pandemic exercises that tested
              testing, tracing or isolation measures in relation to an epidemic or pandemic-scale
              outbreak of an emerging infectious disease.37
           • Neither the Department of Health and Social Care nor Public Health England
              adequately considered how the UK might slow or halt the transmission of a
              novel and significant pathogen that could cause a pandemic.38
           • Lessons from previous exercises were not sufficiently debated and shared
              – in many cases, learning and recommendations were not acted upon or
              were forgotten.39


 International examples of test, trace and isolate
 readiness
 1.18.     Pandemic preparedness plans for testing, tracing and isolation were established
           in countries, territories and regions, such as Hong Kong, Australia, South Korea,
           Singapore, Taiwan and Germany, years before the Covid-19 pandemic.

 1.19.     Hong Kong was significantly affected by the 2002 to 2003 severe acute respiratory
           syndrome (SARS) outbreak, experiencing almost one-quarter of the 8,098 cases
           worldwide, with 302 deaths. There followed significant public investment in health
           infrastructure and diagnostic testing capacity.40 During the Covid-19 pandemic, the

          Hong Kong government was able quickly and effectively to put in place a system
          with one of the highest per capita testing rates in the world.41 In May 2020, the UK’s
          Foreign and Commonwealth Office observed:

              “The real difference in Hong Kong is the comprehensive test and trace regime
               and people’s sense of personal responsibility. It is the combination of these
               issues which mean, despite having been affected by COVID-19 since January
               2020, Hong Kong has kept its spread under control.”42

          As at April 2021, Hong Kong had avoided any city-wide lockdowns and the
          government had been successful in controlling the rate of infection.43

1.20.     The Singapore government also learned lessons from its experience with the
          2002 to 2003 SARS outbreak.44 As a result, it developed a comprehensive crisis
          management plan, which was refined over the years, and it invested heavily in
          epidemic preparedness and response.45 This included the expansion and upgrade
          of its medical facilities and the development of advanced research and diagnostic
          capabilities to study viruses and identify treatment, detection and prevention
          options.46 The Singapore government’s priority at that early stage was to “detect
          every possible case early, contact trace, and contain further spread”.47

1.21.     Similarly, after Taiwan suffered the impact of the SARS outbreak in 2003, significant
          action was taken to ensure that it would be prepared for future international health
          emergencies. This included enhancing the functions of its Centers for Disease
          Control (at the time an agency of Taiwan’s Department of Health), expanding the
          medical workforce and improving its digital technology, disease investigation
          capabilities and strategies for emergency responses.48 The measures meant that
          Taiwan could take early action in response to the Covid-19 pandemic.49 As soon as
          reports of a new and unknown disease in China reached Taiwan in January 2020, an
          inter-ministerial Central Epidemic Command Center was activated. This focused on
          measures for blocking transmission of Covid-19.50 In May 2020, the UK’s Foreign and
          Commonwealth Office listed Taiwan as one of the effective examples of a test, trace
          and isolate strategy in the international community, noting: “Proactive containment
          efforts and comprehensive contact tracing have kept the number of cases low.”51
          Evidence from April 2021 suggests that, by that point, Taiwan had one of the lowest

               per capita incidence and mortality rates of Covid-19 in the world and had reduced
               the spread of Covid-19 without strict lockdowns or school closures.52

     1.22.     During the MERS outbreak in 2015, proactive tracing tools and practices were
               developed in South Korea.53 In May 2020, the UK’s Data Evaluation and Learning
               for Viral Epidemics (DELVE), a multidisciplinary group convened by the Royal Society,
               conducted analysis of testing, tracing and isolation to inform discussions of the
               Scientific Advisory Group for Emergencies (SAGE) in relation to the Covid-19
               pandemic. DELVE reported that the total time it took in South Korea to test an
               infected person, trace their contacts and advise self-isolation was about two to
               three days.54 (This was about half the UK’s then average of five days to test, trace
               and isolate cases.)55 DELVE’s simulation model suggested that reducing the overall
               turnaround time from five to three days could lead to a 60% improvement in the
               effectiveness (defined as a reduction in the reproduction number: the average
               number of people that one person with a disease infects) of the test, trace and
               isolate system.56

     1.23.     South Korea’s well-resourced contact tracing network and high testing capacity
               contributed to keeping infection rates low and avoiding severe lockdowns.57
               Its Covid-19 pandemic strategy would become globally recognised as a gold
               standard approach.58

     1.24.     Germany also entered the Covid-19 pandemic armed with a detailed National
               Pandemic Plan. This plan was developed between 2016 and 2017 and a supplement
               relating specifically to Covid-19 was published on 4 March 2020.59 It aimed to test
               anyone with symptoms, track down all the people with whom they had had contact
               and isolate those contacts.60 Together with generic preparedness plans and other
               disease-specific plans and documents, the National Pandemic Plan allowed the
               German government to activate a Covid-19 response quickly, with rapid development
               of a Covid-19 test and scale-up of testing capacity, efficient use of intensive care unit
               bed capacity, and early visiting restrictions in high-risk settings.61 For example,
               Dr Peter Cotgreave (giving evidence on behalf of the Microbiology Society) told

         the Inquiry that, in a seven-day period in March 2020, 143 laboratories in Germany
         conducted and reported the results of 352,000 tests.62

1.25.    The first Covid-19 infections in Germany and the UK were recorded within days
         of each other (27 and 31 January 2020 respectively).63 However, by 17 April 2020,
         there were about 49 deaths for every million people in Germany, whereas the UK’s
         average was 202 deaths per million people.64 The UK’s Foreign and Commonwealth
         Office noted in May 2020:

              “Heavy focus on contact tracing and effective management of infection chains
               since the beginning is widely viewed as having been an important contributor
               to Germany’s relatively low infection rates.”65

1.26.    Australia’s main pre-existing pandemic plan was the Australian Health Management
         Plan for Pandemic Influenza. This resulted from lessons learned from previous
         public health emergencies and outlined a strategy for minimising the impact of a
         pandemic on health systems and communities.66 It was activated formally on
         25 February 2020, when the Australian government declared Covid-19 a pandemic.67
         On 27 February, the government’s response was adapted to take account of the
         specific nature of Covid-19 and it implemented a new Covid-19 response plan, the
         Australian Health Sector Emergency Response Plan for Novel Coronavirus.68 This
         pandemic preparedness laid the foundation for the swift implementation of
         measures, such as activating and maintaining contact tracing from 25 January 2020,
         when the first confirmed case of Covid-19 in Australia was identified.69 The Australian
         government’s early approach meant that it was successful in the initial phase of its
         response to Covid-19, significantly slowing the import and spread of the virus.70

 1.27.               There was, therefore, a clear link between planning and investment and early control
                     of the Covid-19 pandemic. In May 2020, in a presentation about international testing,
                     tracing and isolation responses to Covid-19, the UK’s Foreign and Commonwealth
                     Office noted:

                        “All leading schemes are born from previous pandemic experience. Hong Kong,
                         South Korea and Singapore have all invested heavily in contact tracing over the
                         last 17 years and modelled how this would operate.”71

 1.28.               There was evidence that a number of countries with detailed pandemic
                     preparedness plans (such as Singapore, Australia and Germany) were able to set up
                     testing, tracing and isolation far earlier than the UK. Those countries also had
                     markedly lower death rates than the UK in the first six months of the pandemic (see
                     Figure 2).72

=== PAGE 31 ===
                        Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system




1.29.     The Inquiry recognises that every country has its own political, demographic and
          geographical characteristics. What suits one country may not suit another. It also
          recognises that success in suppressing the virus in the early stages of the Covid-19
          pandemic may have come at a high social and economic cost that would be
          unacceptable to some and may have unacceptable consequences later in the
          pandemic. However, these examples show that some decision-makers learned
          important lessons from earlier public health emergencies. They improved their
          infrastructure and developed comprehensive strategies for implementing large-scale
          test, trace and isolate systems in the event of a pandemic. They were therefore far
          better placed than the UK government and devolved administrations to respond
          quickly and effectively to Covid-19.73


The UK’s preparedness to test, trace and isolate
1.30.     Although health has been a devolved (or transferred) matter in Scotland, Wales and
          Northern Ireland since 1999, whole-system civil emergencies (ie events or situations
          that threaten serious damage to human welfare, the environment or the security
          of the UK) impact the whole society of the UK.74 They therefore require a cross-
          departmental approach within the UK government and devolved administrations,
          as well as a coordinated approach across the four nations.75


Lack of a suitable pre-pandemic plan
1.31.     There were numerous opportunities over the years for the UK government and
          devolved administrations to learn lessons about the importance of testing, tracing
          and isolation in order to develop an aligned, UK-wide pandemic preparedness plan
          that included all three elements. The Welsh Government developed the Wales
          Framework for Managing Major Infectious Disease Emergencies in 2014, which
          included broad plans for Public Health Wales to lead contact tracing and isolation in
          a public health emergency.76 However, there was no detailed, scalable operational
          plan for a UK‑wide test, trace and isolate system capable of responding to a
          pandemic such as Covid-19.

1.32.     The UK Influenza Pandemic Preparedness Strategy 2011 (the 2011 Strategy) was
          prepared jointly by the UK government and devolved administrations as a UK-wide

          plan for an influenza pandemic.77 However, the influenza virus has different
          characteristics from Covid-19.78 In an influenza pandemic, for example, testing
          and contact tracing does not make a meaningful difference to transmission because
          the incubation period is too short and the transmission rate is too high.79 It is not
          surprising, therefore, that the 2011 Strategy did not envisage either mass testing
          or mass contact tracing beyond the initial phase of a pandemic.80 Accordingly, it
          was not a suitable strategy to use to respond to a novel infectious virus with the
          characteristics of Covid-19.81 As identified in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report, the
          absence of a detailed, UK-wide scalable pandemic plan limited the UK’s readiness
          to test, trace and isolate at scale in January 2020.82 It also limited the time the four
          governments of the UK had to assess how test, trace and isolate measures would
          be deployed separately and in combination.

 1.33.    The May 2020 DELVE report explained:

               “Each of these three aspects of TTI [test, trace and isolate] needs careful
                attention, as do the trade-offs implicit in choices of how precisely to implement
                TTI in terms of who to test, trace and isolate, and when to do so.”83

          However, plans for how to implement test, trace and isolate had to be developed
          after Covid-19 had arrived in the UK and the pandemic was rapidly unfolding.

 1.34.    The Department of Health and Social Care, along with its counterparts in the
          devolved administrations, should have developed a pandemic plan well in advance
          of 2020 that was capable of being adapted to the specific needs of a novel
          pathogen such as Covid-19.


 Capability for mass testing as at 1 January 2020
 1.35.    Before the Covid-19 pandemic, the infrastructure for diagnosing infection in the
          UK largely consisted of routine testing in NHS and public healthcare laboratories.
          The testing system was only capable of dealing with small numbers of cases.84
          Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health and Social Care from July 2018 to
          June 2021) told the Inquiry:

=== PAGE 33 ===
                        Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system




              “The UK entered the pandemic with a very small private sector diagnostics
               industry and a public sector diagnostic capacity that was … not scalable.
               This meant we were not well placed.”85

1.36.    In order to conduct mass testing during the Covid-19 pandemic, laboratories
         were required that could process tests on a large scale (as discussed in Chapter 2:
         Testing). As a result, as described by Professor Alan McNally (Head of the School of
         Infection, Inflammation and Immunology at the University of Birmingham from August
         2024 and former Director of the Institute of Microbiology and Infection at the
         University of Birmingham), the UK was “horribly under prepared to develop and
         deploy a mass testing system for a novel pandemic pathogen”.86


Capability for widespread contact tracing as at 1 January 2020
1.37.    Contact tracing was used routinely in the UK at a local level for smaller-scale
         outbreaks of diseases, such as norovirus, salmonella or Legionnaires’ disease, or
         for specific instances of infection with a sexually transmitted disease.87 Public health
         officers and environmental health officers in local authorities had extensive
         experience of contact tracing.88 England, Wales and Scotland – though not Northern
         Ireland – had local directors of public health (specialists who lead public health
         responses in a particular area).89

1.38.    However, there was relatively limited experience of large-scale contact tracing in
         the UK before 2020. Contact tracing was used for only three months during the
         2009 to 2010 swine flu pandemic to prevent the spread of infection.90 During the
         Ebola outbreak between 2013 and 2016, it was used along with symptom screening
         to identify potentially infected people at ports of entry but, fortunately, there were
         only three Ebola cases in the UK, with no onward spread.91 Large-scale contact
         tracing was not necessary.92

1.39.    Professor Dame Jenny Harries (Deputy Chief Medical Officer for England from July
         2019 to March 2021, Chief Executive of the UK Health Security Agency from April
         2021 to May 2025) told the Inquiry that contact tracing systems before the Covid-19
         pandemic “were not designed to surge to the scale required by, nor to control, an
         established whole-population national pandemic”.93

=== PAGE 34 ===
     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     1.40.    Contact tracing across the UK during the Covid-19 pandemic on the scale required
              involved establishing call centres and recruiting staff for manual contact tracing, as
              well as developing digital tools for digital contact tracing (see Chapter 3: Contact
              tracing). This took time.

     1.41.    As with testing, the UK was wholly unprepared in January 2020 to scale up contact
              tracing to the levels required by the Covid-19 pandemic.


     Capability to implement effective self-isolation as at 1 January 2020
     1.42.    Self-isolation does not require specific infrastructure to implement it on a wide scale
              in the same way that testing requires the capacity to develop and process tests or
              contact tracing requires human or digital contact tracers. People can simply be
              requested to self-isolate if they meet specific criteria. The challenge is encouraging
              and enabling compliance.

     1.43.    Previous international epidemics indicated that financial concerns were a significant
              factor influencing people’s adherence to self-isolation requirements. During the 2002
              to 2003 SARS outbreak in Toronto, Canada, fear of loss of income was the most
              common reason for non-adherence to self-isolation measures among healthcare
              workers.94 In Australia during the swine flu pandemic, a need to work was cited as a
              factor in breaking isolation rules.95

     1.44.    Professor James Rubin (Professor of Psychology and Emerging Health Risks at King’s
              College London) told the Inquiry that the requirement for practical and financial
              support for those self-isolating was foreseeable before January 2020. In January
              2020, he asked the Health Protection Research Unit at King’s College London to
              produce systematic reviews of the literature on how people cope when isolating.96
              The reviews (published in February and March 2020) indicated that self-isolation
              can be distressing and that people sometimes struggle to adhere to the rules.
              The reviews identified financial and practical support as two ways to mitigate the
              negative impacts of self-isolation.97 According to Professor Rubin, the reviews were
              widely cited in academia and circulated within Public Health England.98 Although
              they were based entirely on evidence from 2019 or earlier, there is no indication
              that the evidence on which they were based led to any pre-pandemic plan for
              self-isolation.99

     1.45.    As with testing and contact tracing, there was no plan in place as at 1 January 2020
              to implement effective, widespread self-isolation, as was required during the
              Covid-19 pandemic.

=== PAGE 35 ===
                        Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system




Lack of readiness to test, trace and isolate at scale
1.46.     Years of public health emergencies and pandemic preparedness exercises in the
          lead-up to the Covid-19 pandemic should have meant that the UK government and
          devolved administrations were fully prepared to deploy widespread testing, tracing
          and isolation when Covid-19 struck in January 2020. However, as Professor Keith
          Godfrey (Professor of Epidemiology and Human Development at the University of
          Southampton) told the Inquiry, it was clear that “effective systems were not in place
          for any of testing, tracing or isolation” as at 1 January 2020.100

1.47.     An effective Covid-19 test was developed by mid-January 2020 and infection rates
          were still low in January and much of February 2020.101 January and February 2020
          would have been the optimum time for the UK to implement a pre-prepared plan
          for a wide-scale test, trace and isolate system. Yet, despite a test being almost
          immediately available, there was no system of widespread testing, tracing or
          isolation for several months. Covid-19 was allowed to spread with deadly speed
          across the country, virtually unchecked.

=== PAGE 36 ===
Chapter 2: Testing

=== PAGE 37 ===
                                                                                                        Chapter 2: Testing




Introduction
2.1.     Testing plays a vital role in diagnosing and preventing the spread of infections.
         Identifying people who are infected enables treatment to be provided to them
         where appropriate and helps keep them from contacting others, thereby reducing
         transmission among the wider population. Testing can also identify people who
         are not infectious, allowing them to end self-isolation and to avoid its social and
         economic consequences.1

2.2.     A highly accurate test for Covid-19 was developed by mid-January 2020. However,
         between January and March 2020, demand rapidly outstripped the supply of tests
         and the laboratory capacity to process them. This meant that community testing of
         people with Covid-19 symptoms was paused, and access to testing was subject to
         eligibility requirements.

2.3.     Testing capacity increased during 2020, in large part due to the establishment of
         Lighthouse laboratories (discussed further below), but some prioritisation remained
         until rapid tests for use at home were developed. By April 2021, free lateral flow tests
         (which provided results within 30 minutes) were available for regular use by the UK
         public, including people without Covid-19 symptoms. The introduction of rapid, at-
         home testing significantly improved the UK’s ability to identify infection and monitor
         the spread of the disease across the population.

2.4.     This chapter examines the development of diagnostic tests and the decision-making
         of the UK government and devolved administrations about testing during the
         Covid-19 pandemic.


Principles of testing
2.5.     Testing is primarily used to diagnose people who have symptoms of a disease
         (symptomatic testing). It helps healthcare professionals decide on an appropriate
         treatment. Testing also improves understanding of the disease and how it spreads.2
         This means that diseases can be monitored and controlled, allowing outbreaks and
         pandemics to be identified. In the UK, the public health agencies of the four nations
         of the UK are responsible for monitoring diseases: the UK Health Security Agency
         (prior to October 2021, Public Health England), Public Health Scotland, Public Health
         Wales and the Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland).3 Public Health England

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




               was the designated UK body responsible for communication with the World Health
               Organization in the event of a disease spreading.4

     2.6.      Testing strategies are likely to change during a pandemic as knowledge of the
               disease develops, patterns of transmission evolve, and testing capacity increases.5
               They must take into account when people are most infectious, how long it takes
               for an infection to become detectable, and how long infection is likely to last.6
               They must also consider the availability of tests and the capacity of the systems
               needed to produce and process them.7 Where sufficient capacity exists, testing
               people without symptoms in the community (asymptomatic community testing) can
               help reduce the spread of the disease.8 Where resources are limited, testing may
               need to be prioritised for certain groups or high-risk settings.9


     Test types
     2.7.      Viruses are pathogens made up of genetic material contained within a protective
               layer. This genetic material is either DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) or RNA (ribonucleic
               acid).10 When a virus infects a person, it enters the body’s cells and uses them to
               make copies of itself.11

     2.8.      Tests check whether the genetic material of a virus (DNA or RNA) or another
               substance is present in a biological sample. Testing technologies vary in what
               they can detect, how they are used, how long they take to produce results, and in
               their cost and accuracy.12 Tests developed for Covid-19 mainly used samples from
               the nose, throat or saliva. However, samples can also be taken from blood, urine,
               stool, cerebrospinal fluid or tissue.13 Some tests require more specialist training
               and equipment to carry out, and to analyse results, than others.14

     2.9.      The polymerase chain reaction (PCR) test and a modified version of the PCR test, the
               reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction (RT-PCR) test, are well-established
               and highly accurate forms of molecular testing. They work by detecting the genetic

                                                                                                            Chapter 2: Testing




          material of the virus that causes disease. Standard PCR tests detect DNA. However,
          Covid-19 is caused by a virus that uses RNA, so the modified version was required.
          The RT-PCR test includes an additional step that first converts RNA into DNA (known
          as ‘reverse transcription’) so that it can be detected.15 Samples of genetic material are
          collected using nasal or throat swabs and are sent to a laboratory to be processed,
          and the results returned to the person tested.16 This process takes time, which can
          be critical when quick action is needed to limit the spread of the disease.17

2.10.     Other molecular tests use saliva samples rather than throat or nasal swabs. This
          includes loop-mediated isothermal amplification (LAMP) testing and its equivalent for
          RNA targets (known as reverse-transcription loop-mediated isothermal amplification
          or RT-LAMP testing).18 For Covid-19, RT-LAMP tests can provide fast and accurate
          results, but they require new, large-scale machinery and additional staff training.19

2.11.     Molecular testing is highly accurate because it works by detecting genetic material.
          Antigen testing (using lateral flow tests) detects specific proteins produced by the
          virus in liquid samples. Lateral flow tests are less accurate than molecular tests, but
          they are low-cost, do not need specialised equipment or processing, produce results
          quickly, and can be used at home and without the need to transport samples.20

2.12.     Both molecular and antigen testing detect the virus itself, but antibody (serology)
          testing works by detecting the body’s response to a virus. It is used to identify
          whether a person has previously been exposed to a pathogen rather than to
          diagnose a current infection.21 It is carried out using blood samples and can be
          an important tool for research.22

2.13.     Another important research tool is wastewater testing, which detects viral pathogens
          to monitor the spread of disease across populations, including at the community
          level.23 This approach can be particularly valuable where individual testing rates
          are low, as it can help build understanding of how widely a disease is circulating.24

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     2.14.     Other diagnostic methods include imaging scans (eg MRI scans, ultrasound scans
               or X-rays) and biopsies (where small tissue samples are taken). These methods
               were not suitable for detecting a respiratory virus such as Covid-19, but they may
               be relevant for identifying diseases in the future.


     RT-PCR testing during Covid-19
     2.15.     Severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2), which causes
               the disease Covid-19, was confirmed as a new virus when the Chinese government
               shared its genetic sequence on 12 January 2020.25 Although no diagnostic test was
               then available, existing testing methods used for other diseases could be adapted
               for Covid-19.26

     2.16.     Public Health England led the testing response in the early stages of the pandemic,
               working with its counterparts in the devolved administrations.27 By 21 January
               2020, before the World Health Organization declared Covid-19 to be a public
               health emergency of international concern, scientists at Public Health England had
               developed an accurate diagnostic test using RT-PCR technology.28 The speed with
               which the test was produced put the UK at the forefront of Covid-19 diagnostic
               capability internationally. It became the “gold standard” for diagnosing Covid-19,
               with a sensitivity (correctly identifying infected people) and a specificity (correctly
               identifying those who were not infected) of more than 95%.29 By the end of January
               2020, daily testing capacity was about 300 to 400 tests per day.30


     Determining the number of tests required (January to February
     2020)
     2.17.     Modelling shared by Imperial College London with the UK government on
               23 January 2020 estimated that the reproduction number (R) for Covid-19 (in
               this case R0, the average number of people that one infected person would pass
               the virus to in a population with normal mixing behaviour and no prior immunity)
               was above 1 and likely between 2 and 3.31 If accurate, this meant that, on average,
               each infected person would pass the virus on to two or three others.32

     2.18.     On 12 February 2020, NHS England applied the reasonable worst-case pandemic
               scenario developed for influenza to assess the potential impact of Covid-19.

     (https://www.who.int/emergencies/disease-outbreak-news/item/2020-DON233; INQ000183385_0001)
     26 INQ000251906_0073 para 337
     27 INQ000587292_0015 paras 46-47
     28 INQ000269203_0046 para 6.45
     29 INQ000587347_0009, 0011 paras 35, 44

     31 Statistical models are tools for analysing complex data, identifying patterns, and informing evidence-based decision-making
     where direct measurement is not possible. Figures from modelling are estimates only, and different statistical models will produce
     different outcomes. These estimates are not based on direct observations. They are therefore limited by the underlying assumptions
     used in the models and are subject to uncertainty.

=== PAGE 41 ===
                                                                                                         Chapter 2: Testing




          The model projected that about half the population in England would become
          infected, with a peak of new symptomatic cases on day 42 of the outbreak, when
          an estimated 1.1 million people would experience new symptoms.33 Modelling may
          have its limitations, but this was, nevertheless, a daunting prospect.

2.19.     By 16 February 2020, testing capacity was approximately 2,000 per day, with
          results returned within 24 hours.34 Taking into account the modelling projections
          that suggested a large increase in people with symptoms, this meant that those
          responsible for decision-making were aware of the risk that testing capacity would
          be overtaken by demand.35 Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health and Social
          Care from July 2018 to June 2021) told the Inquiry:

              “While testing capacity at that stage exceeded demand given the limited number
                of suspected cases in the UK, it was clear that it would be quickly overwhelmed
                as transmission escalated.”36

          Decision-makers therefore turned their attention to increasing the availability
          of RT-PCR testing.


Initial limitations in rolling out RT-PCR testing (February 2020)
2.20. RT-PCR testing was first introduced in the UK, and the assay (test methodology) was
      published, in January 2020. Throughout January and February 2020, organisations
      outside government (including those in the private sector and academia) worked
      to develop their own RT-PCR tests.37 This marked an important moment in the
      early response to the Covid-19 pandemic, demonstrating the strength of diagnostic
      expertise available across the UK.

2.21.     Early in the pandemic, the capacity to process tests increased gradually.
          Although more than 10,000 tests had been conducted by the end of February
          2020, the available daily testing capacity was not being fully utilised, indicating
          that there were barriers to expanding access to testing during February.38

Limited laboratory capacity

2.22. At the outset of the pandemic, the UK did not have sufficient testing capacity
      within publicly owned laboratories to meet the demand for Covid-19 testing. In an
      effort to increase capacity, Public Health England expanded its diagnostic capacity

=== PAGE 42 ===
     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




               in February 2020, from a single London-based laboratory to a network of 12
               laboratories across the UK.39 Despite this expansion, the UK government’s approach
               to scaling up existing systems was not capable of meeting testing demands.

     2.23. However, laboratory testing capacity did not only exist in publicly owned
           laboratories. Across the UK, diagnostic testing capacity was spread across a
           combination of public health institutions and private sector laboratories, some of
           which were working on behalf of the UK government and devolved administrations.40

     2.24. Senior decision-makers in the UK government had limited knowledge of this
           diagnostic laboratory infrastructure and its scalability. Mr Hancock described
           the UK’s laboratory network as “a cottage industry of testing in little centres” –
           he understood capacity to be either embedded in the NHS and not scalable or
           embedded in research labs and also not scalable.41 Boris Johnson (Prime Minister
           from July 2019 to September 2022) told the Inquiry that he did not recall being made
           aware of the existing level of infrastructure capacity before the pandemic, but that
           “the scales fell from [his] eyes” during a trip to Public Health England’s Colindale
           laboratory on 1 March 2020.42

     2.25. As discussed in Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and
           isolate system, no pandemic preparedness exercise adequately assessed the UK’s
           readiness for testing on a large scale. The Department of Health and Social Care
           should have developed a greater understanding of the condition and capabilities
           of the diagnostic testing network during appropriate pandemic response exercises.43

     2.26. Limited awareness of the existing infrastructure meant that plans to increase
           testing capacity were not developed prior to the Covid-19 pandemic nor in its very
           early stages. Combined with the view held by senior decision-makers in the UK
           government that diagnostic testing could not be scaled up quickly, this resulted in
           lost time in February 2020. Professor Sir Christopher Whitty (Chief Medical Officer
           for England from October 2019) acknowledged that scaling up capacity “was our
           great weakness in our response at the start of the pandemic”.44

     Constraints on laboratory containment levels

     2.27.     On 13 February 2020, the Advisory Committee on Dangerous Pathogens (an expert
               committee of the Department of Health and Social Care that provides scientific
               advice on the risks of exposure to pathogens) provisionally classified Covid-19 as a
               ‘Group 3’ hazard under the Control of Substances Hazardous to Health Regulations

=== PAGE 43 ===
                                                                                                       Chapter 2: Testing




         2002.45 Group 3 classification required test processing to take place in Containment
         Level 3 laboratories – the second highest biological containment level. This meant
         that samples had to be handled by suitably trained staff in controlled environments
         subject to stringent safety measures. This limited the number of laboratories and the
         methods of transportation that could be used.46

2.28. Professor Dame Jenny Harries (Deputy Chief Medical Officer for England from July
      2019 to March 2021, and Chief Executive of the UK Health Security Agency from
      April 2021 to May 2025) told the Inquiry that the Containment Level 3 designation
      acted as a “brake on the expansion of testing”.47 With laboratories subject to
      containment restrictions, capacity to process tests remained constrained, while
      demand continued to rise.

2.29. On 28 February 2020, Public Health England formally requested that, provided
      appropriate safety conditions were met, test processing could be carried out in
      laboratories operating at less restrictive containment levels (Containment Level 2+).48
      This request was supported by the Health and Safety Executive and approved by
      the Advisory Committee on Dangerous Pathogens on 1 March 2020, widening the
      range of facilities that could be used.49 By that date, 11,750 people had been tested
      across the UK. Of these, 11,715 people had received negative results and 35 people
      had tested positive – 33 in England, 1 in Wales, 1 in Northern Ireland and none in
      Scotland.50 The UK continued to have a much lower testing capacity than other
      economically comparable countries, including South Korea and Germany.51

2.30. Following the change in containment level requirements, daily NHS and Public
      Health England laboratory capacity increased substantially, rising from 2,336 tests
      per day on 2 March 2020 to 10,949 tests per day on 23 March.52

2.31.    February 2020 presented an opportunity to prepare for the rapid expansion that
         modelling and international evidence suggested would be necessary. However,
         as set out in the Inquiry’s combined Report relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C,
         February was a lost month.53 The UK entered March 2020 without a clear testing
         strategy.

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     Pausing community testing (March 2020)
     2.32. On 3 March 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care’s Coronavirus: Action
           Plan set out the UK-wide approach to responding to the pandemic. It described a
           four-stage framework to contain the spread of the virus:

              • ‘contain’ – preventing the spread of the disease for as long as reasonably
                  possible;
              • ‘delay’ – slowing the spread of the disease and reducing the peak impact;
              • ‘research’ – gaining a better understanding of the virus to inform care; and
              • ‘mitigate’ – providing the best possible care, maintaining essential services
                  and minimising the wider impact on society and the economy.54

              The plan outlined action already taken in the UK’s response and action that may be
              taken in future, but the only reference to testing was that work was under way on the
              “rapid development of specific tests” for Covid-19.55

     2.33. At the next two meetings of COBR (the UK government’s national crisis management
           centre for responding to whole-system civil emergencies), on 9 March and 11 March
           2020, testing was not explicitly discussed.56

     2.34. The move from the ‘contain’ to the ‘delay’ stage was agreed at a COBR meeting on
           12 March 2020 following advice about widespread community transmission from
           the Chief Medical Officers for the four nations and Professor Sir Patrick Vallance
           (later Lord Vallance of Balham), Government Chief Scientific Adviser from April 2018
           to March 2023.57 As part of the ‘delay’ stage, a seven-day self-isolation policy was
           to be introduced. Professor Whitty informed the meeting that, once this policy was
           in place, all testing of people in the community would stop. Only those who had
           symptoms would be advised to self-isolate.58

     2.35. Professor Whitty told the Inquiry that limited testing capacity meant there was “no
           obvious viable alternative”.59 Professor Vallance added that there were not enough
           tests to test people coming into hospital and that it was a pragmatic decision to use
           the tests for those who were ill.60

=== PAGE 45 ===
                                                                                                     Chapter 2: Testing




2.36. Mr Johnson announced the move to the delay stage at a press conference on
      12 March 2020.61 Community testing was paused in England the following day,
      on 13 March.62

2.37.    In Northern Ireland, community testing was paused on 12 March 2020. The level
         of transmission among the community was not known, but significant numbers of
         undiagnosed cases were suspected. Professor Sir Michael McBride (Chief Medical
         Officer for Northern Ireland from September 2006) told the Inquiry that testing
         capacity was insufficient to identify all cases, and so self-isolation and social
         distancing “superseded” community testing.63

2.38. In Scotland, community testing was paused on 13 March 2020, and this was
      announced to the public on 15 March.64 Professor Sir Gregor Smith (Interim Chief
      Medical Officer for Scotland from April to December 2020 and Chief Medical Officer
      for Scotland from December 2020) told the Inquiry that “insufficient testing capacity
      and infrastructure” made community testing not feasible.65

2.39. In Wales, the pause in community testing was recommended on 13 March 2020 by
      Dr (later Sir) Frank Atherton, Chief Medical Officer for Wales from August 2016 to
      January 2025.66 By 17 March, community testing had paused.67 Vaughan Gething,
      Minister for Health and Social Services in the Welsh Government from May 2016
      to May 2021, told the Inquiry that the decision ensured that “testing capacity was
      focused where there was greatest need”.68

2.40. Although testing continued for those requiring hospital care across all four nations,
      the suspension of community testing meant that the Department of Health and
      Social Care, the Welsh Government, the Scottish Government and the Department
      of Health (Northern Ireland) were no longer able to monitor how the disease was
      spreading through the wider population. It was estimated that between 5,000 and
      10,000 people had Covid-19, but, without community testing, it was not possible
      accurately to measure its prevalence.69 Professor Alan McNally (Head of the School
      of Infection, Inflammation and Immunology at the University of Birmingham from
      August 2024 and former Director of the Institute of Microbiology and Infection at the
      University of Birmingham) described this as the UK “trying to manage Covid blind
      with no information on cases, transmission, range of symptoms”.70

=== PAGE 46 ===
     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     2.41.    At the same time as community testing was paused in the UK, the importance
              of continuing to test was emphasised by the World Health Organization. Dr Tedros
              Adhanom Ghebreyesus, Director-General of the World Health Organization from
              July 2017, said the message to all countries was to “test, test, test”.71 He added:

                   “This is a controllable pandemic. Countries that decide to give up on fundamental
                    public health measures may end up with a larger problem, and a heavier burden
                    on the health system that requires more severe measures to control.”72

     2.42. Professor Whitty told the Inquiry that, although the UK was aware of this advice,
           “the limited availability of tests” meant that the UK could not follow it.73

     2.43. The consequences of being unable to test suspected cases in the community were
           threefold: monitoring of community transmission became far more difficult; tracing
           the contacts of infected people, and therefore isolating them, became problematic
           (this is considered further in Chapter 3: Contact tracing); and the likelihood increased
           that more restrictive measures, such as lockdowns, would later be required.

     2.44. The governments of the UK may not have been forced to pause community testing
           had they been better prepared to deliver diagnostic testing at scale (see Chapter 1:
           The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system). In the future, the
           UK must be able to scale up testing capacity rapidly in order to avoid a recurrence
           of this situation in health emergencies.


     Scaling up testing capacity (March to May 2020)
     2.45. By mid-March 2020, Mr Hancock had transferred responsibility for testing in England
           from Public Health England to the Department of Health and Social Care, as a result
           of concerns about Public Health England’s ability to expand testing capacity at the
           pace required.74

     2.46. A meeting was held at 10 Downing Street on 17 March 2020 to consider how testing
           capacity could be “urgently ramp[ed] up”.75 Those attending included representatives
           from private sector organisations, the Welsh Government, the Scottish Government,
           the Department of Health (Northern Ireland), the Department of Health and Social
           Care, and Public Health England.76 They discussed increasing NHS laboratory-based
           testing capacity used for testing patients and hospital staff from 5,000 to 25,000
