         Although this marked the first UK-wide plan aimed at mass testing, it came too late.78

2.47.    Private sector representatives at the meeting were asked to consider how their
         organisations could support the testing strategy. In the period that followed, they
         were repeatedly asked to make commitments beyond the capacity they had
         indicated was achievable.79

2.48. Testing capacity increased during March 2020 through a combination of expanded
      activity in NHS and Public Health England laboratories and collaboration with private
      sector providers. This enabled all four governments to begin widening access to
      testing – initially for clinical purposes – with the intention of extending testing further
      to healthcare and social care workers.80

The National Testing Programme

2.49. Although public health is a devolved (or, in Northern Ireland, ‘transferred’) matter,
      the Covid-19 pandemic required close cooperation between the UK government
      and devolved administrations.81 This coordinated approach led to the creation of a
      National Testing Programme, announced in April 2020, to expand testing capacity
      across the UK and to secure testing technologies across the four nations.82

2.50. On 4 April 2020, the UK-wide Coronavirus (Covid-19): Scaling Up Our Testing
      Programmes was published. This was the first publicly available document that
      explained plans to expand testing capacity through five pillars:

         • Pillar 1 – scaling up RT-PCR testing for people with a medical need and, where
             possible, the most critical key workers – using both NHS and Public Health
             England laboratories;
         • Pillar 2 – mass RT-PCR testing for key workers in healthcare, social care and other
             sectors, including the establishment of new laboratories;
         • Pillar 3 – mass antibody testing to help understand levels of population immunity;
         • Pillar 4 – surveillance testing to improve understanding of the spread of Covid-19;
             and
         • Pillar 5 – the Diagnostics National Effort to build overall mass testing capacity.                     83

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     2.51.    The cooperation between the four governments was formalised in an agreement
              signed by the four health ministers in April 2021, which authorised the Department
              of Health and Social Care to procure and deliver tests on behalf of the four nations.84

     2.52. Although it will not always be appropriate for testing decisions to be taken at a
           UK-wide level, during the Covid-19 pandemic this approach delivered clear benefits,
           particularly in relation to the procurement of tests.85 To support close working during
           future public health emergencies, a formal agreement should be established at an
           early stage, setting out the roles and responsibilities of each government, to provide
           clarity and to support effective cooperation.

     2.53. By the start of June 2020, each government announced its own approach to testing
           (including eligibility and allocation of available tests), tracing and isolation, while
           continuing to work together on the National Testing Programme:86

              • The Northern Ireland Executive’s Test, Trace and Protect scheme became fully
                  operational on 18 May 2020. This was led by the Department of Health (Northern
                  Ireland) and the Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland).87
              • In England, NHS Test and Trace was established on 28 May 2020, taking over
                  responsibility for testing in England from the Department of Health and Social
                  Care.88 Its establishment was followed by rapid and substantial expansion of
                  testing capacity in England.89
              • In Scotland, Test and Protect launched on 28 May 2020. This was a partnership  90

                  led by the Scottish Government and involved NHS Scotland health boards, Public
                  Health Scotland and NHS Scotland.91
              • In Wales, Test, Trace, Protect was implemented on 1 June 2020. The Welsh
                  Government provided oversight, and Public Health Wales contributed technical
                  expertise and experience.92

     Daily testing targets

     2.54. Mr Hancock announced a target of carrying out 100,000 tests per day in England
           by the end of April 2020.93 He told the Inquiry that the purpose of setting this target

          was “to galvanise the system to grow radically”.94 Oliver Munn (Director of Testing
          and Tracing Delivery in the Cabinet Office Covid-19 Taskforce from April 2020 to
          February 2022) explained that the figure would have been intended “to challenge
          departments to aim high”.95 Professor John Newton (Director of Health Improvement

2.55. The devolved administrations adopted different approaches to testing targets.
      The Scottish Government set a target of 3,500 tests per day by the end of April
      2020 and announced on 1 May 2020 that this target had been exceeded.97
      The Welsh Government set a target of 9,000 tests per day by the end of April
      but abandoned it on 20 April because of limitations in the supply of test kits.98
      The Department of Health (Northern Ireland) did not set a specific testing target.99

2.56. On 1 May 2020, the UK government announced that it had met the target of carrying
      out 100,000 tests per day.100 That claim was subsequently challenged.101 Sir Paul
      Nurse (Director and Chief Executive of the Francis Crick Institute from 2010 to 2025),
      for example, told the Inquiry that the UK government had stated that:

              “100,000 had been reached when in fact over half of them or thereabouts was
                actually in the post … when that emerged it actually undermines the public
                confidence. That if a government can say they are meeting 100,000, when
                50,000 of them are in the post, what else are they meant to believe?”102

2.57.     The testing target was also criticised for being arbitrary. NHS Providers described
          the focus on meeting the target as a “red herring”, arguing that it did not provide the
          most meaningful or comprehensive measure of testing effectiveness.103 Dr Duncan
          Robertson (Senior Lecturer in Management Sciences at Loughborough University,
          with expertise in simulation modelling) expressed concern that an arbitrary target
          risked becoming an end goal, rather than supporting the broader aim of expanding
          testing.104 Professor Mark Woolhouse (Professor of Infectious Disease Epidemiology
          at the University of Edinburgh) described daily test counts as “uninformative”,
          combining different types of tests and testing approaches.105

=== PAGE 50 ===
     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     2.58. The target placed significant pressure on laboratory staff. Dido Harding, Baroness
           Harding of Winscombe (Executive Chair of NHS Test and Trace from May 2020 to
           May 2021), said that it left staff “utterly shattered”.106 Laboratory workers described
           sustained pressure on facilities that were already understaffed and under-resourced
           before the pandemic.107

     2.59. Nonetheless, the testing target contributed to an overall increase in testing capacity
           during March and April 2020. As capacity increased, the UK government was able
           to extend eligibility for testing. By 18 May 2020, anyone experiencing Covid-19
           symptoms was eligible for testing across the UK.108 At that point, testing capacity
           had reached about 120,000 tests per day, and the UK government had announced
           a further target of 200,000 daily tests, reaching that target on 30 May.109 Both testing
           capacity and the number of tests carried out continued to rise throughout 2020,
           reaching capacity for more than 500,000 daily tests on 31 October 2020.110

     Lighthouse laboratories

     2.60. This increase in testing capacity was largely driven by the establishment of a new
           network of laboratories created by the UK government, known as the Lighthouse
           laboratories. These were set up to provide mass testing across the UK, and
           they aimed to process tests within 24 hours, using a single shared IT system
           and coordinated end-to-end processes.111 Mr Hancock formally opened the first
           Lighthouse laboratory in Milton Keynes on 9 April 2020.112 Between April 2020 and
           June 2021, a total of 10 Lighthouse laboratories were established, including one in
           Scotland and one in Wales (see Figure 3).113 The Lighthouse laboratory network was
           used by England, Scotland and Wales. The Department of Health (Northern Ireland)
           relied primarily on the Randox laboratory in Northern Ireland to process tests.114

Under-utilisation of academic and local capacity
2.61.     The UK government’s centralisation of testing was intended to simplify logistics
          and reduce duplication.115 Although Lighthouse laboratories ultimately became
          an important and necessary part of the testing system, they took time to establish
          and equip.

2.62. In the meantime, universities, medical schools and existing private laboratories
      already had suitable machinery, equipment and expertise that could have been
      used to support early testing efforts. Professor Deenan Pillay (Emeritus Professor
      of Virology at University College London and Consultant Medical Virologist at
      University College London Hospital from about 2007 to October 2022) told the
      Inquiry that insufficient use was made of the many clinical virologists who were
      considering how testing could be scaled up and who had “the capacity, insight
      and knowledge to develop plans through their own laboratories and institutions”.116
      Sir Paul Nurse described this as a “fundamental strategic error”.117

2.63. While local, academic and existing private laboratories would not have been capable
      of providing the level of capacity required over the longer term, they could have

115 INQ000562617
116 INQ000475152_0006 paras 21, 23

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




               contributed to scaling up testing in the short term. Professor McNally and Dr Peter
               Cotgreave (Chief Executive of the Microbiology Society) told the Inquiry that local
               laboratories could have helped the system to increase capacity to meet testing
               demands.118 Many university and industry laboratories can accommodate between
               50 and 100 laboratory staff and have access to the necessary equipment to process
               tests.119

     2.64. However, Professor McNally told the Inquiry that universities with existing laboratory
           capacity, equipment and expertise were “plundered” to resource the new Lighthouse
           laboratories rather than being used primarily as test-processing facilities.120 In the
           short term, establishment of the Lighthouse network involved the redistribution
           of existing capacity rather than the creation of new capacity.

     2.65. The Inquiry accepts that the ability to use existing capacity may have been
           constrained early in the pandemic by the high level of containment required by
           laboratories handling SARS-CoV-2 samples. However, testing capacity remained low
           after the containment level was changed at the end of February 2020, and existing
           laboratory capacity was still not used. The capacity to process tests could have been
           increased – while the Lighthouse laboratories were being established – by using
           academic and local laboratories.

     2.66. Industry expertise was also not consistently recognised or used by the UK
           government and devolved administrations. One private sector organisation
           reported that, in April and May 2020, guidance was issued by Public Health
           England’s Colindale laboratory that advised NHS hospitals not to use private
           commercial laboratories to diagnose Covid-19.121 There was no clear alternative
           provider for many hospitals, so some did not follow the guidance.122

     2.67.     The Inquiry was told that the development of the Lighthouse laboratories could
               have been approached differently, and that better value for money might have
               been achieved through closer partnership with existing laboratories. Dr Colin Fink
               (co-founder of Micropathology Ltd, a diagnostic and research company) explained:

                   “We had 26 years of rapid diagnostic assay development and laboratory rapid
                     diagnostic service experience and we had privately estimated that we could set
                     up a high through put facility in the University Science Park for about 15% of the
                     cost of the later published £1.1 billion which was finally spent on the Mega
                     [Lighthouse] laboratory in Leamington.”123

=== PAGE 53 ===
                                                                                                          Chapter 2: Testing




2.68. A number of possible explanations were advanced for the limited use of existing
      laboratory networks. These included:

          • a lack of awareness within the UK government of existing capacity;
          • a lack of engagement with existing laboratories;
          • the view that such capacity was “not scalable”;
          • concerns about data protection and the complexity of data-sharing;
          • the challenge of maintaining consistent testing quality; and
          • the logistical difficulties associated with coordinating multiple smaller providers.                                   124



2.69. Professor McNally considered these concerns to be overstated. He told the Inquiry
      that staff processing diagnostic tests did not require access to patients’ personal
      information and that the tests being used were validated using appropriate controls
      to monitor quality.125

2.70. Existing private and academic laboratories represented a valuable but under-utilised
      resource in the early months of the pandemic. Many were effectively ‘ready to go’,
      with established equipment, laboratory space and trained staff. There was typically
      a delay of one to two months between the decision to establish a Lighthouse
      laboratory and it becoming operational.126 Making greater use of existing sites could
      therefore have provided a reasonable, and possibly more cost-effective, short-term
      means of increasing testing capacity while longer-term solutions were developed.


The UK’s diagnostic testing infrastructure
2.71.     Professor McNally told the Inquiry that having a stronger testing infrastructure in
          place “could have allowed us to very quickly identify hotspots of transmission and
          get slightly ahead of the virus”.127 As noted in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report, the UK
          government and devolved administrations should have invested in this infrastructure
          in advance of the Covid-19 pandemic.128

2.72. Internationally, the governments of Germany and South Korea provide funding to
      academic, clinical and commercial laboratories that are accredited as emergency
      testing laboratories.129 In Germany, these laboratories register their trained staff and
      remain on standby to divert to diagnostic testing when required – overseen by the

=== PAGE 54 ===
 Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




          Federal Government.130 Their ability to switch rapidly to infectious disease diagnostic
          testing is reviewed on an annual basis.131

 2.73. Establishing a comparable laboratory funding model in the UK would require
       investment.132 However, the use of such ‘surge’ laboratories and staffing
       could strengthen routine diagnostic capacity and provide a more resilient and
       flexible infrastructure for future pandemics. The UK government and devolved
       administrations should examine the feasibility of adopting a similar approach.

 2.74.    Since the pandemic, most Lighthouse laboratories have been decommissioned, and
          the testing infrastructure created for Covid-19 no longer exists.133 Although it would
          not be appropriate to maintain full testing infrastructure outside of a pandemic, the
          UK government and devolved administrations must be prepared to scale up again.
          Professor McNally told the Inquiry that if another pandemic were to occur in the
          near future, “in terms of delivering tests, we’ll be exactly where we were at the start
          of 2020”.134 In March 2026, the UK government committed to working with industry
          and academia to explore the use of surge laboratories and a reserve scientific
          workforce in England.135 The Inquiry supports this commitment, which should
          also be considered by the devolved administrations.

 2.75. The UK government and devolved administrations should prioritise developing
       a clearer understanding of the existing life sciences and diagnostic expertise
       available across the UK, alongside stronger working relationships with those sectors.
       It is vital that, in a future outbreak of a highly infectious disease, decision-makers
       have immediate access to accurate and comprehensive information about the
       infrastructure available to support testing.

 2.76. The Inquiry recommends that the UK government and devolved administrations
       maintain up-to-date information on the UK’s research and clinical laboratory
       capabilities, including details of laboratory accreditation, equipment and staffing
       expertise. This would support the ambition described by James Bethell, Lord
       Bethell (Parliamentary Under Secretary of State for Technology, Innovation and
       Life Sciences at the Department of Health and Social Care from March 2020 to
       September 2021, who had junior ministerial responsibility for the establishment
       of NHS Test and Trace), of having a comprehensive, pre-established testing strategy
       that could be implemented immediately at the outset of a pandemic.136 Plans for the
       rapid repurposing of these laboratories during a pandemic should be developed
       and kept under regular review, alongside plans to increase testing capacity swiftly.

          Recommendation 1: Monitor testing capabilities and prepare plans
          for rapid scale-up of test processing
          The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and the Northern
          Ireland Executive should compile information about the testing capabilities of
          academic, clinical and research laboratories, including the types of diagnostics
          they process and the equipment they use.
          This information should be updated regularly and shared between the four
          governments of the UK.
          Each government should also develop a plan, as part of their wider planning
          for pandemics, to enable the test processing capacity in these laboratories
          to be increased quickly in a public health emergency.



Material shortages
2.77.     Between March and April 2020, the number of tests processed was considerably
          lower than the laboratory capacity available, despite the increased capacity. It soon
          became clear that laboratory capacity alone was not the only factor limiting testing.

2.78. The UK-wide Coronavirus (COVID-19): Scaling Up Our Testing Programmes policy
      paper acknowledged that a range of other challenges affected testing output such
      as “global shortage of materials needed to run the end-to-end testing process at
      full capacity”.137 This included reagents – the substances used to trigger chemical
      reactions to detect the presence of a virus.

2.79. As discussed in the Inquiry’s Module 5 Report, the production of healthcare
      equipment, including diagnostic tests, relies on a global supply chain involving
      raw-material suppliers, manufacturers and distributors cooperating and competing
      in the open market to meet the demands of end users. The pandemic placed great
      pressure on supply chains and created challenging market conditions. This made it
      difficult for the UK government and devolved administrations to secure and distribute
      essential materials.138 These included supplies for testing, such as swabs, the single-
      use plastics required for laboratory analysis and reagents.139

2.80. The UK government and devolved administrations recognised the risk of supply
      constraints at an early stage. Professor (later Sir) Jonathan Van-Tam, Deputy Chief
      Medical Officer for England from October 2017 to March 2022, had noted in early
      January 2020 that it can take several weeks to distribute reagents internationally.140

programmes; INQ000106460)

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




              A Department of Health and Social Care presentation dated 9 February 2020
              referred to joint working with the devolved administrations to “improve the supply
              chain for reagents”.141

     2.81.    The UK did not produce sufficient volume of the necessary chemical reagents
              domestically. Plans to increase domestic manufacture were considered in March
              2020.142

     2.82. The issue of reagent supply was not one that the UK faced alone. Between 4 and
           5 March 2020, the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (an agency
           of the European Union focusing on infectious diseases surveillance, response and
           preparedness) identified a number of key material shortages that could restrict
           testing efforts across Europe, which included shortages of RT-PCR reagents.143

     2.83. By 19 March 2020, reagent supply was described as a “limiting factor” to the
           expansion of RT-PCR testing capacity by officials in NHS England.144 The UK
           government started directing resources to the issue, with close working between
           Public Health England and NHS England, supported by procurement specialists
           from the Department of Health and Social Care and the Cabinet Office.145

     2.84. On 2 April 2020, Mr Hancock publicly stated that the UK was “tackling the shortage
           of reagents”.146 By mid-April, a dedicated triage team and expert procurement
           group had been formed, and the Department of Health and Social Care reported
           that current reagent suppliers could meet demand sufficiently.147 The UK’s testing
           capacity continued to increase, but the reliance on international suppliers amid
           global competition meant the supply of reagents remained precarious.148

     2.85. Reagent supply constraints were also compounded by reliance on ‘closed’ RT‑PCR
           testing platforms. These platforms require reagents produced by the same
           manufacturer that supplies the machine to test the sample.149 Although ‘closed’
           platforms were considered to be more efficient, the UK’s dependence on a small
           number of global manufacturers further limited supply options, particularly as none
           of those suppliers were manufacturing test kits or reagents in the UK at that time.150
           Efforts were later made to expand the use of ‘open’ platforms (testing machines that

         are able to use a wider range of reagents), including by utilising machines within the
         NHS and in academic and university laboratories.151

2.86. Better planning is needed. It should include measures to secure the supply of the
      materials required for laboratory testing alongside expanding laboratory processing
      capacity. The Inquiry has already recommended (see Recommendation 6 of the
      Inquiry’s Module 5 Report) that emergency and international trade arrangements,
      together with domestic industry, should form part of the whole-system civil
      emergency strategy to support testing efforts and to ensure that testing capacity
      can be scaled up efficiently.152 In March 2026, the UK government committed to
      build appropriate stockpiles for England of chemicals and equipment required for
      testing, to increase UK-based production of critical diagnostic components, and to
      put in place mitigations for supply chain risks.153 The UK government and devolved
      administrations should also consider targeted investment to support increased
      domestic production of essential testing materials, including reagents. This should
      be considered alongside efforts to expand the use of ‘open’ testing platforms.


Prioritisation of RT-PCR tests
2.87.    Prioritising access to RT-PCR testing was necessary in the early stages of the
         pandemic, because of limited testing capacity across the UK. The principal purpose
         of prioritisation was to identify the people who required urgent clinical care and to
         protect workforces in high-risk settings.

2.88. Once RT-PCR tests had been allocated to the four nations of the UK according to
      population share, each government retained discretion over how those tests were
      prioritised within its jurisdiction.154 Weekly meetings between health ministers from
      the UK government and devolved administrations provided a forum to discuss
      testing policy and to align approaches, where appropriate. Robin Swann (Minister of
      Health for Northern Ireland from January 2020 to October 2022 and from February
      to May 2024) told the Inquiry:

              “While I do not now remember the detail of all policy changes, timings and
                differences, I believe that, in general, across the course of the pandemic,
                eligibility and prioritisation of PCR and LFD [lateral flow device] testing at
                strategic, population level was generally consistent.”155

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     2.89. In England, the Department of Health and Social Care, Public Health England and
           NHS England agreed initial testing priorities on 11 March 2020. This resulted in a
           focus on testing symptomatic hospital patients, with priorities as follows:

               • Group 1 – patients requiring critical care for the management of pneumonia, acute
                   respiratory distress syndrome or influenza-like illness, or an alternative indication
                   of severe illness;
               • Group 2 – all other patients requiring hospital admission for pneumonia, acute
                   respiratory distress syndrome or influenza-like illness; and
               • Group 3 – clusters of disease in residential or care settings, such as long-term
                   care facilities, prisons and boarding schools.156

               The devolved administrations adopted similar approaches to prioritisation during
               this period. Testing for symptomatic hospital patients was rolled out across all four
               nations between 13 and 19 March 2020.157

     2.90. Prioritisation was later extended beyond clinical care. By the end of March 2020,
           symptomatic healthcare workers across the UK were entitled to access testing.158
           Symptomatic residents of residential care (care home) settings became eligible for
           testing across the UK between March and May 2020.159 Testing for symptomatic
           social care workers was also introduced across the UK during the same period.160

     2.91.     While the UK government and devolved administrations broadly followed
               similar approaches to prioritisation, the timing of policy implementation differed.
               For example, in Northern Ireland, routine symptomatic testing of all care home
               residents and staff followed an initial focus on settings experiencing potential
               outbreaks or clusters of infection.161 Offers of asymptomatic testing for care home

          residents and social care workers varied, reflecting differences in the resources
          available in each nation.162

2.92. Given the constraints on RT-PCR testing in the early stages of the pandemic,
      some form of prioritisation was necessary. The Inquiry notes the UK government’s
      commitment to develop a prioritisation model for testing in the early stages of
      a pandemic.163 Any such model – and any equivalent models developed by the
      devolved administrations – must be adaptable to different types of virus and to
      varying levels of testing capacity.

2.93. However, decisions to prioritise groups for testing are of limited value if, in practice,
      those groups cannot access the tests. During the Covid-19 pandemic, concerns were
      raised about instances in which healthcare and social care workers were unable to
      obtain testing, despite being designated as priority groups.164 In future pandemics,
      the UK government and devolved administrations must ensure that prioritisation
      decisions are supported by sufficient and accessible testing capacity.


Antibody testing during Covid-19
2.94. An antibody is a component of the immune system that is produced after infection
      or vaccination with a pathogen, and it identifies – and helps to neutralise – future
      infections from the same pathogen. Testing for antibodies involves analysing blood
      samples to identify people who have previously been exposed to a pathogen, rather
      than diagnosing an active infection.165 It can be used to build an understanding
      of how many people have been infected with a virus. The presence of antibodies
      does not, however, mean that a person is immune from being infected again or from
      transmitting the virus to others. Scientific assessment is required to determine how
      long antibodies persist and the extent to which they provide protection.166

2.95. The UK government and devolved administrations considered the role of antibody
      testing for Covid-19 as early as February 2020.167 Antibody testing was discussed at
      a mass testing meeting at 10 Downing Street on 17 March 2020 and was included as

               Pillar 3 in the UK-wide Coronavirus (COVID-19): Scaling Up Our Testing Programmes,
               published in April 2020.168

     2.96. At this early stage of the pandemic, antibody testing was viewed by the UK
           government and devolved administrations as a means of enabling people who
           had previously been infected to “get back to work and normal life”.169 However, at
           that time, there was no definitive scientific evidence to establish whether a positive
           antibody test meant that a person was protected from reinfection.170

     2.97.     Scientific understanding of the role and significance of Covid-19 antibodies began
               to develop in April 2020. On 9 April, the Scientific Advisory Group for Emergencies
               (SAGE) advised that it was not yet known whether antibodies provided protection
               against infection or transmission and noted that international data showed low
               levels of antibodies in the population.171 By 21 April, SAGE reported that a clear
               understanding of the duration of any protection provided by antibodies would
               not be available for some time.172

     2.98. On 21 May 2020, the UK government purchased 10 million antibody tests and
           announced that testing would be offered to NHS and care staff in England. It stated
           that a positive antibody result did not indicate immunity from Covid-19, and that
           those tested would still need to follow public health measures, including social
           distancing measures and the use of personal protective equipment (PPE). The stated
           purpose of the programme was to support scientific understanding of the prevalence
           of the virus.173 The devolved administrations received antibody tests through the UK
           government’s procurement arrangements and made their own decisions about their
           use. The Welsh Government offered antibody testing to NHS staff, care staff and
           teachers from June 2020, whereas the Scottish Government and the Department
           of Health (Northern Ireland) limited the use of antibody tests primarily to research
           purposes.174 By late August 2021, a UK-wide antibody surveillance programme
           allowed all adults to opt in to antibody testing when booking an RT-PCR test.
           Of those who volunteered, up to 8,000 per day would receive two antibody tests
           to complete at home.175

                                                                                                         Chapter 2: Testing




2.99. The Inquiry understands the decision of the UK government to purchase antibody
      tests at a time when scientific understanding of Covid-19 antibodies was still
      developing. It wished to procure a range of testing technologies in order to assess
      their effectiveness and to support research into the disease, and it expressed
      caution about their likely effectiveness.176

2.100. However, even if a large proportion of the population had been tested and found to
       have antibodies, it would not have been possible to ease measures such as social
       distancing or to reduce tracing or self-isolation requirements, without scientific
       evidence that antibodies provided protective immunity.177 Antibody testing was a
       useful research tool to develop understanding of Covid-19, but testing methods
       capable of identifying active infection offered greater potential to support the safe
       easing of restrictions.


Antigen testing during Covid-19
2.101. Antigen testing is not a new public health concept. By way of example, pregnancy
       tests are a form of lateral flow test.178 In the context of Covid-19, lateral flow tests
       involved nasal and throat swabs, similar to the RT-PCR test, but they produced
       results at home within minutes.179

2.102. Although lateral flow tests are less sensitive than RT-PCR tests, they are effective
       at identifying most people who are infectious. Their relative speed and low cost
       mean that they can be deployed more readily for repeated mass testing.180 Mass
       testing enables rapid identification of infections, including among people without
       symptoms.181 It can also allow earlier initiation of contact tracing and self-isolation
       which, taken together, may reduce the spread of a virus.182


Development and rollout of lateral flow testing
2.103. Antigen testing was considered an “urgent need” at the 17 March 2020 meeting
       at 10 Downing Street, about testing.183 In August 2020, Public Health England
       established an evaluation programme for lateral flow devices.184 Most of the products
       assessed were found to be non-viable. The first lateral flow test passed validation

           in September 2020.185 Following direction from Mr Johnson in October 2020, the
           Department of Health and Social Care bought lateral flow tests from three suppliers

           16 October 2020, Mr Johnson announced that millions of the lateral flow tests had
           been purchased. He stated that these tests would be trialled and distributed across
           the UK over the following weeks.187

 Operation Moonshot

 2.104. At about this time, the UK government was also considering a proposal to eradicate
        or substantially reduce the prevalence of the virus through mass population testing.
        The proposal involved testing the entire population over a one- to two-week period,
        requiring those who tested positive to self-isolate, and allowing those who tested
        negative to resume normal activities without restrictions. This became known as
        ‘Operation Moonshot’. It was distinct from the broader test, trace and isolate system
        and was intended to reduce the need for lockdowns.188 The concept had been under
        consideration by the UK government since the summer of 2020, and it was launched
        by the Department of Health and Social Care in September 2020.189

 2.105. Baroness Harding identified two significant challenges with Operation Moonshot.
        The first, as she told the Inquiry, was that the success of the proposal depended on
        extremely high levels of public participation and compliance.190 Even relatively small
        levels of non-participation could enable continued transmission of the virus.191 Lord
        Bethell also expressed reservations about the possibility of eradicating the virus
        through this approach, particularly given the high levels of prevalence of the virus
        at the time the proposal was being considered.192

 2.106. The second difficulty with the Moonshot programme was the sheer scale of testing
        it required.193 Delivering the volume of tests envisaged would have required a
        substantial expansion in testing sites and associated infrastructure.194 Baroness
        Harding told the Inquiry that she was “deeply sceptical that it was practically
        possible”.195 This assessment was shared by Professor Van-Tam, who described
        the proposal as “practically unworkable”.196

2.107. The Moonshot programme was influenced, in part, by plans for a similar mass-testing
       initiative undertaken in Slovakia.197 In October to November 2020, the Slovakian
       government tested approximately 97% of its population aged between 10 and 65
       over a two-day period, identifying about 38,000 new cases.198 In November 2020,
       there was a reduction in infection rates in Slovakia, but there was a subsequent
       increase in the number of people becoming ill and requiring hospital care in
       December 2020. This led to the imposition of a lockdown and the abandonment
       of further mass testing.199 Given that the UK’s population is more than 10 times
       larger than Slovakia’s population of 5.4 million, it is likely that the scale and cost
       of delivering a comparable programme in the UK would be far greater.

2.108. As doubts about the value and practicality of the Moonshot programme grew, the
       UK government shifted its focus towards more targeted community testing. Lateral
       flow tests that had been procured for the Moonshot programme were repurposed
       for this plan.200 On 9 November 2020, Mr Johnson announced that lateral flow tests
       would be used for asymptomatic community testing among people living in selected
       English local authorities with a high prevalence of Covid-19.201 Baroness Harding told
       the Inquiry that she and her team were relieved by this change in direction, as focus
       on the Moonshot programme “undoubtedly” delayed the development of alternative
       testing strategies by NHS Test and Trace during October and November 2020.202

Testing pilots

2.109. Research played an important role in demonstrating the potential effectiveness
       of community-based lateral flow testing to the UK government and devolved
       administrations.

2.110. The most notable early trial of lateral flow tests was the Liverpool mass testing
       pilot, which began on 6 November 2020 and focused on testing people without
       symptoms.203 It represented an early step towards the use of mass testing in the UK.
       It achieved voluntary participation from approximately one-quarter of Liverpool’s
       population within one month.204 It increased the identification of people infected
       with Covid-19 by about a fifth, including people who were likely to be infectious but
       who did not have symptoms, enabling them to self-isolate promptly.205 There was
       also a reduction in the number of people in Liverpool requiring hospital care for
       severe Covid-19.206 Although the pilot exposed limitations (including the significant
       proportions of the population that did not participate and the lower-than-expected
       sensitivity of lateral flow tests), it also demonstrated the value of collaborating with

 2.111.    The devolved administrations also implemented pilot exercises for lateral flow
           testing. Trials began in Wales and Scotland in November 2020 and in Northern
           Ireland in December 2020.208 As with RT-PCR testing, lateral flow tests were
           procured centrally by the UK government and allocated to the four nations on
           the basis of population share.209 The devolved administrations retained discretion
           to opt out of procurement arrangements, to request additional procurements by the
           UK government, and to determine how to use their supplies of tests.210

 Expansion of community testing

 2.112. As transmission of the virus increased during the second wave of the pandemic,
        the UK government published its COVID-19 Winter Plan on 23 November 2020.
        The plan formally launched the community testing programme.211 This programme
        aimed to deliver mass testing in local authority areas with high levels of Covid-19
        transmission.212 It was subsequently expanded to cover all local authorities in
        England, following the announcement of lockdown restrictions on 4 January 2021.213
        Expansions of community testing using lateral flow tests also took place in Scotland
        from 18 January and in Wales and Northern Ireland from March 2021.214

 2.113. The COVID-19 Winter Plan also set out an expansion in asymptomatic testing.
        This included the introduction of rapid testing for patient-facing healthcare staff,
        social care staff and care home residents and visitors.215 By January 2021, lateral
        flow testing was available to patient-facing staff across all healthcare systems in the
        UK.216 Testing for social care staff and care home visitors became available across all

2.114. Asymptomatic testing was later extended to the education sector, following
       pilot exercises in late 2020. Regular testing of school staff and pupils began to
       be implemented in Wales and England in January 2021, in Scotland in February
       2021, and in Northern Ireland in March 2021.219 In England, modelling estimated
       that regular testing in secondary schools prevented between 5,000 and 8,000
       infections among pupils during a five-week evaluation period, between March and
       April 2021.220 The Inquiry is examining the use of rapid testing in schools during this
       period in Module 8: Children and young people.

2.115. The successful expansion of asymptomatic lateral flow testing across the four nations
       during late 2020 and the spring of 2021 increased the identification of people with
       Covid-19. In the absence of this testing, many infected people would have remained
       undetected, increasing transmission and the risks for those who were clinically
       vulnerable or working in high-risk settings. Strategies that combined testing in
       specific sectors with targeted testing in areas with high transmission made effective
       use of the growing availability of lateral flow tests.

2.116. As the Covid-19 pandemic continued into 2021, lateral flow testing was applied in a
       number of additional contexts:

          • The universal testing offer enabled anyone in the population (including people
              without symptoms) to access free lateral flow tests. This was rolled out across
              England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland in April 2021.221

Limitations to the rollout of lateral flow tests

Route to market

2.119. While the introduction of lateral flow tests represented a significant turning point
       for the UK’s testing capability and eventually allowed for asymptomatic community
       testing, their development and rollout were subject to important limitations. One
       significant limitation was the substantial delay before lateral flow tests were available
       at scale. Widespread use was not achieved until almost 10 months after the first
       people with Covid-19 were identified in the UK.

2.120. Prior to the summer of 2020, no lateral flow test kits met the required standards for
       use in the UK. To support the development of viable test kits, the Medicines and
       Healthcare products Regulatory Agency (the UK’s medicines regulator) produced
       ‘Target Product Profiles’ of tests, which summarised key features and anticipated
       performance standards.230 These profiles were published on 15 June 2020,
       approximately three months before equivalent test profiles were issued by the
       World Health Organization. This earlier publication likely supported manufacturers
       in aligning test development with UK regulatory requirements.231

2.121. Once manufacturers had developed a test kit, it was required to undergo validation
       against pre-determined standards in order to comply with The Medical Devices
       Regulations 2002 and to be placed on the UK market.232 In addition to regulatory
       approval, test kits were required to pass further evaluation by Public Health
       England.233 This evaluation programme assessed whether test kits met the required
       standards for specificity (the accurate identification of people who were not infected)
       and sensitivity (the accurate identification of people who were infected).234

 Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




 2.122. All lateral flow tests procured by the UK government were subject to this evaluation
        programme.235 Although a large number of potential suppliers were identified, most
        did not have products that met the required performance standards.236 By July 2021,
        34 lateral flow tests had successfully passed all phases of validation, from a pool of
        more than 500 products reviewed by the Department of Health and Social Care.237
        The evaluation protocol and the process through which lateral flow tests were
        validated are considered further below.

 2.123. Following successful validation, manufacturers were required to comply with the
        regulatory requirements set by the Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory
        Agency.238 While regulatory standards for Covid-19 testing kits were not reduced,
        the agency adopted a flexible and pragmatic approach to support a timely response
        to the pandemic.239 This included permitting components of sample collection
        kits – such as swabs and test containers, which would ordinarily require separate
        conformity assessments – to be assessed and approved as a single unit.240

 2.124. In addition, where sample collection kits met the necessary criteria but had not
        yet received formal assurance marking, they could be authorised for use under
        ‘exceptional use authorisations’.241 These provided temporary permission to
        place devices on the UK market in the “interests of protection of public health”.242
        Manufacturers operating under such authorisations were required to report any
        adverse incidents to the Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency
        on a monthly basis.243

 2.125. Once lateral flow tests had been authorised for use in the UK, the Department of
        Health and Social Care and NHS Test and Trace were responsible for recommending
        purchasing decisions.244 In total, more than 2.7 billion lateral flow tests were procured
        for use across the UK during the course of the pandemic.245

 2.126. The Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency played an important
        role in enabling the timely approval of diagnostic testing technologies during the
        pandemic. Its flexible approach enabled diagnostic tools to be approved at pace to
        support the pandemic response. Although the rollout of lateral flow tests took many
        months, the UK’s experience broadly reflected the pace at which such technologies
        were introduced worldwide.246

2.127. Nonetheless, the first lateral flow test manufactured in the UK was not deployed
       until September 2021.247 This highlighted limitations in the UK’s domestic diagnostic
       manufacturing capability. The UK government and devolved administrations must
       invest in the diagnostics sector to enable the early development of rapid at-home
       tests in a future pandemic. Earlier development and rollout of lateral flow tests would
       reduce reliance on tests requiring laboratory processing capacity.248 This would
       help alleviate the challenges in laboratory capacity experienced during the Covid-19
       pandemic. Professor Vallance told the Inquiry: “[T]he ability to make an effective
       lateral flow type test should be a priority for any future pandemic of this type.”249

2.128. Professor Christopher Molloy (Chief Executive Officer from November 2016 to
       January 2026 of Medicines Discovery Catapult, a service that was instrumental
       in the scaling up of testing) emphasised that it was:

              “an important part of our preparation to make sure that we have lateral flow
               ready to go, and it is vital that we maintain a UK manufacturing capacity, and a
               supply chain of invention and innovation through our diagnostic companies, to
               make sure that we have sovereign capability for, if this happens again, we can
               turn lateral flow on more quickly”.250

2.129. The 100 Days Mission, co-chaired at its establishment in 2021 by Professor Vallance,
       identified the need for rapid at-home tests to be available within 100 days of the
       emergence of any new epidemic or pandemic.251 During the Covid-19 pandemic,
       however, the first rapid diagnostic tests that did not require laboratory processing did
       not receive World Health Organization approval until 236 days had elapsed.252 The
       Inquiry endorses the objective of accelerating the development of rapid diagnostic
       tests through investment in diagnostic research and improved coordination between
       government, the diagnostics industry and research institutions.253

2.130. The Inquiry also welcomes the UK Health Security Agency’s announcement in
       December 2024 of a ‘Diagnostic Accelerator’, a specialist team intended to enhance
       the UK’s preparedness to diagnose and test for new and emerging infectious disease
       threats.254 In March 2026, the UK government further committed to strengthening

     Quality of lateral flow test kits

     2.131. While speed was a critical factor in developing testing devices, it was also essential
            that such tests met sufficiently high quality and safety standards.

     2.132. The first lateral flow test to pass validation, Innova, was reported to have a specificity
            of 99.68% and an overall sensitivity of 76.8%.256 However, subsequent assessments
            indicated that sensitivity was lower when tests were self-administered. A Public
            Health England evaluation estimated the sensitivity of Innova at 57.5%, while an
            independent study estimated it to be as low as 47.9%. This meant that about half
            of infected people might have been given false reassurance by a negative result.257
            This variability in sensitivity was not reflected in official press communications, which
            tended to emphasise the high sensitivity of lateral flow tests.258

     2.133. The deployment of lateral flow testing generated debate within the scientific
            community. Some scientists expressed concerns that lower sensitivity could result
            in infected people failing to self-isolate, particularly if they relied on negative
            test results. Others welcomed the use of lateral flow tests as a valuable tool
            for widespread screening, enabling the identification of infectious people who
            might otherwise not have been tested, rather than limiting testing to people
            with symptoms.259

     2.134. International approaches to lateral flow testing reflected similar uncertainties.
            In some countries, the greater accuracy of RT-PCR tests and uncertainty about
            the performance of antigen tests led to their more cautious adoption. New Zealand
            relied primarily on RT-PCR testing for much of 2021, introducing antigen testing
            in November 2021, in response to emerging Covid-19 variants.260

     2.135. Lateral flow testing was also deployed in the USA, but concerns later emerged
            about the clinical performance of certain tests. On 10 June 2021, the Food and Drug
            Administration advised people in the USA to stop using the Innova lateral flow test

         due to a range of concerns, one of which was test performance.261 In response,
         the Department of Health and Social Care conducted an investigation into whether
         these concerns applied to use of the Innova test in the UK. It concluded that
         they did not, because the tests were used for different purposes in the UK, and
         because additional mitigations were in place that had not been adopted in the
         USA. The Department of Health and Social Care also relied on real-world use, which
         suggested that the tests were performing better than early sensitivity estimates.262

         in the UK, the Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency accepted the
         findings of the investigation and did not require tests to be recalled.263

2.136. Developing a clearer and more standardised process for the evaluation of diagnostic
       tests would help to ensure that appropriate tests are deployed quickly and to avoid
       these concerns. Such a process should be developed in consultation with scientists,
       regulators and manufacturers, and it should be made publicly available to support
       transparency.264

2.137. Ongoing (or ‘post-market’) surveillance is also necessary to monitor the performance
       of rapid testing tests, particularly where emerging disease variants may affect test
       reliability. During the Covid-19 pandemic, the Medicines and Healthcare products
       Regulatory Agency relied on its Yellow Card scheme, through which individuals could
       report adverse incidents, faults or concerns relating to testing kits.265 While useful,
       the Yellow Card scheme relies primarily on members of the public voluntarily
       submitting reports.

2.138. The post-market surveillance regime has been strengthened since the Covid-19
       pandemic, through legislative reform, providing the Medicines and Healthcare
       products Regulatory Agency with enhanced powers to monitor medical devices
       and impose more stringent obligations on manufacturers. The Medical Devices
       (Post‑market Surveillance Requirements) (Amendment) (Great Britain) Regulations
       2024 came into force on 16 June 2025.266 The Inquiry endorses these reforms: in a
       future public health emergency, they should contribute to improved device quality
       and to the availability of diagnostic tests that are both rapid and reliable.

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     Trust in lateral flow testing

     2.139. Public and media scepticism about the accuracy of lateral flow testing emerged
            as the tests became more widely used.267 Throughout 2021, levels of confidence in
            the accuracy of RT-PCR tests remained higher than confidence in lateral flow tests.268
            For example, some contributors to the Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story
            Matters, were not convinced that a lateral flow test done at home, which provided a
            result so quickly, could be accurate when they were accustomed to accurate medical
            testing being done in a clinical setting.269

     2.140. Differences in the underlying technology of diagnostic tests contributed to public
            confusion. Different diagnostic tests could produce different results. This is because
            RT-PCR tests detect fragments of RNA before, during and after the infectious period;
            lateral flow tests primarily detect infection during the infectious period. As a result,
            RT-PCR tests could return positive results for days – and sometimes weeks – after
            a person was no longer infectious.270

     2.141. The distinctions were well understood by the scientific community and by officials,
            who advised decision-makers about the relative strengths and limitations of different
            testing technologies.271 In recognition of this, the UK government and devolved
            administrations introduced confirmatory RT-PCR testing following a positive lateral
            flow test result to mitigate the limitations.272

     2.142. Nonetheless, evidence indicates that some members of the public remained
            confused about the difference between the two diagnostic tests and the role
            of asymptomatic testing. As a result, some public behaviour was not aligned
            with the testing guidance in place.273 Detail about the ways in which testing was
            communicated to the public, including areas for improvement, is set out in Chapter 6:
            Communications and data.

Impact of lateral flow testing
2.143. The development and distribution of lateral flow tests across the UK represented
       a significant advance in the testing response to Covid-19. Their widespread
       availability had a considerable impact on the UK’s ability to monitor infection and
       manage transmission. By June 2022, more than 2.4 billion lateral flow tests had
       been distributed across the UK, compared with more than 178 million RT-PCR tests
       distributed over the same period.274 The UK implemented large-scale lateral flow
       testing earlier than other countries did.275

2.144. The sustained use of lateral flow testing also played an important role in enabling the
       majority of the population to adapt to living with Covid-19. Lateral flow tests provided
       results directly to users within minutes, cost about 10 times less than RT-PCR tests
       and removed the need for laboratory-based sample processing and transport.276
       Professor Julian Hiscox (Professor of Infection and Global Health at the University of
       Liverpool and Director of the UK International Coronavirus Network) described lateral
       flow tests as a “game changer in handing responsibility to people for their own
       disease management”.277 Similar themes were reflected through Every Story Matters.


               “The lateral flow testing was so much easier than the PCR in the sense of not
                 having to wait for the results to be sent away and come back. You knew
                 there and then whether you had to start your … isolation or not […] if you were
                 to have sent a PCR away, thinking you had Covid, you’d … been … isolating
                 for three days …, and then it … turns out you’ve … isolated for nothing
                 because it’s not Covid. So that aspect of it was great, I thought.”278

                Carer, Every Story Matters


2.145. The Inquiry recognises the considerable contribution made by rapid at-home
       testing to the UK’s pandemic response, as well as the significant efforts of those
       who developed, validated, manufactured and distributed lateral flow tests at scale
       across the UK. Planning for future pandemics should place greater emphasis on
       the role of rapid at-home testing and on the steps that the UK government and
       devolved administrations can take to support its early development and deployment.
       Improving preparedness in this area will be an important element of strengthening
       the UK’s resilience to future public health emergencies.

     Consideration of alternative test types
     2.146. Throughout the Covid-19 pandemic, the UK’s testing strategy focused predominantly
            on RT-PCR testing and the later introduction of lateral flow tests. While both
            technologies played an important role, there were opportunities for the UK
            government and devolved administrations to improve access to testing, or to
            make greater use of alternative testing methods.

     2.147. RT-PCR testing provided high accuracy but involved significant cost, complex
            laboratory processing, and, in some circumstances, it was overly sensitive.
            Lateral flow tests, by contrast, provided rapid results and could be used at home, but
            concerns about their quality remained, particularly in the early stages of deployment.
            Although the UK government and devolved administrations focused efforts on these
            two technologies, the use of other diagnostic and surveillance tests could have
            strengthened and enhanced the effectiveness of the National Testing Programme.


     Diagnostic testing
     2.148. RT-LAMP is a well-established rapid diagnostic technology.279 LamPORE,
            which combines RT-LAMP with nanopore sequencing (a technology that reads
            genetic code), was developed in England during the Covid-19 pandemic.280 Both
            technologies demonstrated high sensitivity and specificity, and they were capable of
            producing laboratory results in under 90 minutes.281

     2.149. Pilot exercises using RT-LAMP and LamPORE were carried out in July and October
            2020: both demonstrated effectiveness.282 Although the tests were validated
            by the Department of Health and Social Care in the final months of 2020, their
            implementation remained limited.283 The UK government and Health and Social
            Care (Northern Ireland) deployed RT-LAMP testing on a small scale for healthcare
            workers.284 The Scottish Government and Welsh Government opted not to adopt
            the tests.285

     2.150. Several barriers contributed to the limited use and deployment of these
            technologies.286 The Technical Report on the Covid-19 Pandemic in the UK,

2.151. Nonetheless, RT-LAMP and LamPORE offered potential advantages for certain

       swabs, which were easier for children with special educational needs to use.291
       No similar provision was offered in England, Wales or Scotland.

 Wastewater surveillance
 2.155. Wastewater testing is a well-established public health tool. It has been used to
        monitor diseases, following the discovery of a link between high concentrations
        of polio virus in sewage and increases in polio infection in the population.292
        Unlike individual diagnostic testing, wastewater surveillance does not depend
        on public participation or adherence to self-reporting, reducing the impact of
        behavioural factors on surveillance.293

 2.156. Internationally, the first wastewater testing studies for Covid-19 were initiated in
        February 2020.294 By March 2020, research studies had begun across England,
        Wales and Scotland.295

 2.157. Wastewater monitoring programmes were initiated in England, Wales and Scotland
        following these research studies. In England, a wastewater monitoring programme
        was established by the Joint Biosecurity Centre in the summer of 2020, following
        a pilot programme coordinated by the Department for Environment, Food and
        Rural Affairs.296 By 2022, the UK Health Security Agency led the programme, which
        covered approximately 74% of the population of England.297 In Wales, a wastewater
        monitoring programme began in September 2020, following monitoring earlier in
        the year, capturing close to 70% of the Welsh population by December 2020.298
        Scotland’s wastewater monitoring programme launched in June 2020 and covered
        more than 60% of the population by February 2021.299 At a SAGE meeting on 8 April
        2021, Professor Vallance described wastewater surveillance as being “particularly
        useful in detecting outbreaks when prevalence was low, and to detect the presence
        and geographic spread of new variants”.300

2.158. In Northern Ireland, wastewater surveillance was adopted more slowly. A research
       study was initiated and samples were taken from two sites in Northern Ireland by
       the end of 2020.301 A full surveillance programme was initiated by the Department of
       Agriculture, Environment and Rural Affairs (Northern Ireland), with support from the
       Department of Health (Northern Ireland) in April 2021, and it covered approximately
       64% of the population by February 2022.302

2.159. While it is disappointing that a full wastewater testing programme was not initiated in
       Northern Ireland until April 2021, this delay may, in part, have been linked to a lack of
       existing wastewater research into other viruses in Northern Ireland prior to 2020.303

2.160. A key legacy of the Covid-19 pandemic should be the improved and continued
       use of wastewater surveillance to monitor infectious diseases across the UK.
       Retrospective studies have demonstrated the value of wastewater surveillance for
       Covid-19: analysis, in July 2020, of archived sewage samples from February 2020
       showed that the virus was present in north London three days before the first person
       with Covid-19 was identified in the relevant catchment area.304 The Inquiry therefore
       welcomes the UK Health Security Agency’s announcement on 26 August 2025 of a
       programme to evaluate how wastewater monitoring could be used to detect a range
       of high-risk pathogens.305


Adherence to testing guidance
2.161. Levels of adherence to testing guidance varied over time, across communities and
       regions and for different testing strategies and test types.306 The COVID-19 Rapid
       Survey of Adherence to Interventions and Responses (CORSAIR) study in England
       estimated that, in January 2021, during the third lockdown in England, 62.5% of the
       population intended to request a test if they developed symptoms. This represented
       an increase compared with earlier stages of the pandemic.307

2.162. Despite this increase in expressed intention, the proportion of people who reported
       actually requesting a test when they experienced symptoms remained consistently
       lower than the proportion who stated an intention to do so. In January 2021, 22.2%
       of people reported having requested a test when symptomatic, illustrating what has

               been described as an ‘intention–behaviour gap’.308 Similarly, uptake of asymptomatic
               testing remained limited. When twice-weekly asymptomatic testing was available
               to the UK population, a survey of more than 6,000 adults in England and Scotland
               conducted between April and June 2021 found that only 11% had completed at least

     2.163. Much of the data collected to measure adherence to testing was based on self-
            reported information, for example, through survey responses.310 Although some
            objective data were collected (eg the number of tests carried out), it was not possible
            to determine objectively how many people should have sought testing.311 Given the
            likelihood of ‘social desirability bias’ (ie people are likely to answer questions in a
            way that will be viewed favourably by others), estimates of adherence to testing
            were likely to be overstated.312

     2.164. Adherence to testing guidance required people to navigate a series of steps.
            They needed to recognise when testing was required and then either book and
            attend a testing centre for an administered test, or order, complete and report the
            results for self-administered tests. At each stage, people could fail, reducing overall
            adherence.313

     2.165. A prerequisite for effective adherence was public understanding of when to
            test. This relied on people knowing they had been in contact with someone who
            had tested positive or correctly recognising they had symptoms of Covid-19.314
            Effective public communication should therefore have provided clear and consistent
            information about the symptoms that required testing. However, survey data
            between May and August 2020 indicated that awareness of symptoms remained
            relatively low, with about half of adults able to identify correctly the three main
            symptoms.315 This is explored further in Chapter 6: Communications and data.
            Through Every Story Matters, the Inquiry heard that some contributors found it
            difficult to keep up with changes to testing guidance.

              “I just felt really quite confused throughout the whole thing as to when we

2.166. For those who were aware that testing was required, motivation remained an
       important factor influencing adherence. For some individuals, this was not viewed
       as a choice but as a moral obligation to protect others.317 For others, however,
       the decision was shaped by the potential consequences of receiving a positive
       result, including contact tracing and the need to self-isolate.318 These concerns
       were more pronounced among people who required financial or practical support
       to self-isolate.319 Evidence from the Liverpool mass testing pilot (discussed above)
       suggested that fear of income loss contributed to the reluctance to undergo
       testing.320


              “I was in quite a moral conflict of whether do I put myself first and not test,
                or do I actually put everyone else first and test for their benefit.”321

               Zero-hours contract worker, Every Story Matters


2.167. Even when people understood that they needed to test, and wanted to do so,
       further barriers to adherence existed in an individual’s ability or opportunity to
       access testing.322 Professor Madelynne Arden (expert witness on adherence to
       behaviours associated with test, trace and isolate) explained that focusing solely
       on increasing motivation to test was insufficient:

             “[P]olicy should address the range of factors that cause non-adherence in
               different groups of people … rather than assuming that ‘telling people what
               to do’ is sufficient to promote adherence.”323

2.168. Factors such as the clarity of testing requirements and the ease with which tests can
       be accessed and completed must be addressed when designing testing strategies
       for future pandemics. In doing so, governments must draw more consistently on
       behavioural science expertise.

 Accessibility of tests
 2.169. In order for testing to support contact tracing, self-isolation and monitoring
        of prevalence, it needed to be available across the population. Despite this
        need, accessibility was not addressed as a central design principle in the early
        development of the testing programme. As described by Kristen McLeod (Senior
        Responsible Officer for Pillar 2 from March to May 2020):

               “There was not a great deal of specific consideration given to accessibility for
                particular categories of vulnerable people during the initial period of getting
                Pillar 2 up and running; the focus was, and had to be, on getting the basics of
                the system established in the first place. It could then be tailored to particular
                groups or cohorts of people after the initial set-up period.”324

           However, the UK government and devolved administrations did work to improve the
           accessibility of the testing programme over the course of the pandemic.

 2.170. The rollout of RT-PCR testing through a limited number of testing centres meant that
        access varied significantly by location. Some members of the public were required
        to travel long distances outside their local area to obtain a test.325 This presented
        particular challenges for people who relied on public transport, including students
        and those living in Scotland and Wales (given that data from the most recent
        pre-pandemic census indicated that 30% of households in Scotland and 23% of
        households in Wales did not have access to a car).326

 2.171. By contrast, where individuals had access to a car, the testing process was often
        considerably easier, as described by contributors to Every Story Matters.


                “I booked a Covid test which was really quick and easy. The test centre was
                  about 20 minutes’ drive from me […] The whole process was really clear and
                  well-planned.”327

                 Contributor, Every Story Matters

2.172. Disabled people faced persistent barriers to accessing Covid-19 testing. These
       included physical accessibility issues such as inadequate wheelchair access, a lack
       of designated parking for disabled people and limited accessible lavatory facilities.329
       While the later introduction of pedestrianised testing centres and mobile testing
       units improved accessibility, these measures took time to be implemented across
       the four nations.330 Access difficulties also persisted in some remote and rural areas,
       including Scottish island communities, although such areas generally experienced
       lower numbers of people with Covid-19 and fewer deaths.331

2.173. The introduction of at-home postal RT-PCR testing and the widespread availability
       of lateral flow tests reduced many barriers, particularly those related to geographic
       access.332 However, self-administered tests presented different accessibility
       challenges, as they required a level of mobility, dexterity and motor skills to
       assemble and complete them.333


               “I found them physically hard to open at times. I have a bit of a tremor, so
                 tipping the tiny little bits of liquid [was difficult], and it was a very intricate
                 process which was quite physically challenging.”334

 2.174. Language barriers also created obstacles to accessing testing. People who did not
        speak English, or for whom English was a second language, reported difficulties
        understanding instructions and communicating with testing staff.335


               “[T]he person who was doing the test on me would say something, and I
                 wouldn’t understand what they said, which just makes it more stressful
                 for me.”336

               Contributor, Every Story Matters


 2.175. Although translation services for booking tests, for instructions for self-testing and
        for support at testing centres were introduced over time, people who did not speak
        English were likely to have been disadvantaged in accessing testing during the early
        stages of the pandemic.337 Later developments, such as the inclusion of diagrams in
        lateral flow test kits, helped address some of these challenges.


               “[E]ven if you didn’t read or speak English, looking at the pictures you would
                 instantly know what to do […] there was even […] online videos where you can
                 […] see how people are doing it […] for you to just copy […] it wasn’t hard
                 whatsoever: very, very straightforward.”338

               Contributor, Every Story Matters


 2.176. Blind and partially sighted people were unable independently to access
        drive-in testing or complete at-home testing in the early stages of the pandemic.
        Improvements were delivered, including the introduction of alternative instruction
        formats and use of the Be My Eyes app, but these changes also took time.339
        Michael Wordingham, Policy Manager at the Royal National Institute of Blind People
        from June 2022, said: “‘[R]etrofitting’ meant that the tests could only be made
        partially accessible.”340

 2.177. Digitally excluded people, including those without access to digital technology
        or without confidence in completing basic online tasks, were also affected by
        requirements to book tests and report test results digitally.341 During the Liverpool
        pilot, lower test uptake and higher levels of infection were observed in areas with

2.178. Although the accessibility of the UK’s testing programme improved significantly as
       the pandemic progressed, many of the challenges experienced were predictable,
       and some changes took months to be delivered. It was understandable that the
       testing programme was not fully accessible from the outset, due to the speed
       at which it was created and delivered. Making testing and guidance accessible
       may have delayed rollout. However, had the UK government and devolved
       administrations been better prepared for testing during a pandemic, these
       challenges could have been addressed more quickly.

2.179. Accessibility should be treated as a core design principle in future testing strategies,
       ensuring that rollout and communications are inclusive from the outset and adapted
       to meet different needs. Future pandemic planning must ensure that people who are
       vulnerable are not excluded from access to testing.


A future testing strategy
2.180. Although the testing programme was ultimately successful, its development took
       time. The delay reflected the absence of a comprehensive and well-developed
       testing strategy within the UK government and devolved administrations before and
       during the early stages of the Covid-19 pandemic. As a result, a plan for testing had
       to be developed during the emergency itself. Had an appropriate plan been in place
       at the outset of the pandemic, the efforts to control the spread of the virus might
       have been more effective, and many of the dreadful consequences – if not averted
       entirely – might have been reduced.
