     2.181. The UK government and devolved administrations should now prepare a testing
            strategy for future pandemics. That strategy must be sufficiently flexible to account
            for the differing characteristics of potential future outbreaks. The four governments
            should continue to work closely together to ensure that the benefits delivered by the
            National Testing Programme during the Covid-19 pandemic are preserved for future
            pandemics. The development of such a strategy, as part of a wider test, trace and
            isolate system, is considered further in Chapter 7: An effective future test, trace and
            isolate system.

=== PAGE 85 ===
Chapter 3: Contact tracing

=== PAGE 86 ===
     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




     Introduction
     3.1.      Contact tracing has been used for years to prevent the spread of infectious disease,
               both in the UK and globally.1 It identifies people who have been in contact with a
               person infected with a disease. Action can then be taken to reduce the risk of them
               transmitting that infection to others.2 During the Covid-19 pandemic, this typically
               involved asking or requiring those people to self-isolate.

     3.2.      Two methods of contact tracing were used during the Covid-19 pandemic. The first
               was manual contact tracing, in which contact tracers identified and contacted people
               who had been in contact with an infected person. The second was digital contact
               tracing, which used mobile phone applications (commonly known as apps) to identify
               and notify an infected person’s contacts.

     3.3.      This chapter considers the ways in which both forms of contact tracing were used
               throughout the UK during the Covid-19 pandemic. It analyses the effectiveness of
               their implementation and makes recommendations to ensure that contact tracing
               can be used more effectively in future pandemics.


     Overview of contact tracing
     The benefits of contact tracing
     3.4.      Contact tracing can be extremely valuable in slowing or even stopping the spread
               of infectious disease. Combining contact tracing with testing and isolation has been
               estimated to reduce the reproduction number of the virus (the average number of
               people to whom an infected person would pass the virus) far more than mass testing
               or self-isolation alone. Modelling in June 2020 – based on an analysis of 40,162
               voluntary UK participants in a BBC Four pandemic simulation experiment conducted
               in 2017 to 2018 – suggested that self-isolation together with household quarantine
               (the isolation of an entire household when one person develops symptoms) reduced
               transmission by 37% on average.3 However, when manual contact tracing of all
               contacts was also used, transmission was reduced by 64%.4

     1    For example, contact tracing has been used to control the spread of HIV in Cuba, the USA, USSR and Senegal in the 1980s, the
     spread of rabies in the USA in the early 2000s and small outbreaks of Legionnaires’ disease, norovirus and salmonella as well as
     sexually transmitted infections in the UK over a number of years (INQ000587454_0011 para 48).
     2    INQ000587346_0004-0005 para 11
     3    ‘Contagion: The BBC Four pandemic’, BBC, 22 March 2018 (https://www.bbc.co.uk/programmes/p059y0p1; INQ000562990);
     ‘Effectiveness of isolation, testing, contact tracing, and physical distancing on reducing transmission of SARS-CoV-2 in different
     settings: A mathematical modelling study’, AJ Kucharski, P Klepac, AJK Conlan, SM Kissler, ML Tang, H Fry et al, The Lancet (2020),
     20(10), 1151-1160, pp1151, 1156 (https://doi.org/10.1016/S1473-3099(20)30457-6; INQ000255418). Statistical models are tools for
     analysing complex data, identifying patterns and informing evidence-based decision-making where direct measurement is not
     possible. Figures from modelling are estimates only, and different statistical models will produce different outcomes. These estimates
     are not based on direct observations. They are therefore limited by the underlying assumptions used in the models and are subject to
     uncertainty.
     4    ‘Effectiveness of isolation, testing, contact tracing, and physical distancing on reducing transmission of SARS-CoV-2 in different
     settings: A mathematical modelling study’, AJ Kucharski, P Klepac, AJK Conlan, SM Kissler, ML Tang, H Fry et al, The Lancet (2020),
     20(10), 1151-1160, p1156 (https://doi.org/10.1016/S1473-3099(20)30457-6; INQ000255418)

=== PAGE 87 ===
                                                                                                Chapter 3: Contact tracing




3.5.      During the Covid-19 pandemic, a modelling exercise analysed the effectiveness
          of the test, trace and isolate system in Wales in a range of different scenarios.
          Modelling has its limitations, but in one scenario it was estimated that about 10%
          of the forecast reduction in the infection’s reproductive rate was due to contact
          tracing.5 In another scenario, it was estimated that contact tracing accounted for
          about 22% of the reduction in the infection’s reproductive rate.6 Overall, the study
          concluded that contact tracing “remained a significant component” in most of the
          scenarios.7

3.6.      Contact tracing has the greatest potential to affect the course of a pandemic when
          infection rates are low.8 This does not mean that contact tracing has no use when
          infection rates are high. During the Covid-19 pandemic, contact tracing combined
          with isolation was more effective in reducing transmission than mass testing
          or self-isolation alone.9 Testing, tracing and isolation as a whole had significant
          benefit during times of high infection when combined with high levels of physical
          distancing.10


The meaning of a ‘contact’
3.7.      There is no single definition of a contact – or, specifically, a ‘close contact’ – that
          applies across all pandemics, because pathogens differ in how they are transmitted.
          The definition can only be determined once sufficient information is available
          regarding the particular characteristics of a pathogen and its mode of transmission.

3.8.      The definition of a close contact can vary between countries and change over
          time as understanding of the pathogen evolves. For example, during the Covid-19
          pandemic, on 14 May 2021, the Environmental Modelling Group (a sub-group
          of the Scientific Advisory Group for Emergencies (SAGE), which advises the UK
          government in emergencies) discussed changing the definition of a close contact to
          “clearly reflect new scientific evidence on transmission”.11 A 2021 academic review
          of 101 studies of Covid-19 identified differences in the definition across countries.12
          For example, in relation to airline passengers, some governments defined close

           contacts as all passengers on the flight, some as those seated within two rows of the
           infected person, and some as those within three rows of the infected person.13

 3.9.      Although there were some differences in the detail of the definitions adopted
           across the UK, they were broadly consistent in their core elements. Each government
           defined a close contact by reference to proximity to an infected person, generally
           within two metres for about 15 minutes, with some governments including additional
           criteria.14 For example, Public Health England specified that direct or face-to-
           face contact for any length of time fell under the definition of close contact.15
           The principal differences related to travel settings, where the criteria varied
           between governments.16

 3.10.     Professor Matthew Keeling (Professor of Maths and Life Sciences and Director of the
           Zeeman Institute for Systems Biology and Infectious Disease Epidemiology Research
           at the University of Warwick) suggested that, in future pandemics, obtaining more
           detailed information voluntarily from infected people about their contacts could
           help to shape the most appropriate definition of a close contact.17 That definition
           could then be incorporated into, and updated through, a digital contact tracing tool
           (see below).


 Backward and forward contact tracing
 3.11.     ‘Forward contact tracing’ looks forward by identifying close contacts of an infected
           person from the point at which the infected person is likely to have been infectious
           and so is likely to have infected their close contacts.18 ‘Backward contact tracing’
           looks back at the activities and interactions of an infected person around the time
           they were likely to have been infected, in order to determine the original source of
           their infection.19

 3.12.     Backward contact tracing can be particularly effective for highly infectious diseases
           such as Covid-19, where a relatively small number of infected people can result in a
           large number of secondary cases.20 For example, if a person became infected with
           Covid-19 at a social gathering, forward contact tracing would identify people with

         whom they were in contact after becoming infected. By contrast, backward contact
         tracing would seek to trace others who attended the same event and may have
         been exposed at the same time, enabling them to be advised to test or self-isolate.
         It focuses on where people were infected (linking new cases to events or places),
         allowing links between individuals to be recognised and clusters of infections to be
         identified.21 It can help identify ‘superspreader’ events, where one person infects a
         disproportionately high number of people.

3.13.    Backward contact tracing also plays an important role in identifying people who
         transmit a pathogen without knowing they are infected.22 If an asymptomatic person
         (someone not showing recognised symptoms) infects another person who later
         becomes unwell, backward contact tracing may help to identify the asymptomatic
         person as the original source.

3.14.    Used together, backward and forward contact tracing have the potential to be
         significantly more effective than forward contact tracing alone.23 Figure 4 illustrates
         the number of contacts reached through forward contact tracing alone (left) and
         through a combination of backward and forward contact tracing (right). As noted
         in Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system,
         countries such as Singapore implemented effective responses to Covid-19.
         Singapore used backward as well as forward contact tracing.24 An interim report
         from the UK government’s Department of Health and Social Care dated 11 December
         2020 observed that “(backward tracing) is increasingly used by countries with the
         most successful test and trace strategies”.25

 3.15.                        From as early as February 2020, SAGE repeatedly advised the UK government
                              that backward contact tracing was an important part of an effective contact tracing
                              system, particularly in identifying clusters of linked infections and limiting further
                              spread.26 However, its use was inconsistent and there was no coordinated approach
                              across the four nations of the UK.

 3.16.                        The Inquiry heard that, for at least the first eight months of 2020, the use of
                              backward contact tracing was limited at best. Professor Dame Jenny Harries (Deputy
                              Chief Medical Officer for England from July 2019 to March 2021 and Chief Executive
                              of the UK Health Security Agency from April 2021 to May 2025) told the Inquiry
                              that, in England, local testing and tracing teams sometimes used backward contact
                              tracing in response to particular outbreaks in the early months of 2020.27 Ben Dyson
                              (Director of Policy for NHS Test and Trace at the Department of Health and Social
                              Care from May 2020 to June 2021) told the Inquiry that it was used to some extent,
                              particularly from the summer of 2020.28 However, it was unclear when, if at all, it
                              was implemented more widely. Taken together, the evidence suggests that the use
                              of backward contact tracing was partial and delayed, rather than coordinated and
                              comprehensive. Professor Sir Patrick Vallance, Lord Vallance of Balham (Government
                              Chief Scientific Adviser from April 2018 to March 2023), told the Inquiry that “it was
                              disappointing” that backward contact tracing “took quite so long to get started”.29

         Trace from May 2020 to May 2021), agreed that its implementation was “undoubtedly

3.17.    In Wales, the benefits of backward contact tracing were recognised, but a
         comprehensive approach was considered impracticable due to resource constraints.
         Instead, it was used selectively in areas of higher transmission and in particular
         situations.31

3.18.    In Northern Ireland, backward contact tracing was implemented from 16 November
         2020.32 However, it is unclear whether this was widespread or targeted.

3.19.    In Scotland, on 4 June 2020, the Scottish Government Covid-19 Advisory Group
         discussed advice from SAGE about the importance of backward contact tracing.33
         It was implemented by the autumn of 2020, although the precise timing and extent
         (whether it was widespread or targeted) are also unclear.34

3.20. The absence of widespread backward contact tracing likely weakened the UK’s
      pandemic response, given the asymptomatic transmission of Covid-19. As noted in
      the Inquiry’s combined Report relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C, there were
      signs by late January 2020 that suggested asymptomatic transmission was taking
      place.35 By the end of April 2020, a Public Health England study of care home
      residents established that 43.8% of people who tested positive for Covid-19 were
      asymptomatic.36

3.21.    The absence of widespread backward contact tracing in England might also have
         affected public trust. For example, one member of Covid-19 Bereaved Families for
         Justice UK questioned the value of a system that was unable to link infections to
         shared settings or events:

              “We were told that Test and Trace were unable to trace positive cases and link
                them to one singular event. This made us call into question … what use did it
                serve if there was an outbreak at a venue?”37

3.22. Backward contact tracing has clear advantages in controlling the spread of
      infection and should be included in planning for a future test, trace and isolate
      system. The Inquiry recognises that the availability of resources may have limited

           the early implementation of backward contact tracing during the Covid-19 pandemic.

 Manual contact tracing during the Covid-19 pandemic
 The development of manual contact tracing during the pandemic
 3.23. In January and February 2020, contact tracing took place on a relatively small scale
       in the UK:

           • In England, following guidance and protocols for managing infectious diseases,
              manual contact tracing was implemented using existing public health structures
              and systems.38 At this stage, Public Health England led contact tracing but liaised
              with local directors of public health who engaged with local communities.39
           • In Wales, Public Health Wales set up a Contact Tracing Cell on 27 February 2020,
              which reported directly to Public Health Wales.40
           • In Scotland, existing health protection teams undertook contact tracing in the
              early months of 2020.41
           • In Northern Ireland, in the early months of 2020, the Public Health Agency
              (Northern Ireland) conducted contact tracing for the relatively small numbers
              of cases it had at that time.42

 3.24. In February 2020, community transmission increased following the widespread influx
       of Covid-19 from multiple countries.43 The UK needed:

           • sufficient testing capacity to identify infected people; and
           • to scale up its contact tracing systems and infrastructure to cope with the large
              spike in demand.44

Pausing community contact tracing

3.25. When the UK moved from the ‘contain’ to the ‘delay’ stage of the pandemic response
      in mid-March 2020 (discussed in Chapter 2: Testing), both community testing and
      widespread contact tracing were paused. This was agreed at a COBR meeting on
      12 March 2020, where Professor (later Sir) Christopher Whitty, Chief Medical Officer
      for England from October 2019, noted that this was consistent with the Department
      of Health and Social Care’s Coronavirus: Action Plan.45

3.26. The Coronavirus: Action Plan (which set out the UK-wide approach to responding
      to the pandemic) stated that, when transmission became established within the UK
      population, there would be less emphasis on large-scale preventative measures
      such as intensive contact tracing.46 However, it did not expressly say that widespread
      contact tracing should be stopped altogether. On the contrary, it emphasised the
      importance of identifying infected people to delay the spread of the disease:

              “Many of the actions involved in the Contain phase also act to help Delay the
               onset of an epidemic if it becomes inevitable. These include case finding and
               isolation of early cases.”47

3.27.    The pausing of community testing and widespread contact tracing removed two
         important ways of identifying infected people across the UK. In the absence of
         widespread testing and contact tracing, self-isolation depended on members of the
         public developing recognised symptoms of Covid-19, identifying those symptoms
         and choosing to isolate.48

3.28. Some targeted contact tracing continued. Professor Harries explained to the Inquiry
      that it continued where “it was possible to keep going”.49 Duncan Selbie (Chief
      Executive of Public Health England from July 2012 to August 2020) stated that
      “at no point did PHE [Public Health England] ‘turn off’ its contact tracing capacity”
      but instead it shifted to a targeted rather than widespread approach.50 However, the
      absence of a UK-wide system meant that contact tracing could no longer operate
      at scale.

3.29. The decision to pause widespread contact tracing likely further weakened the ability
      of the UK government and devolved administrations to monitor and respond to the
      spread of infection. There was reduced detection of infected people, a limited ability
      to interrupt transmission by identifying contacts and diminished understanding of

           the extent of the spread. In April 2020, a report on contact tracing by the European
           Centre for Disease Prevention and Control described the importance of contact
           tracing and noted that it was a key part of pandemic response in several Asian
           countries that had successfully reduced case numbers.51 It also commented that data
           from contact tracing could contribute to a better understanding of the epidemiology
           of Covid-19.52 While contact tracing was paused, scientific advisers and decision-
           makers could not collect those data.

 3.30. Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health and Social Care from July 2018 to June
       2021) told the Inquiry that Public Health England “of course, infamously turned off
       the contact tracing system, and that was wrong and a mistake”.53 Although not all
       contact tracing was stopped, the Inquiry considers that the UK government and
       devolved administrations should not have been in a position where they had to
       pause widespread contact tracing. Contact tracing has great value in controlling the
       spread of infection and should have been widely available.54 This decision was taken
       at a time when deaths from Covid-19 were rising rapidly. On 12 March 2020, the UK
       government’s online ‘daily death count’ recorded that 10 people in England had died.
       By 1 April 2020 this had risen to 2,698.55 At such a time, the UK should have been
       making full use of all available tools to identify infected people and limit transmission.
       Had there been better planning, the decision may not have been necessary.

 3.31.     The Inquiry heard competing evidence about the reason for pausing contact
           tracing. Public Health England claimed in May 2020 that, once there was evidence
           of widespread, sustained community transmission, contact tracing “was unlikely to
           control the outbreak alone”.56 This explanation is not persuasive, particularly given
           the evidence of its continued value, even when levels of transmission are higher.57
           It also does not align with the subsequent reintroduction of contact tracing in May
           and June 2020 while case numbers remained high.

=== PAGE 95 ===
                                                                                                 Chapter 3: Contact tracing




3.32. The decision to pause community testing and widespread contact tracing was
      undoubtedly driven by the lack of testing and tracing capacity (see Chapter 2:
      Testing).58 Professor Whitty told the Inquiry that the lack of testing at scale and the
      relatively limited scaling capacity of the contact tracing system were two heavy
      constraints on contact tracing in the first few months of 2020.59 He explained that
      there was not sufficient capacity “both to use testing for clinical management [of sick
      patients] and for contact tracing” and that contact tracing “was the lower priority
      given this forced choice”.60 An article in the British Medical Journal in May 2020 also
      supported this; Professor Harries was quoted as saying that the decision to pause
      widespread contact tracing was partly driven by lack of testing capacity.61 Richard
      Pengelly (Permanent Secretary to the Department of Health (Northern Ireland) from
      July 2014 to April 2022) confirmed that the decision to pause contact tracing was
      influenced by the lack of capacity to test at scale and the inability of the contact
      tracing workforce, resources and systems to handle the increase in demand.62

3.33. Even if sufficient capacity for community testing had been available, the UK’s
      contact tracing systems lacked the capacity to undertake widespread contact
      tracing.63 The Technical Report on the Covid-19 Pandemic in the UK (produced in
      2022 by the Chief Medical Officers and Chief Scientific Advisers for the four nations)
      concluded that “the existing contact tracing workforce, resources and systems were
      not able to handle such a large spike in demand”.64

3.34. Poor pre-pandemic planning by the Department of Health and Social Care,
      the Welsh Government, the Scottish Government and the Department of Health
      (Northern Ireland) (see Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and
      isolate system) meant that, at the start of the pandemic, across the UK, there were
      no rapidly scalable contact tracing systems. As a result, contact tracing was paused
      in March 2020 and the UK’s pandemic response was weakened at a time when it
      should have been as strong as possible.

The resumption of community contact tracing (April, May and June 2020)

3.35. As mandatory lockdown measures were eased in the spring of 2020, all four
      governments of the UK concluded that community contact tracing should again form
      part of the strategy for the next phase of the pandemic.65 A presentation from Public

=== PAGE 96 ===
 Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




         Health England in mid-April 2020 identified several factors supporting the need for
         widespread contact tracing at that time:

         • Behavioural surveys and modelling data suggested that social distancing
            measures had been effective in reducing the spread of infection.
         • There was very limited information on the proportion of the population that had
            been exposed to the virus, but it was believed that a large proportion remained
            susceptible.
         • There was limited evidence on the duration of immunity following infection and
            the risk of re-infection.
         • There was a high likelihood that transmission would increase again once social
            distancing measures were lifted, with a consequent risk of increased mortality
            and increased pressure on health and social care services.
         • When the social distancing measures were lifted, there would be a need for
            robust surveillance to identify people with Covid-19 promptly, alongside sufficient
            capacity to trace and isolate those who had been in contact with them.
         • Finally, the World Health Organization had recently recommended tracing
            and monitoring of close contacts. It recommended a standardised and simplified
            process for the rapid identification and monitoring of close contacts as a matter
            of priority. It considered this an absolute necessity for reducing the intensity
            of transmission.66

 3.36. Widespread contact tracing was introduced in Northern Ireland on 18 May 2020,
       in England and Scotland on 28 May 2020 and in Wales on 1 June 2020.67 On 1 May
       2020, SAGE advised that for a test and trace system to be effective, it would be
       necessary to reach at least 80% of close contacts of a person who had tested
       positive for Covid-19.68 The next phase of the pandemic therefore required contact
       tracing at an “unprecedented level and a new delivery model”.69

 3.37.   The introduction of widespread contact tracing across all four nations was an
         important step in the pandemic response. However, as set out above, this action
         came after months of increased transmission. Contact tracing systems should have
         been capable of widespread delivery as soon as transmission rates began to rise,
         so that increased transmission could be identified and contained at the earliest
         opportunity.

                                                                                             Chapter 3: Contact tracing




Widespread manual contact tracing and the use of local authorities
(May 2020)

3.38. Widespread contact tracing not only started at different times across the four nations
      but there was also no agreed approach on how it should be delivered.

3.39. The Welsh Government and the Northern Ireland Executive adapted existing public
      health structures, whereas the UK government established a new, England-wide
      system.70 The Scottish Government created a large-scale, Scotland-wide contact
      tracing centre through a partnership between Public Health Scotland and NHS
      National Services Scotland.71

3.40. Most significantly, there were differences in how quickly the four governments made
      use of local authority expertise and health protection teams. These teams operate
      within the UK Health Security Agency in England (previously Public Health England),
      Public Health Scotland, Public Health Wales and the Department of Health (Northern
      Ireland).72 They provide specialist public health protection advice and operational
      support to any organisation within their areas.73 In usual circumstances, when there
      is a suspected outbreak of infectious disease, health protection teams lead the
      investigation and management of the outbreak; this may include contact tracing.74

3.41.     Professor Madelynne Arden, expert witness on adherence to behaviours associated
          with test, trace and isolate, told the Inquiry about the benefits of using local health
          teams for contact tracing: “[L]ocal public health teams have got a great deal of
          knowledge about their local communities, how to communicate with -- through
          trusted leaders, things like that.”75 Joanna Killian, Chief Executive of the Local
          Government Association from March 2024 to July 2026, agreed, noting that local
          teams have longstanding relationships with their community and a history of
          handling infectious disease outbreaks via contact tracing.76

3.42. Scotland’s contact tracing model combined local health boards with a centralised
      service. Local health protection teams led contact tracing at the local level.77
      The centralised contact tracing service was run through a new organisation

     Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




               called the National Contact Centre (initially called the National Contact Tracing
               Centre). The National Contact Centre commenced operations on 22 June 2020.78
               It was commissioned by Public Health Scotland and jointly delivered by NHS National
               Services Scotland and Public Health Scotland.79 By 26 August 2020, 98.9% of
               the close contacts of a person infected with Covid-19 were reached by Scotland’s
               combined local and national contact tracing system.80

     3.43. From the outset of population-wide contact tracing in Wales on 1 June 2020, the
           Welsh Government made use of local partnerships.81 Its contact tracing system
           operated through a decentralised, three-tier model, with local teams based in local
           authorities and regional teams at health board level, and with oversight provided by
           a national Public Health Wales co-ordinating team.82 Vaughan Gething (Minister for
           Health and Social Services in the Welsh Government from May 2016 to May 2021)
           explained that the Welsh Government made a conscious decision not to adopt the
           model adopted in England. Instead, it decided to draw on local authority expertise.83
           Mark Drakeford (First Minister of Wales from December 2018 to March 2024)
           emphasised the importance of ensuring that people who were contacted (and were
           asked about highly personal matters) could confidently engage with someone who
           understood the local context and geography and could speak in Welsh, if this was
           preferred.84 During the first year of operation, tracing teams in Wales reached 99.7%
           of people with Covid-19 and almost 95% of their close contacts who were eligible
           for follow-up.85

     3.44. In Northern Ireland, a contact tracing service was launched in May 2020. It was
           established and delivered by the centralised Public Health Agency (Northern
           Ireland), working closely with the Department of Health (Northern Ireland).86
           Given Northern Ireland’s relatively small population of 1.8 million people, the
           system was able to operate with a comparatively small contact tracing team, with
           about 78 tracers trained by 25 May 2020, working from a central office.87 The team
           adopted a specific strategy that emphasised being personable and convivial in their
           interactions.88 By June 2020, 92% of people who had tested positive for Covid-19
           and those who had been in close contact with them were reached within 24 hours.89

3.45. The UK government chose to rely primarily on a new centralised organisation, NHS
      Test and Trace, which was set up by the Department of Health and Social Care to
      deliver mass testing and contact tracing in England.90 The service began operations
      on 28 May 2020.91 NHS Test and Trace contracted commercial providers to operate
      call centres for contact tracing.92 Serco Ltd (an outsourcing and professional services
      company that operates across a range of government sectors including justice,
      defence, customer services and health) entered into a contract on 18 May 2020 to
      provide up to 10,500 call centre agents, along with the necessary infrastructure
      to support manual contact tracing (including buildings, telephones and other
      resources).93 At the same time, Sitel Ltd (a company providing call centres, as well
      as services including customer service and telemarketing) entered into a contract
      to provide 4,500 full-time equivalent call centre agents.94 There were relatively few
      partnerships with local organisations to deliver contact tracing.95 The centralised
      system in England had limited effect. One analysis found that, in June 2020, 42%
      of close contacts of people with Covid-19 were reached, rising to 47% in July 2020
      and falling to 44% in August 2020.96

3.46. England has a much larger population than the devolved nations and therefore
      what is suitable for Northern Ireland, Scotland or Wales may not be suitable for
      England. The centralised system worked in Northern Ireland because its population
      is relatively small and it has a single, comparatively small healthcare system that
      enabled flexibility and a more rapid response.97 The scale of its system meant that it
      was able to replicate some of the advantages typically associated with local teams.

3.47.     Given the size of the population in England, the Inquiry understands the initial choice
          of a centralised system. However, this system saw a significantly lower success rate
          in the early months and had to undergo modification.98 Following feedback from
          local authorities and the public that the centralised model may have hindered the

           timely identification and management of local clusters and outbreaks, NHS Test and
           Trace expanded its use of local teams and partnerships from 10 August 2020.99

 3.48. Greg Fell (President of the Association of Directors of Public Health from November
       2023) told the Inquiry that the initial, single national approach in England “wasn’t
       optimal in terms of the speed by which cases were completed”.100 He said that the
       introduction of local services allowed tracing to be completed more swiftly and in
       more detail.101 Between 10 and 16 September 2020, 74.7% of the identified contacts
       of an infected person were successfully traced by England’s contact tracing system,
       using local authority partnerships.102 This represented a significant improvement
       when compared with the figures for June, July and August 2020.103

 3.49. Baroness Harding told the Inquiry that, “with the benefit of hindsight, you’d do it [use
       local expertise] earlier”, but added that the UK government exerted considerable
       effort from May 2020 onwards to “get this balance right of local and national working
       together”.104 The Inquiry agrees that the expertise of local authorities should have
       been engaged at an earlier stage in England. In a pandemic, the rapid identification
       of potentially infected people is critical and the tracing expertise of local authorities
       is a valuable resource.

 3.50. The Inquiry was told of different reasons for the delay in using that expertise.
       James Bethell, Lord Bethell (Parliamentary Under Secretary of State for Technology,
       Innovation and Life Sciences at the Department of Health and Social Care from
       March 2020 to September 2021), told the Inquiry that local partnerships were not
       utilised sooner due to resource constraints faced by local teams. He provided an
       illustrative example of a local authority lacking the necessary funds to procure a
       gazebo for conducting tracing operations.105 Similarly, Boris Johnson, Prime Minister
       from July 2019 to September 2022, told the Inquiry that the existing capacity of
       local authorities could not have coped with contact tracing demands during the
       pandemic.106

 3.51.     Mr Hancock said that the delay in widespread use of local resources arose from the
           difficulty in integrating NHS Test and Trace with existing local systems.107 He also told
           the Inquiry: “[T]here was one leader of public health per council and they were busy,

         because they were doing everything else as well.”108 However, Mr Fell suggested
         that the UK government lacked understanding of the knowledge possessed by
         local public health teams during the initial stages of the pandemic.109 He explained
         that directors of public health operate within teams – his own consisting of 30 or
         40 people – and during the pandemic they were fully deployed on managing the
         response: “So it’s not true to say DsPH [directors of public health] were one-man
         bands or one-woman bands. We have a team.”110

3.52. Had there been a pre-pandemic plan for contact tracing (see Chapter 1: The UK’s
      readiness to implement a test, trace and isolate system), appropriate allocation
      of resources, including local public health expertise, could have formed part of a
      pre-planned strategy for widespread contact tracing.

3.53. Despite the clear value of local partnerships, a system based solely on local contact
      tracing also presents disadvantages, depending on the size and nature of the
      population. For example, the German government relied entirely on a decentralised
      system, where contact tracing was delivered through its local authorities. A report
      in October 2020 indicated that these local authorities had reported becoming
      overwhelmed and sought to discontinue the work.111 The German government
      consequently suspended contact tracing when confronted with escalating rates
      of infection in the autumn of 2020 and the spring of 2021.112

3.54. The optimal model of contact tracing for a pandemic is therefore one that combines
      national capability with local expertise. As Baroness Harding noted, the scale of a
      national call centre offers surge capacity and consistency while local authorities
      contribute relevant knowledge and expertise.113 Mr Hancock agreed, adding that
      effective integration relies on data structures to allow national and local systems to
      work together.114 Future contact tracing strategies should therefore be based on a
      combined model, unless – as exemplified in Northern Ireland during the Covid-19
      pandemic – the size and structure of the nation mean that a centralised system
      alone can deliver comparable benefits.

The manual contact tracing workforce

3.55. Manual contact tracing was undertaken by people employed as contact tracers,
      operating either in call centres or from their homes.115 By 27 May 2020, 25,000
      contact tracers had been recruited in England, establishing a capacity to trace
      the contacts of up to 10,000 people per day.116 In Scotland, approximately 2,000

               contact tracers were recruited by 23 June 2020.117 In Wales, the contact tracing
               workforce amounted to 700 people by the same date.118 In Northern Ireland, there
               were 78 tracers by 25 May 2020.119 This disparity may be attributable to variations
               in population size.120

     3.56. Concerns arose that too many contact tracers had been recruited in England. In
           July 2020, Theodore Agnew, Lord Agnew of Oulton (Minister of State at the Cabinet
           Office and the Treasury from February 2020 to January 2022), questioned low
           utilisation rates of contact tracers.121 He emphasised the importance of efficient
           expenditure, rather than simply paying for people to be available.122 In August 2020,
           the British Medical Journal reported that NHS Test and Trace contact tracers in
           England were left with no work to do.123 Media outlets also reported that contact
           tracers were left idle, with headlines such as ‘Coronavirus contact tracer paid to
           watch Netflix’.124 The UK government requested a reduction in the number of call
           centre agents supplied by Serco Ltd.125 By 11 August 2020, NHS Test and Trace had
           agreed with both Sitel Ltd and Serco Ltd that the total number of contact tracers
           would be reduced to 12,000.126

     3.57.     Mr Hancock was unable to explain the reasons for the initial decision regarding the
               number of contact tracers required:

                    “I don’t recall. There will have been -- somebody will have made an estimate
                     based on a piece of advice, but you’ve got to remember that this was the first
                     time this has happened in living memory so it inevitably was an estimate.”127

     3.58. By contrast, the Welsh Government carried out modelling in May 2020 to estimate
           the size of the workforce required for contact tracing, based on the number of
           people infected, with options to adjust this in line with the number of available
           tests.128 It also enabled the Welsh Government to calculate anticipated fluctuations
           in workforce requirements on a weekly basis between 1 June and 31 August 2020.129
           This demonstrated the effective use of modelling and analysis to inform decisions
           about resourcing a manual contact tracing system.

3.59. Detailed modelling exercises should be undertaken in future to estimate the likely
      contact tracing workforce required in the next pandemic, to avoid inefficiency and
      the unnecessary use of limited resources.


Maintaining manual contact tracing infrastructure between
pandemics
3.60. The systems put in place during the pandemic to enable manual contact tracing
      – such as call centres or a contact tracing workforce – have not been retained.
      Mr Hancock told the Inquiry: “I am concerned that at present our current capacity
      is being dismantled and we will find it much harder to scale again in the future as
      a result.”130 While it may not be practicable to maintain buildings and equipment
      on a permanent stand-by basis for a future pandemic, it should be possible to
      retain the capability to scale up at speed. This could include developing framework
      agreements with call centre providers and providers of contact tracing staff so that
      they can be implemented rapidly.

3.61.     This is important for three principal reasons. Firstly, there should be an alternative
          to digital contact tracing (eg through mobile phone apps) during a pandemic for
          those people who are unable or unwilling to use such tools, including people who
          are digitally excluded. Secondly, it would be prudent to maintain an alternative to
          digital contact tracing in the event of unforeseen issues with digital tools (such as
          technical failures or cyber attacks or incidents). Thirdly, the greatest benefits arise
          from combining manual and digital contact tracing during a pandemic. Digital contact
          tracing can operate more quickly than manual methods and can identify contacts
          that an infected person may be unable to recall.131 However, manual contact tracing,
          as a human-fronted process, enables people to receive assurance, guidance and
          explanation at what may be an anxious time.132 Each approach, therefore, provides
          distinct advantages that are not provided by the other.

3.62. The data and expert opinion support the use of a combined manual and digital
      system. A 2021 study of contact tracing during the Covid-19 pandemic found that
      infection control was improved when both manual and digital methods were used
      together, compared to reliance on digital contact tracing alone.133 Professor Martin
      McKee, Professor of European Public Health at the London School of Hygiene &
      Tropical Medicine from 1997 and a member of Independent SAGE (a group of UK

           scientists, independent of the UK government, set up to monitor and assess UK
           strategy in relation to the Covid-19 pandemic), told the Inquiry that, despite the
           use of digital technologies, manual contact tracing remained essential.134 Professor
           Wolfgang Emmerich (former Chief Executive Officer of Zühlke Engineering Ltd,
           which provided services for the NHSX contact tracing app, discussed below)
           similarly explained that manual and digital contact tracing are complementary and
           that both have a role in public health interventions.135 He suggested that there is an
           opportunity to improve integration between the two. For example, he suggested
           enabling contact tracing staff to add details of venues that have been visited to the
           data underpinning venue alerts sent through digital apps.136

 3.63. Planning for a future manual contact tracing system should include:

           • developing a ‘combined’ model comprising a centralised system alongside
               locally delivered services led by local authorities, unless the size and structure
               of the country (such as in the example of Northern Ireland above) mean that a
               centralised system alone can deliver comparable benefits;
           • analysing the number of people likely to use the system, alongside other relevant
               data, to inform workforce recruitment; and
           • developing a framework agreement for contact tracing services and staff to be
               available and ready to deliver services as quickly as possible at the outset of the
               next public health emergency.


           Recommendation 2: Manual contact tracing during a public health
           emergency
           The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
           Ireland Executive should maintain the capability to deliver manual contact tracing
           using local public health expertise.
           Each government should also prepare plans to scale up manual contact tracing
           during a public health emergency. Plans should include:

           • a contact tracing model that incorporates local public health expertise;
           • the recruitment of a trained and appropriately sized workforce; and
           • a framework agreement for contact tracing services so that providers and staff
               can be engaged to deliver those services quickly.

Digital contact tracing during the Covid-19 pandemic
3.64. Digital contact tracing involves the use of a technological tool, such as a mobile
      phone app, to identify people who may have been exposed to Covid-19. These tools
      analyse data on a person’s location and proximity to detect when people have been
      close to someone who is infected. Those who may have been exposed to the virus
      can thereby be identified, informed of the risk and given advice as to how to proceed
      (see Figure 5, which illustrates the contacts reached through digital contact tracing
      compared with manual contact tracing).137

=== PAGE 106 ===
 Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




 The benefits of digital contact tracing
 3.65. The use of digital contact tracing can have a significant impact on reducing the
       spread of infection during a pandemic. With sufficiently high levels of engagement,
       the spread of an infectious disease like Covid-19 can be significantly reduced or even
       stopped.

 3.66. In April 2020, a team at Oxford University (led, among others, by Professor
       Christophe Fraser, Professor of Infectious Disease Epidemiology and consultant
       for the NHS COVID-19 app) modelled the spread of coronavirus in a simulated city
       of 1 million inhabitants.138 The results suggested that, if approximately 60% of the
       population used a contact tracing app, the pandemic could be stopped.139

 3.67.     A 2021 modelling study of contact tracing during the Covid-19 pandemic in three
           counties in the USA estimated that, where 75% of people downloaded and used a
           contact tracing app, total infections were reduced by as much as 81% in one county,
           with a minimum of 56% in another. Even with relatively lower rates of adoption,
           benefits were observed, with uptake of 15% leading to estimated reductions in total
           infections as high as 11.8% in one county, with a low estimate of 3.9% in another.140
           A study in England and Wales found that digital contact tracing enabled more
           contacts to be reached than by manual methods. Between December 2020 and
           January 2021 in England and Wales, the average number of contacts identified per
           infected person was 4.2 through digital tracing, compared with 1.8 through manual
           tracing.141

 3.68. The experience in other countries supports these findings. Singapore, for example,
       was the first country to develop a contact tracing app, in March 2020.142 The app
       was complemented by a wearable dongle introduced in June 2020 and was used
       by more than 90% of the population.143 Singapore is widely regarded as having been
       successful in suppressing Covid-19.144 In August 2020, its system was able to reach
       100% of close contacts of people infected with Covid-19 through contact tracing.145

The development of digital contact tracing
3.69. During the Covid-19 pandemic, three digital tools for contact tracing were launched
      in the UK, all of which were based on mobile phone apps. The Welsh Government
      chose to adopt an app developed by the UK government (the NHS COVID-19 app),
      whereas the Scottish Government and Northern Ireland Executive chose to develop
      their own apps. Although the UK government began development of a contact
      tracing app first, its app was the last to be launched.

3.70. All three apps used Bluetooth technology to record, anonymously, proximity
      between users who had the app installed and activated. When a user tested positive
      for Covid-19, those who had been in close contact with them – meeting the definition
      of ‘close contact’ discussed above – during the period in which they were likely to
      have been infectious would receive a notification advising them to self-isolate.146
      Over time, the three apps became interoperable, meaning they were able to
      exchange diagnosis keys, anonymous identifiers associated with confirmed Covid-19
      cases. This was achieved through a federated server, which acted as a gateway for
      sharing information between the different systems.147

The STOPCovid NI app: launched on 30 July 2020

3.71.    On 21 May 2020, the Northern Ireland Executive announced that it would develop
         its own contact tracing app rather than adopt the one in development in England.148
         Its app would use the same technology and provider as in the Republic of Ireland.149
         This approach was intended to facilitate interoperability of the apps in the two
         adjoining nations, which had an open border.150 The Northern Ireland and Republic
         of Ireland apps were the first in the world to achieve interoperability.151

3.72. Northern Ireland’s app was called ‘STOPCovid NI’ and was the first of all the UK’s
      apps to become operational, launching on 30 July 2020 for those aged 18 or
      over. This was later extended to 11 to 17 year olds on 1 October 2020, making the
      Northern Ireland Executive the first government in the UK to provide a contact
      tracing app to this age group.152 The app was built to work with an application
      programming interface developed by Google and Apple, meaning it would work
      on phones developed by those companies, and it was led by the Department of
      Health (Northern Ireland).153 During its operational life, the STOPCovid NI app was

 The Protect Scotland app: launched on 10 September 2020

 3.73. The Scottish Government had initially planned to adopt the UK government’s NHS
       COVID-19 app.157 The NHS COVID-19 app was intended to be launched in August
       2020, allowing NHS Test and Trace to assess its performance before a larger-scale
       launch.158 However, in the summer of 2020, Jeane Freeman (Cabinet Secretary for
       Health and Sport in the Scottish Government from June 2018 to May 2021) directed
       that alternative options be considered.159 This reflected concerns within the Scottish
       Government about the direction being taken by the UK government in relation to the
       NHS COVID-19 app at that time (see below) and continuing uncertainty as to whether
       the app would operate effectively in Scotland.160

 3.74.   An assessment by the Scottish Government’s Digital Health and Care Division
         raised further concerns. It noted that the range of functions proposed for the
         NHS COVID-19 app (such as a QR code check-in at venues and a test booking
         facility) risked making the app overly complex.161 It identified concerns that the UK
         government was not providing the devolved administrations with the necessary
         assurance on certain matters, such as technical details.162 In addition, it was thought
         that adopting the NHS COVID-19 app would limit the Scottish Government’s ability to
         make rapid changes to functionality in line with its own policy requirements.163

 3.75. On 31 July 2020, Ms Freeman informed the Scottish Parliament that the Scottish
       Government would develop its own contact tracing app.164 She told the Inquiry that
       the Scottish Government decided not to adopt the NHS COVID-19 app because “this
       was not possible as the app for England had been developed in England to integrate
       with England’s (and Wales) contact tracing system”.165

 3.76. The Protect Scotland app was launched on 10 September 2020. It was developed
       using the Google/Apple application programming interface and operated on a

The NHS COVID-19 app: launched on 24 September 2020

3.77.     The NHS COVID-19 app underwent two iterations, with the second launched in
          England and Wales in September 2020.171 The first iteration, NHS COVID-19 app 1,
          began development in early March 2020.172 It was developed by NHSX following
          meetings in early March 2020.173

3.78. The NHS COVID-19 app 1 was based on a centralised system, meaning that data
      were stored on a central server.174 Matthew Gould (Chief Executive Officer of NHSX
      from May 2019 to January 2022, responsible for the development of the NHS
      COVID-19 app 1) told the Inquiry that this approach was chosen because it was
      able to produce data that could provide greater insight into patterns of transmission
      and emerging hotspots. He described the choice between using a centralised or
      decentralised model as a “trade-off” between concerns about privacy and the
      ability to generate insightful data. The development team acknowledged that the
      decentralised model presented fewer privacy concerns but considered that privacy
      concerns in the centralised model could be mitigated through measures such as
      anonymisation of data.175

          an application programming interface that would only operate with apps built on
          a decentralised model, in which data collected by an app are stored on a person’s

 3.80. On 6 May 2020, a technical team was established to develop a second version of the
       NHS app.178 This work was completed by the end of May, resulting in two functioning
       prototypes: the original ‘centralised’ NHS COVID-19 app 1 and a ‘decentralised’ NHS

 3.81.    Initial testing of both versions indicated that the NHS COVID-19 app 1 did not
          integrate effectively with Apple systems.180 On 15 June 2020, Mr Gould briefed
          Baroness Harding and Mr Hancock:

               “Extensive field trials have shown that … [the NHS COVID-19 app 1] is picking up
                only a small proportion of contact events on Apple phones; by contrast, the
                version using the Google/Apple API [application programming interface] is
                picking up almost every contact event.”181

          The issue arose from a technical flaw in the operation of the NHS COVID-19 app 1 on
          Apple systems, which allowed the phone to transmit data but not receive data from
          other phones. Attempts to resolve this issue had not been successful by
          15 June 2020.182

 3.82. On 18 June 2020, Mr Hancock decided to prioritise development of the
       decentralised NHS COVID-19 app 2.183 The NHS COVID-19 app was launched in
       England and Wales on 24 September 2020 and was available to people aged 16
       and over.184 By 13 October 2020, it had been downloaded by 17.5 million people,
       representing 36.4% of the approximately 48 million people aged 16 years and over
       in England and Wales.185 A study published in 2022 estimated that, during its first
       year of operation, the app may have prevented 1 million Covid-19 infections, 44,000
       hospital admissions and 9,600 deaths.186

3.83. The value of enlisting major technological companies in the analysis of data, and
      in the building and distribution of the app, appears to have been recognised at an
      early stage.187 In-depth engagement with appropriate third parties (such as major
      technology providers) should take place at an early stage in development of the next
      app. This should help to reduce the risk of a digital tool progressing to an advanced
      stage of development before compatibility issues are identified.

Avoiding delay in digital contact tracing

3.84. It is vital to avoid delay in launching digital contact tracing for use in a public health
      emergency.

3.85. Although the benefits of digital contact tracing were recognised in the UK as early
      as March 2020, the tools were not available and implemented until 30 July 2020 in
      Northern Ireland and September 2020 in England, Wales and Scotland. The many
      months during which the four nations operated without digital contact tracing – while
      apps were developed and tested – are likely to have had an impact on transmission
      of the infection. This is particularly relevant given that, during the early stages of the
      pandemic, the UK relied solely on manual contact tracing, which was significantly
      reduced for a period in the spring of 2020.

3.86. The Inquiry recognises that those involved in developing these apps worked
      under significant pressure to put in place an effective digital tool within challenging
      timescales. In addition, the Inquiry heard that the UK was one of the first Western
      democracies to begin developing a digital contact tracing app, which demonstrates
      strong insight and innovation.188 However, the time required for design, development
      and testing prior to launch was significant in the context of a rapidly evolving
      pandemic. This demonstrates the importance of developing a digital contact tracing
      tool in advance of the next pandemic, so that it can be deployed at the outset.


Trust in digital contact tracing
3.87.     While a digital contact tracing tool has the potential to play an important role in
          limiting the spread of infection, its effectiveness depends on whether the public
          chooses to use it. In particular, it relies on people self-reporting that they are infected
          and providing details of the people they have been in contact with, so that those
          contacts can be traced.189

3.88. The benefits of contact tracing are primarily for society as a whole, rather than for the
      individual. Motivations to self-report are therefore likely to be influenced by a sense
      of responsibility to others or compliance with social and community expectations

     3.89. Professor Arden told the Inquiry:

                    “Digital applications, no matter how sophisticated their technology, are only as
                     good as the level of engagement that they produce. Relying on digital solutions
                     when trust has not been established is extremely risky and should be considered
                     and mitigated against in a future pandemic response.”191

               Without this engagement, contact tracing systems cannot function effectively.

     3.90. However, one study estimated that the number of devices with the NHS COVID-19
           app downloaded and contact tracing enabled peaked at 13.9 million in June 2021,
           which represented less than a third of people aged 16 years and over in England and
           Wales.192 The same study estimated that, of users who recorded a positive result on
           the app, between 40% and 55% consented to share their data for contact tracing.193
           Another study in December 2020 reported that more than a third of people in a
           sample group who were aware of the app either did not intend to download the
           NHS COVID-19 app or had downloaded it but then deleted it.194 Three of the most
           common reasons given for not using the contact tracing app were related to a lack
           of trust in the tool:

               • concerns about privacy, with 36.8% of respondents indicating they did not want to
                   be tracked;
               • concerns about effectiveness, with 31.3% stating they did not think the app would
                   be effective; and
               • a lack of trust in those responsible for developing the app, with 24.9% expressing
                   mistrust of the authorities.195

      Concerns about privacy

      3.94. Privacy protection measures for digital contact tracing during the Covid-19 pandemic
            included:

                • the use of a decentralised framework; and                201


                • provisions within an interoperability agreement between the four governments in
                    October 2020, which enabled the three apps to work together while limiting the
                    sharing of personal data.202

                In accordance with data protection principles, users could delete the apps and
                associated data.203 The apps also collected only necessary data.204

      3.95. However, as set out above, concerns about privacy remained the most significant
            reason for non-engagement with digital contact tracing.205 For example, although
            the press release for a trial of the NHS COVID-19 app 2 in Newham, East London,
            dated 13 August 2020, emphasised that the app “tracks the virus, not people”, one
            of the main reasons for low uptake was a “lack of belief or awareness in the privacy
            protections”.206

      3.96. Addressing low engagement due to concerns about privacy is complex. During
            the Covid-19 pandemic, concerns arose from a range of factors, including fears
            that government authorities might use data to monitor them in a ‘Big Brother’
            fashion, worries that apps could make devices vulnerable to hacking and a broader
            perception that contact tracing apps were an invasion of users’ right to private life.207

         those concerns.

                 some of the major hacks that have been happening lately with all our
                 information. I think it could be quite vulnerable to that. That’s probably
                 why I deleted it, if I remember rightly.”208

                Contributor, Every Story Matters


3.98. The UK government and devolved administrations should seek to understand and
      respond to these underlying concerns. This is likely to require input from behavioural
      science experts, analysis of available data from the Covid-19 pandemic and direct
      engagement with the public, including through focus groups.

3.99. Planning for the future use of digital contact tracing should include ensuring that
      privacy protections are clearly embedded into the design of digital contact tracing
      systems and, importantly, that these protections are communicated openly and
      consistently to the public. For individuals who remain unwilling to use digital
      tools, alternative options, such as manual contact tracing, should be explained.
      The Inquiry’s analysis of communications and data collection during the Covid-19
      pandemic, and recommendations for both in a future pandemic, are set out in
      Chapter 6: Communications and data.

The effectiveness of the app and the ‘pingdemic’

3.100. If people do not understand or believe that contact tracing will work, then they may
       have little incentive to self-report, reducing the effectiveness of the system. Accounts
       given to Every Story Matters indicate that the effectiveness of contact tracing may
       not have been clearly communicated to the public during the pandemic.

                                                                                              Chapter 3: Contact tracing




               “I suppose if there are millions of people using it [the contact tracing app],

3.102. The NHS COVID-19 app notified individuals to self-isolate by issuing a notification
       or a ‘ping’, alerting them that they had been in close contact with an infected
       person. In the summer of 2021, concerns about its effectiveness were exacerbated
       when a large number of people began receiving such notifications. This resulted in
       high levels of self-isolation, a phenomenon that became commonly known as the
       ‘pingdemic’. At its peak in July 2021, 7% of UK adults – approximately 4.5 million
       people – were self-isolating within a seven-day period.217 This had a significant
       impact on labour supply, as large numbers of people were temporarily unable to
       attend work.218


               “[E]verybody else on my team was off, and it was just ridiculous. It was like,
                 ‘I’m turning this off, this is so stupid.’ Like, they didn’t even know if they had
                 Covid or not, but regardless they had to stay off work for two weeks. It was
                 just ridiculous.”219

                Zero-hours contract worker, Every Story Matters


3.103. There was also a direct impact on the economy. A Treasury briefing for the Covid-19
       Operations Committee (a Cabinet sub-committee) reported that, in part due to the
       ‘pingdemic’, gross domestic product (the total market value of goods and services
       produced in a country within a specific period) in the UK fell by 0.2% in July 2021.220
       The initial estimate was for 0.1% growth that month, indicating that the economic
       effects of the ‘pingdemic’ were greater than initially predicted.221

3.104. The impact of the ‘pingdemic’ attracted media attention and might have affected
       public trust in digital contact tracing.222 A 2022 study of the NHS COVID-19 app
       found a consistent decline in usage after late June 2021. It concluded that this

                was possibly due to the ‘pingdemic’ and the unfavourable media attention that it

                notifications”.224 As a result, she said the public regarded the app as a “nuisance

      3.105. Contributors to Every Story Matters told the Inquiry that their use of contact tracing
             apps declined after they, or people they knew, received notifications that they did
             not trust.

                       possible contact, so I sort of distrusted it on my side because some of the
                       times I hadn’t even been out and I was getting pinged and everybody I know

                      ‘This is just a load of [obscenity], so, you know, I’ll carry on with my life.’”227

                      Contributor, Every Story Matters


      3.106. When digital contact tracing is functioning as intended, periods of increased
             infection rates will result in a higher number of alerts. However, for the system
             to be effective, those alerts must lead to people self-isolating.

      3.107. It is fundamentally important that those developing digital tools ensure that they are
             as accurate as possible. The consequences of large volumes of alerts during the
             Covid-19 pandemic should be studied and consideration given as to how to mitigate
             negative consequences. Mitigation measures could include explaining clearly the
             measures taken to ensure the accuracy of the app, circulating press releases during
             periods of increased alerts to explain the reasons for them and providing users with
             information within the app itself.

3.108. At the same time, the UK government and devolved administrations must recognise
       the financial and practical challenges associated with frequent self-isolation. If these
       challenges are not addressed, people may be less likely to adhere to guidance, even
       where they understand the reasons for it. Practical and financial support for self-
       isolation – such as assistance with accessing essential supplies and medication and
       financial support for those facing financial hardship because they are self-isolating
       – will therefore be critical in supporting compliance. Encouraging adherence with
       self-isolation is addressed further in Chapter 4: Isolation.


Accessibility of the digital tool
3.109. Even when people are willing to use digital contact tracing tools, they will have
       limited impact if sections of the population are unable to access them. It is therefore
       essential to ensure that digital contact tracing is accessible to as many people as
       possible. As noted in the Inquiry’s combined Module 2ABC Report, those most
       likely to require assistance in accessing information during the Covid-19 pandemic
       – such as disabled people or older people – were also among those at greatest
       risk of infection.228 This creates an added imperative to ensure that digital tools are
       designed in a way that does not exclude those who may benefit from them the most.

3.110. The public sector equality duty requires public authorities to have regard to equality
       considerations when exercising their functions.229 Prior to the launch of the NHS
       COVID-19 app, an equality assessment was carried out.230 Simon Thompson,
       Managing Director of the NHS COVID-19 app from June to October 2020, told the
       Inquiry that this assessment was intended to ensure that the app could be accessed
       by as many people as possible.231 The assessment considered the impact of the
       app on people with a range of protected characteristics (including disability, race
       and ethnicity, age and sexual orientation) and set out measures to address potential
       barriers. For example, it recognised that older people might have lower levels of
       digital literacy and identified NHS 111 and manual contact tracing as alternative
       means of engagement.232 In relation to accessibility for disabled people, it noted that
       the app would meet the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines Level AA standard
       and that manual contact tracing would remain available.233

3.111.    The Web Content Accessibility Guidelines set international standards of accessibility
          for digital content, with three tiers. As noted above, the NHS COVID-19 app 2 met
          the middle Level AA standard, which included requirements such as having sufficient
          colour contrast between text and background and content presented in a logical

               order.234 The Protect Scotland app was also tested to Level AA.235 The Inquiry
               heard that the STOPCovid NI app was also designed to align with accessibility
               guidelines, although the standard it achieved is unclear.236 Level AAA represents the
               highest standard and includes features such as sign language translation and audio
               descriptions.237 When asked why the NHS COVID-19 app only reached Level AA,
               Mr Thompson explained:

                    “We took a judgement call that getting to that standard, which was a really
                      good standard, within the timeline that we had, would mean that we would
                      accommodate the vast majority of needs that would be out there.”238

      3.112. It may not have been possible to achieve the highest level of accessibility within
             the time pressures of the Covid-19 pandemic. However, this experience highlights
             the importance of developing digital tools in advance of a future pandemic. With
             more time available, there should be an expectation that such tools are made as
             accessible as possible. This would help to ensure that everyone, including those
             with disabilities and other accessibility needs, is able to engage fully with public
             health interventions and receive the support they require.

      Access for disabled people

      3.113. Disability accessibility testing for the NHS COVID-19 app was sub-contracted to the
             research agency Open Inclusion, which worked with charities to recruit focus groups
             of people with a range of disabilities to test early versions of the app.239 Videos
             with audio descriptions were also shared on social media to explain how the app
             worked for those with visual impairments and for users of British Sign Language.240
             In Scotland, supporting documentation for various services was made available in
             formats such as Braille and British Sign Language, although it is unclear whether
             this extended specifically to the Protect Scotland app.241 In Northern Ireland, digital
             products were subject to accessibility testing and online resources included features
             such as audio descriptions to assist with accessibility.242

      3.114. These measures represented positive steps to improve accessibility for disabled
             people. NHSX’s use of test groups comprising people with different disabilities
             was a particular example of good practice. This approach should be adopted in the
             development of future digital tools for contact tracing. Engagement with people with
             a range of disabilities at the conceptual and design stages should be considered and
             implemented, wherever practicable.

Accessibility for those unable to use digital contact tracing

3.115. Some people were unable to use contact tracing apps due to ‘digital exclusion’
       (ie they lacked sufficient financial resources to own a phone capable of supporting
       the app or lacked the ability to use the internet in the ways the app required).243

3.116. Digital exclusion affected different groups in different ways. A 2022 study found
       that some people were affected because they lived in rural areas with limited
       or unreliable internet access.244 Others, particularly older people, preferred to
       communicate by telephone rather than online.245 A letter to Mr Hancock from Sadiq
       Khan (later Sir Sadiq Khan), Mayor of London from May 2016, in May 2020 said that
       one in eight London residents did not own a smartphone and many did not keep
       their devices switched on at all times, for example due to limits on data use.246

3.117. Digital exclusion had a direct impact on engagement with contact tracing apps
       during the Covid-19 pandemic. Common reasons for not downloading NHS
       COVID-19 app 1 during a trial on the Isle of Wight in May 2020 included not having
       the appropriate equipment, insufficient storage space on mobile devices and a
       perception that the app would provide limited benefit because individuals did not
       regularly use their mobile phones.247 Contributors to Every Story Matters provided
       similar accounts.


               “[T]he main reason I didn’t have the app is because I had a very old phone …
                 and I wasn’t going to buy a new phone just to get the app.”248

                 Contributor, Every Story Matters



               “[The] test and trace system was not applicable to me as I do not operate a
                 mobile phone.”249

                 Contributor, Every Story Matters

artscouncil.org.uk/sites/default/files/download-file/Good_Things_Foundation_Arts_Council_Report.pdf; INQ000653942)
