=== PAGE 122 ===
      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      3.118. The 2022 study (see above) found that financial poverty was another barrier to
             engagement with digital contact tracing, affecting people across all age groups.250
             Participants in the study indicated that the cost of maintaining sufficient mobile data
             was prohibitive for those experiencing severe social and economic deprivation.251
             Many people relied on pay-as-you-go contracts and could experience periods
             without connectivity, when they could not afford to pay for mobile phone top-ups.252
             Some people shared mobile phones with others.253 One participant said:

                     “I think in terms of the various tracing and symptom apps that are around,
                      my impression is that they are not really on the radar of a lot of folk in lower
                      socioeconomic groups. They are more of a phenomenon amongst a mobilised
                      middle-class.”254

      3.119. Digital contact tracing, by its nature, depends on access to and familiarity with
             smartphone technology. As Mr Gould told the Inquiry: “[I]t’s a sort of unavoidable
             feature of digital contact tracing that if you’re digitally excluded you’re not part
             of it.”255 However, this limitation raises important considerations for equity and
             inclusion. Access to the benefits of contact tracing should not be determined by
             an individual’s access to technology or their financial means. It is important that
             the design of contact tracing systems does not exclude sections of the population
             from participation or benefit.

      3.120. This issue is particularly significant given the evidence that people who were digitally
             excluded or experiencing financial poverty were more likely to become infected with
             Covid-19.256 It is concerning that these groups experienced reduced ability to engage
             with an important preventative tool and underscores the importance of ensuring that
             alternative systems are available and accessible.

      3.121. Manual contact tracing provides a critical complement or alternative to digital tools.
             It offers a means of reaching individuals who cannot, or do not, use smartphone
             apps. It is therefore essential that manual contact tracing is maintained but also
             clearly communicated to the public as a viable and effective alternative.

=== PAGE 123 ===
                                                                                          Chapter 3: Contact tracing




Use of digital contact tracing by minority community groups
3.122. During the Covid-19 pandemic, minority communities faced particular barriers to
       engaging with digital contact tracing, including reduced levels of trust arising from
       historical misuse of data, as well as language barriers that affected accessibility.
       Some people also faced challenges due to lower literacy levels.

3.123. Data from trials of the NHS COVID-19 app indicate that individuals from ethnic
       minority groups were less likely to use digital contact tracing. During the Isle of Wight
       trial of the NHS COVID-19 app 1, 87% of participants with a White ethnic background
       downloaded the app, compared to 77% of non-White participants.257 Concerns about
       privacy were identified as a primary reason for non-engagement.258 Similarly, two of
       the key barriers to engagement in the trial of the NHS COVID-19 app 2 in Newham
       (one of the most diverse boroughs in the UK) were lack of awareness and lack of
       trust in government.259

3.124. Professor Fraser told the Inquiry that trust must be earned through trustworthiness,
       noting that some communities had experienced inappropriate use of their data in the
       past and have legitimate reasons for distrust.260 Ade Adeyemi (Joint Secretary of the
       Federation of Ethnic Minority Healthcare Organisations) also explained to the Inquiry
       that many ethnic minority groups believed there had been instances of data misuse
       by the government.261 He said:

              “Mistrust about breaches of privacy and data misuse runs deep within Black,
               Asian and Minority Ethnic community [sic]. It was not uncommon to hear rumours
               and conspiracy theories about the government’s real motivation for collection of
               data and contact tracing.”262

3.125. Mr Hancock acknowledged the importance of recognising diversity within
       communities to ensure that people from all communities acted upon public
       health advice.263 However, Mr Adeyemi said that not enough was done to combat
       conspiracy theories and that the introduction of contact tracing created a lot of
       uncertainty among some ethnic minority communities.264 His view was that the
       opportunity to rebuild trust within ethnic minority communities was missed by
       policy-makers.265 Professor Arden told the Inquiry that “more could have been

3.129. Although some positive steps were taken to reach minority groups, they were
       not always successful. For example, the NHS COVID-19 app was available in a
       number of languages, including Bengali.275 Yet a contributor from the Bangladeshi
       community told Every Story Matters that there were low levels of engagement in
       their community due to language barriers and low levels of digital literacy. Instead,
       the community tracked infections in their local area by alerting community members
       to infections in their neighbourhood.276 This suggests the community was unaware
       that the NHS COVID-19 app was available in Bengali.

3.130. Professor Sir Michael McBride (Chief Medical Officer for Northern Ireland from
       September 2006) told the Inquiry that he understood the Department of Health
       (Northern Ireland)’s digital products were subjected to accessibility testing and that
       online resources enabled Google Translate.277 However, the Inquiry has seen no
       evidence that the STOPCovid NI app was ever made available in different languages.

3.131. The Protect Scotland app was initially released in English.278 An equality impact
       assessment dated 14 September 2020 (convened by the Scottish Government
       and conducted by representatives of vulnerable groups, the human rights sector,
       NHS National Services Scotland and the Digital Health and Care Division in the
       Scottish Government) attributed the lack of additional languages to the timescales
       for release of the app.279 At the point of release, the Scottish Government indicated
       in its Interim National Equality Impact Assessment that it would consider adding
       additional languages for later versions.280 However, by 12 March 2021, the Scottish
       Government’s Data Protection Impact Assessment indicated that it was still only at
       the stage of “considering the future development of versions of the app, to address
       accessibility, e.g. in terms of languages other than English”.281 Explanatory videos
       about the app were available in multiple languages from 15 September 2020.282
       Yet the Inquiry has seen no evidence that the Protect Scotland app itself was ever
       expanded to include languages other than English.

3.132. If engagement does not take into account the needs of different communities
       (such as communicating in a language with which people feel most comfortable),
       individuals are less likely to feel able to use digital tools. This will directly impact

           the effectiveness of those digital tools. In a future pandemic, there should be
           greater focus on encouraging digital contact tracing within different communities.
           Measures may include:

           • involving people from a diverse range of backgrounds in the design of digital

  Developing a new digital tool for contact tracing
  3.133. None of the digital contact tracing tools developed during the Covid-19 pandemic
         remain in operation. The NHS COVID-19 app was closed down on 27 April
         2023.286 The Protect Scotland app was decommissioned on 29 April 2022 and the
         STOPCovid NI app in June 2022.287 Professor Timothy Spector (Professor of Genetic
         Epidemiology at King’s College London and co-founder of ZOE Ltd, which develops
         nutrition apps and products) told the Inquiry that it is “frightening” that there is no
         digital tool “ready to go for the next pandemic”.288 The Inquiry agrees. Drawing on
         the evidence set out in this chapter, a key lesson is that digital contact tracing should
         form part of pandemic preparedness. Without advance development, time is likely
         to be lost in designing, testing and implementing such tools during a public health
         emergency, thereby delaying their potential contribution to controlling transmission.


  A single digital tool for all four nations
  3.134. The UK government committed in March 2026 to developing digital capability
         for a surge response service to support contact tracing and other features (such
         as ordering tests) in England.289 However, there would be even greater benefit in
         developing a single digital contact tracing tool for use in all four nations. The use
         of different apps across the four nations introduced the potential for confusion.
         For example, Ms Freeman noted that changes to the app in England in the summer
         of 2021 (to reduce the volume of self-isolation notifications by changing the criteria
         for how recently a person had to be in close contact with an infected person before

3.135. The use of different apps could also complicate infection control in relation to

       adopt the NHS COVID-19 app, which would have simplified cross-border contact
       tracing and avoided duplication of effort in developing separate systems.294
       Mr Thompson told the Inquiry that, while interoperability of different apps during the
       Covid-19 pandemic was very important, “even more preferable is not needing the
       interoperability, actually having one app as part of the response I think would have
       been a much better approach”.295

3.137. The Inquiry therefore recommends that a single digital contact tracing tool should
       be developed, taking into consideration the particular needs and infrastructure in
       each of the four nations. While it is positive that the UK government has indicated
       that it will develop a digital tool, it should work with the devolved administrations to
       design one tool that is applicable across all four nations.296 Engagement with those
       responsible for developing digital tools in the Republic of Ireland would help to
       ensure interoperability between systems.

3.138. To ensure that digital contact tracing can be deployed at the outset of a public health
       emergency, a suitable digital tool must be developed in advance. This tool should
       incorporate robust privacy protections and achieve the highest possible standards
       of accessibility. It should also be subject to regular review and updating in order to

      Secondary functions of a digital tool for contact tracing
      3.139. A digital contact tracing tool may include additional features to support the wider
             pandemic response.

      3.140. As well as contact tracing, the NHS COVID-19 app provided locally relevant public
             health information on Covid-19 prevention, the ability to ‘check in’ to venues using
             QR codes and symptom checking linked to test bookings.297 The Protect Scotland
             app did not include functions beyond contact tracing.298 The Scottish Government’s
             press release about the app explained that symptom checking was not included as
             it was available through the NHS COVID-19 app.299 Similarly, the StopCOVID NI app
             focused on contact tracing, with symptom tracking delivered through a separate app
             called COVIDCare NI.300

      3.141. The Inquiry heard that digital symptom tracking can provide valuable support during
             a pandemic. A symptom tracking programme asks users to submit details of their
             symptoms and uses those data to increase understanding of the disease, support
             research and understand its spread.301

      3.142. Professor Spector told the Inquiry about the ZOE symptom tracking app, launched on
             23 March 2020 – many months before any of the contact tracing apps.302 It produced
             regional maps of likely spread of Covid-19 based on user-reported symptoms.303
             As well as being useful for tracking the progression of Covid-19, Professor Spector
             suggested that it could be a useful predictor of whether or not someone had the
             disease.304 He said that its predictive value may have been comparable to much of
             the testing performed in the UK at the time, and that it was much faster.305

      3.143. Professor Spector told the Inquiry that he attempted to provide the ZOE app to
             NHSX to use it across the UK, but his attempts were not well received.306 Mr Gould
             indicated that adoption of the ZOE app, as suggested by Professor Spector, would

         not have negated the need for a separate contact tracing app.307 He said that
         repurposing the ZOE symptom tracking app to become a contact tracing app “would

         Van-Tam (Deputy Chief Medical Officer for England from October 2017 to March

3.144. Incorporating symptom tracking functions into a digital contact tracing tool would
       provide useful data to track the spread of the virus, as well as help users predict
       whether they have a disease, even before widespread testing is available. If the
       symptom tracking function were part of a contact tracing tool, users would only
       need to download and engage with one digital tool rather than having to deal with
       various different ones. This would likely decrease confusion and increase successful
       engagement. One contributor to Every Story Matters told the Inquiry about the
       different apps launched for different reasons during the pandemic.


              “I would just say there seemed to be a lot of apps, all at the one time.
                And unless you knew what you were doing it was easy to get a little bit
                lost and to be using the wrong one as I obviously did a few times.”310

               Contributor who accessed mental health support, Every Story Matters



         Recommendation 3: Develop a digital contact tracing tool
         The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
         Ireland Executive should develop a single digital tool to support forward and
         backward contact tracing across the UK in a public health emergency.
         The tool should include:

         • mechanisms to record contacts;
         • mechanisms to receive automatic contact tracing notifications; and
         • wider test, trace and isolate system features such as a symptom
             tracking feature.
         The tool should be developed in consultation with regional, local, community
         and voluntary bodies to ensure that it is accessible. It should be regularly tested
         and updated.

=== PAGE 130 ===
Chapter 4: Isolation

=== PAGE 131 ===
                                                                                                   Chapter 4: Isolation




Introduction
4.1.     During the Covid-19 pandemic, what became known as ‘self-isolation’ was
         introduced across the UK as a central public health measure to reduce the spread of
         the virus, limit mortality rates and ease pressure on healthcare systems. People who
         showed symptoms, had tested positive for the virus or whose close contacts had
         tested positive were expected to stay away from other people for a specific period of
         time while they were considered infectious. By separating those who were infected
         – or potentially exposed – from the wider population, self-isolation formed a crucial
         part of the wider test, trace and isolate systems adopted across the UK.

4.2.     The importance of self-isolation as a measure to control the spread of infection was
         internationally recognised from the early stages of the pandemic. On 16 March 2020,
         the World Health Organization, while urging adoption of a number of measures,
         noted: “[T]he most effective way to prevent infections and save lives is breaking
         the chains of transmission. And to do that, you must test and isolate.”1

4.3.     However, adherence to self-isolation requirements demanded significant personal
         and economic sacrifice. Many people were unable to work and faced loss of income.
         Others were prevented from caring for relatives or carrying out essential tasks such
         as obtaining medicines. The social and psychological impact on people who were
         forced to self-isolate was considerable for some – for example, for disabled people
         or older people living alone. The Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story Matters,
         heard people’s experience of self-isolation.


              “[I] couldn’t get outside, couldn’t go for a walk; it [self-isolation] affected my
                mental health, my health. I thought things would get better, but it didn’t; it
                took a long time.”2

               Contributor, Every Story Matters


4.4.     Ensuring that people adhered to self-isolation measures posed a significant
         challenge for the UK government and devolved administrations. This chapter
         examines the decision-making processes underpinning self-isolation policies
         and considers the adequacy of financial and practical support provided to people
         to encourage their compliance.




1   ‘WHO Director-General’s opening remarks at the media briefing on COVID-19 – 16 March 2020’, World Health Organization,
16 March 2020 (https://www.who.int/news-room/speeches/item/who-director-general-s-opening-remarks-at-the-media-briefing-on-
covid-19---16-march-2020; INQ000512530)
2   Every Story Matters: Test, Trace and Isolate, p73 (INQ000475132)

=== PAGE 132 ===
  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




  Development of self-isolation policies
  4.5.      The UK’s emergency response strategy for dealing with a pandemic was set out
            in the UK Influenza Pandemic Preparedness Strategy 2011 (the 2011 Strategy).3
            However, as noted in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report, it focused on only one type of
            pandemic and was predicated on the assumption that it would not be possible to
            contain or eradicate a new virus.4 Professor Graham Medley (Professor of Infectious
            Disease Modelling at the London School of Hygiene & Tropical Medicine and Co-
            Chair of the Scientific Pandemic Influenza Group on Modelling, Operational sub-
            group (SPI-M-O) from January 2020 to February 2022) told the Inquiry that test,
            trace and isolate was not “part of the discussion of preparedness plans”.5

  4.6.      In late January 2020, the UK’s Chief Medical Officers, the UK government and the
            devolved administrations had to confront the fact that Covid-19 was a “real potential
            threat to the UK”.6 The UK government focused on cases of Covid-19 entering the UK
            by giving advice to returning travellers from Wuhan (see Chapter 5: Border control
            measures) to self-isolate. On 27 January 2020, Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for
            Health and Social Care from July 2018 to June 2021) published a statement asking
            anyone in the UK who had returned from Wuhan in the last 14 days to self-isolate by
            staying at home and to call NHS 111.7

  4.7.      On 30 January 2020, the World Health Organization declared a public health
            emergency of international concern.8 As understanding of the key features of the
            virus was developing, including the possibility of community transmission outside
            China, the Scientific Advisory Group for Emergencies (SAGE) advised the UK
            government to update its advice and expand the list of countries and territories
            from mainland China to include Thailand, Japan, South Korea, Hong Kong, Taiwan,
            Singapore, Malaysia and Macau.9 On 6 February 2020, travellers from these
            countries and territories who developed symptoms within 14 days of returning
            to the UK were also asked to self-isolate and contact their GP or 111 services.10

                                                                                                       Chapter 4: Isolation




          As Mr Hancock had told COBR at its meeting the previous day, “there were no legal
          powers in place to force nationals into isolation”.11

4.8.      By mid-February 2020, it was known that the virus was spreading across the world
          and had reached Europe and the UK.12 However, the UK government and devolved
          administrations had no detailed plans in place for test, trace and isolate measures
          that could be scaled up in the event of a pandemic.13 SAGE turned its attention to
          non-pharmaceutical interventions to delay transmission within the UK, including
          household isolation, social distancing, shielding for the clinically vulnerable and
          isolation within prisons.14 Although SAGE had supported the principle of self-isolation
          during its first full meeting on 28 January 2020, it highlighted the fact that further
          input was required from behavioural scientists, including SPI-M-O.15

4.9.      By the end of February 2020, modelling work was under way to better understand
          measures to contain and delay transmission – such as self-isolation and whole-
          household isolation – and to develop illustrative scenarios “showing the plausible
          impacts of combinations of interventions in the UK”.16

4.10.     The increasing urgency of the situation meant that there was limited time for SAGE
          and SPI-M-O to evaluate fully their operational feasibility and wider implications
          for different population groups.17 By the start of March 2020, it was clear to the UK
          government and devolved administrations that the crisis was escalating.18

4.11.     Further work was commissioned from SAGE and its sub-groups, together with
          various government bodies including Public Health England, the Department of
          Health and Social Care, and the Cabinet Secretariat, to compare timings and triggers
          for interventions such as widespread self-isolation and to consider how to measure
          the public’s engagement and compliance.19

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      Preparing the public for self-isolation
      4.12.     On 3 March 2020, the Coronavirus: Action Plan was published.20 As noted in
                the Inquiry’s combined Report relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C, it was
                commissioned by Mr Hancock as a public-facing “coronavirus version” of the 2011
                Strategy. Although its development was primarily driven by the UK government,
                the Coronavirus: Action Plan was a four-nations publication and outlined the
                governments’ initial response to the virus through four phases: ‘contain’, ‘delay’,
                ‘research’ and ‘mitigate’.21 (Further details about the phases of the Coronavirus:
                Action Plan are outlined in Chapter 2: Testing.)

      4.13.     The Coronavirus: Action Plan identified self-isolation as the “primary means” to
                contain the spread of the disease.22 It advised that the majority of people would
                manage Covid-19 through “self isolation at home and simple over the counter
                medicines”.23

      4.14.     However, the publication of the Coronavirus: Action Plan took place before the
                UK government and devolved administrations had fully formed internal plans for a
                comprehensive policy for self-isolation and other non-pharmaceutical interventions,
                such as testing and contact tracing.24 As the 2011 Strategy did not set out the steps
                that could be taken to either mitigate or suppress the outbreak of a novel infectious
                disease, detailed planning and coordination of the first self-isolation measures were
                urgently needed.

      4.15.     On 4 March 2020, COBR (the UK government’s national crisis management centre
                for responding to whole-system civil emergencies) met to discuss proposals. The
                meeting was chaired by Mr Hancock and attended by health ministers from the
                devolved administrations.25 Those attending were sent a copy of a SAGE paper
                – ‘Potential impact of behavioural and social interventions on an epidemic of
                Covid-19 in the UK’ – which set out the “illustrative impact of behavioural and social
                interventions” in high-level terms and some general conclusions about impact.26

=== PAGE 135 ===
                                                                                                 Chapter 4: Isolation




         Although SAGE noted that a survey conducted in February 2020 found that 84%
         of people in the UK supported a form of mandatory self-isolation, it highlighted that
         there was still “a lot of uncertainty” about the measures and, in particular, the biggest
         variable was adherence.27 Only brief details were provided of the potential barriers
         to self-isolation, including loss of income and inability to pay for food, heating,
         lighting and access to the internet. The paper also highlighted different forms of
         support, practical and financial, that may be required to encourage compliance with
         the measures.28 In its response to the paper, the Government Office for Science
         acknowledged that more work was needed to understand the social impacts of
         the measures, as existing analyses to date had focused primarily on the economic
         impacts.29

4.16.    The same day, the Civil Contingencies Secretariat (the coordinating body for whole-
         system civil emergency planning, based in the Cabinet Office) commissioned the
         Department of Health and Social Care and other government departments (including
         the Home Office, the Treasury, and the Department for Business, Energy and
         Industrial Strategy) to assess the societal and operational impacts of implementing
         self-isolation measures.30

4.17.    The next day, 5 March 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care’s findings
         included an overview of assumptions provided by SAGE, the Scientific Pandemic
         Influenza Group on Modelling (SPI-M, a sub-group of SAGE that provided
         expert advice to the UK government based on infectious disease modelling and
         epidemiology) and the Scientific Pandemic Insights Group on Behaviours (SPI-B,
         an expert group that provides independent, expert, social and behavioural science
         advice to SAGE).31 It noted that the measures might have a financial impact “on those
         who work in healthcare on zero-hours contracts” as well as those who could not go
         to work due to staying at home for childcare purposes. Ben Dyson (Director of Policy
         for NHS Test and Trace at the Department of Health and Social Care from May 2020
         to June 2021) told the Inquiry that the paper highlighted a range of issues relating
         to the measures, including financial impact, compliance and the “stigma placed on
         certain groups in society”.32 Although financial support for the proposed measures
         was being discussed within SAGE, the Inquiry has seen no evidence that there was a
         plan for a support package to mitigate the impact of the interventions.33

4.18.    By 9 March 2020, SAGE considered that containment of the virus – to avoid it
         becoming an epidemic – was “unlikely to be achievable”.34 As Professor Sir Patrick
         Vallance (later Lord Vallance of Balham), Government Chief Scientific Adviser from
         April 2018 to March 2023, told the Inquiry, Covid-19 was more prevalent in the

  The introduction of widespread self-isolation measures in
  March 2020
  4.19.    On 9 March 2020, COBR met to discuss transitioning from the ‘contain’ phase
           to the ‘delay’ phase of the Coronavirus: Action Plan. The meeting was chaired by
           Boris Johnson (Prime Minister from July 2019 to September 2022) and attended
           by health ministers and Chief Medical Officers from the devolved administrations.36
           It considered planned interventions in three stages, including self-isolation of
           symptomatic people, full household isolation where one person is symptomatic,
           and measures to safeguard older people and clinically vulnerable people.37 Professor
           Vallance explained that the measures had two aims: to reduce the peak of the virus
           to enable the NHS to cope with demand and to reduce the mortality rate.38 SAGE
           recommended that symptomatic people should self-isolate for 7 days and whole
           households should isolate for 14 days where one person is symptomatic.39 Social
           distancing for people over 70 and other at-risk groups was recommended as a third
           measure, to be implemented over the following two to three weeks.40

  4.20. COBR discussions about the implementation of these measures were
        understandably shaped by the aims of reducing loss of life and preventing
        healthcare services from becoming overwhelmed, as case numbers increased and
        modelling estimated severe pressure on hospitals.41 However, the proposed self-
        isolation measures carried considerable social and economic costs for individuals
        and households. These challenges were acknowledged by SAGE, but “further urgent
        work” was still required across the UK government and the devolved administrations
        to “test impacts and develop robust plans for mitigations and reassurance to the
        public”.42

  4.21.    During its meeting the next day, 10 March 2020, SAGE acknowledged again the
           potential challenges that members of the public would likely face when trying to
           comply with self-isolation measures, providing examples of those from poorer
           households and those reliant on relatives for childcare.43 Professor Vallance

          told the Inquiry that such measures “would result in social, health and economic

4.22. SAGE estimated that unconfirmed cases could number between 5,000 and 10,000.45
      Given the rise in case numbers, on 12 March 2020, the UK’s Chief Medical Officers
      raised the UK risk level to ‘high’ in response to increased transmission. That same
      day, the UK government and devolved administrations agreed to move from the
      ‘contain’ phase to ‘delay’.46

4.23. A COBR meeting was convened on the afternoon of 12 March 2020, during which
      SAGE recommended that the UK government and devolved administrations advise
      symptomatic people to stay at home and self-isolate for seven days. It was noted that
      this measure should be implemented “almost immediately”, with the other options to
      be kept under active review. COBR agreed to issue advice to the public on the new
      self-isolation measures later that day.47

4.24. Mr Johnson announced the first widespread self-isolation measures later that day,
      asking people experiencing mild symptoms of Covid-19 to stay at home for “at least
      seven days” to protect others and help slow the spread of the disease. People were
      urged not to call NHS 111 but to “use the internet for information if they can”.48 These
      measures applied to all four nations of the UK, in line with the Coronavirus: Action
      Plan.49 The announcement also notified the public that the UK government would
      likely impose whole-household isolation “at some point in the next few weeks”.50

4.25. When Mr Johnson made this announcement, there were no robust plans for how
      people would be supported through the practical, financial and emotional challenges
      they were likely to face in complying with the new measures. This had been
      recommended by SAGE on 9 March 2020.51

4.26. During its meeting on 13 March 2020, SAGE acknowledged the delay in obtaining an
      accurate picture of the extent of community transmission. Notwithstanding the lag in
      data, SAGE considered that the UK might be “further ahead on the epidemic curve”
      and recommended that further measures – household isolation and social distancing

                of older people and clinically vulnerable people – should be implemented “as soon

      4.27.     On 16 March 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care’s Situation Report
                stated that the total number of cases in the UK had reached 1,544.53 Mr Dyson told
                the Inquiry that the scientific advice suggested that the UK was “at the cusp of a
                fast swing up the infection curve”.54 Following a COBR meeting that same day, it was
                agreed that household isolation, social distancing and shielding measures should
                be implemented to delay and reduce the peak of the virus.55 Modelling suggested
                that household isolation would delay the peak by two to three weeks and reduce
                the peak by approximately 25%.56 Mr Johnson announced this new advice at the
                10 Downing Street evening press conference on 16 March.57 According to Professor
                Sir Christopher Whitty, Chief Medical Officer for England from October 2019, this
                change to the isolation guidance represented a “very major policy shift” and was
                “very important in terms of its impact on COVID-19”.58

      4.28. The Inquiry recognises that the Department of Health and Social Care’s initial
            planning for the implementation of self-isolation measures was undertaken in
            conditions of limited information and uncertainty, including lags in real-time health
            data on the registration of new cases.59 However, had there been better planning,
            their task would not have been so difficult and it may have been possible to
            introduce the first self-isolation measures with a framework to support their delivery
            in place. The absence of such a framework created a clear risk that some individuals
            and households would face difficulties in complying with the requirements of self-
            isolation.


      Adherence to self-isolation measures
      4.29. Although self-isolation is a well-established public health measure to control the
            spread of infectious disease outbreaks, the scale of the self-isolation measures
            introduced in response to Covid-19 was unprecedented and placed significant
            restrictions on people’s lives and freedoms.

=== PAGE 139 ===
                                                                                                     Chapter 4: Isolation




4.30. As Professor James Rubin (Chair of SPI-B from February 2020 to June 2021)
      explained, test, trace and isolate systems require people to “engage in a complex
      set of unfamiliar behaviours. Adherence to TTI [test, trace, isolate] guidance cannot
      be assumed.”60 It was therefore crucial that mechanisms were in place to monitor
      adherence to the measures from the point at which they were implemented, so as
      to ascertain whether they were having the desired effect.


An incomplete picture
4.31.    Despite the importance of ensuring widespread adherence to the restrictions,
         the UK government and devolved administrations lacked any form of precedent
         for measuring adherence. As the 2011 Strategy did not include a detailed analysis
         of the use of measures to slow the spread of an influenza virus, it also lacked any
         basic assumptions about levels of compliance with restrictive measures over a
         sustained period of time.61

4.32. In the days leading up to the implementation of the first widespread self-isolation
      measures, SAGE and its sub-groups worked on the assumption that compliance
      with individual self-isolation at home would be about 70%. SAGE caveated this
      assumption by stating that there was considerable uncertainty about this figure
      and that modelling suggested that the effects of the measures “vary in proportion
      to the compliance rate”.62

4.33. On 13 March 2020, a day after the first self-isolation measures were implemented
      across the UK, SAGE acknowledged that there was “no strong evidence for public
      compliance rates changing during a major emergency”.63 SAGE recommended that
      the impact of the measures should be evaluated and stressed that measuring the
      impact of all interventions depended on sufficient, relevant data delivered on time.64

4.34. However, the Inquiry heard that there were no formal mechanisms established by the
      UK government at the start of the pandemic to gather data on levels of adherence
      in a sustained and systematic way.65 Some small-scale surveys and polling were
      carried out in the initial weeks and months of the pandemic, but these relied
      primarily on self-reported data, where people either chose to sign up or were paid
      for their responses.66 Dr Rob Orford (Chief Scientific Adviser (Health) for Wales from
      January 2017 to April 2024) told the Inquiry that the “self-report nature of most data”

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




               collected during the pandemic added to the uncertainty when it came to assessing
               adherence.67 Inevitably, the data collected did not accurately reflect reality.68

      4.35. SPI-B noted that, while there were numerous “initiatives underway”, including
            surveys carried out across the UK and analysis of Google Maps and transport
            data, there was an overall lack of coordination of the data, which limited their use
            for modellers.69 It also concluded that there remained gaps in its understanding
            of levels of adherence among children and for household isolation.70

      4.36. Ministers in the UK government considered adherence levels across England to be
            low. The Department of Health and Social Care’s tracker surveys, conducted across
            England between 30 March and 1 April 2020, revealed that only 30% of symptomatic
            people reported staying at home for seven days. In addition, surveys indicated
            that people’s intended compliance with self-isolation rules following the onset of
            symptoms was also low, at 48%.71

      4.37.    Professor Sir Michael McBride (Chief Medical Officer for Northern Ireland from
               September 2006) told the Inquiry that adherence was considered to be “reasonably
               good on the part of most of the public” in Northern Ireland but varied across different
               groups.72 Survey data suggested that younger people, people from financially
               disadvantaged backgrounds and men were less likely to adhere to the guidance.73

      4.38. The results of surveys conducted in Scotland concluded that compliance with self-
            isolation was “generally high”.74 However, Caroline Lamb (Director General for Health
            and Social Care in the Scottish Government and Chief Executive of NHS Scotland
            from January 2021 to August 2026) explained that levels of compliance did not
            “appear to be significantly different across existing studies that have looked at
            compliance in England”.75

      4.39. In Wales, the first systematic approach to collating data on adherence to self-
            isolation guidance, including potential barriers, was carried out by Public Health
            Wales in September and October 2020. The study found that “the majority of
            contacts” reported that they did not leave their house during the self-isolation
            period, but 12.3% of people did leave home once or more.76

                                                                                                      Chapter 4: Isolation




4.40. The four nations commissioned further studies on adherence to self-isolation
      towards the end of 2020 and into 2021.77 However, Professor Dame Theresa Marteau
      (a member of SPI-B from March 2020 to February 2022) told the Inquiry that,
      despite NHS Test and Trace having a budget of £37 billion over two years, and its
      effectiveness depending critically upon self-isolation of those infected with the virus
      and their contacts, there was no valid measure of self-isolation at any time during
      the pandemic.78

4.41.    As the pandemic progressed, there remained difficulties in obtaining a complete
         or fully reliable picture of self-isolation adherence levels.79 A study published in
         July 2024 in the journal Public Health found there were significant flaws in how
         self-isolation was defined, operationalised and measured during the pandemic
         and, overall, there was no consensus for how adherence to self-isolation should
         be monitored.80 In Professor Rubin’s view, gathering data on levels of adherence
         to measures such as self-isolation is a “key component” of any effective test, trace
         and isolate system.81 The Inquiry agrees.

4.42. Notwithstanding the limitations in the available data on adherence – including
      inconsistencies and weaknesses in methodology – by the summer of 2020, there
      was a growing consensus within the UK government that levels of adherence with
      the self-isolation requirements were too low.82 In response, in September 2020,
      the UK government passed legislation making self-isolation a legal requirement;
      the Welsh Government adopted the same policy in November 2020.83


Introduction of a legal duty to self-isolate
4.43. Ministers in the Department of Health and Social Care first considered voluntary
      and mandatory options for self-isolation for positive cases and their contacts in
      May 2020.84 In a paper requested by Mr Johnson, the option of mandating self-
      isolation was considered to carry significant risks, including the likelihood that an
      enforced approach would likely undermine public cooperation with “the previous
      steps in the ‘test and trace’ journey”.85 It highlighted that people would be less likely

               to report symptoms and take tests, to download and use the NHS COVID-19 app
               and to provide information on their recent contacts if they thought that they, or their
               contacts, would then be penalised for subsequent failure to self-isolate.86 On this
               basis, the paper concluded that there were “strong arguments” for building on the
               voluntary approach.87

      4.44. Despite this assessment, the question as to whether self-isolation should become
            a legal requirement was revisited later that summer. In a Covid-19 Operations
            Committee (Covid-O) meeting in June 2020 regarding NHS Test and Trace, Dido
            Harding, Baroness Harding of Winscombe (Executive Chair of NHS Test and Trace
            from May 2020 to May 2021), reiterated her view that test and trace should not
            be subject to police enforcement and further expressed her concerns that the
            enforceability of measures would be limited without a significant surveillance
            and digital infrastructure.88

      4.45. Mr Hancock told the Inquiry that, in August 2020, the Department of Health
            and Social Care:

                   “recommended continuing for the time being with developing the communication
                    and engagement strategy, but working on increased enforcement measures and
                    looking at options for introducing legal requirements”.89

      4.46. On 18 September 2020, the Cabinet Office provided Mr Hancock with a set
            of proposals to improve compliance with self-isolation by way of enforcement.
            In particular, the proposals suggested introducing a payment of £500 to those
            required to self-isolate and implementing a legal requirement to self-isolate following
            a positive test or being identified as a close contact of someone infected with
            Covid‑19.90

      4.47.    That same day, the Covid-19 Taskforce consolidated these proposals in a paper
               that recommended taking a “carrot and stick” approach to self-isolation, combining
               financial support with mandatory requirements and enforcement measures.91 At a
               COBR meeting on 22 September 2020, attended by Mr Hancock, the committee was
               asked to agree:

                   “a strong UK-wide message on the need for behavioural change, increased
                    compliance and stronger enforcement, and a package of measures to bring
                    about the same”.92

                                                                                                        Chapter 4: Isolation




Mandatory requirements

4.48. On 28 September 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care introduced The
      Health Protection (Coronavirus, Restrictions) (Self-Isolation) (England) Regulations
      2020, which required people in England to self-isolate when they tested positive
      for Covid-19 or came into contact with someone who tested positive. They set
      out financial penalties for individuals who failed to self-isolate after being notified
      to do so by NHS Test and Trace, including fines ranging from £1,000 to £10,000.
      The Regulations also prohibited employers or agencies from allowing workers
      who are required to self-isolate to work in any place except the place where they
      are self-isolating.93 Wales adopted the same approach, with The Health Protection
      (Coronavirus Restrictions) (No. 4) (Wales) Regulations 2020 coming into force on
      9 November 2020.94

4.49. Scottish ministers decided not to implement new legislation, on the basis that it
      was not in line with the Scottish Government’s “broader strategy of achieving public
      compliance through encouragement, persuasion and support”.95 Northern Ireland
      also relied on a voluntary compliance model, which sought to achieve adherence
      through strong public health guidance and the public’s willingness to “do the right
      thing”.96

4.50. This was in accordance with the 2011 Strategy, which emphasised that making public
      health restrictions legal obligations should be a “last resort”.97 It stated:

              “[T]he Government will encourage those who are well to carry on with their
                normal daily lives for as long and as far as possible, whilst taking basic
                precautions to protect themselves from infection and lessen the risk of
                spreading influenza to others.”98

          It also noted that “the presumption is that the Government will rely on voluntary
          compliance with national advice”.99

4.51.     Mr Johnson told the Inquiry that the UK government initially thought that “advice
          alone would be enough”, but by September 2020, as the second wave of cases
          increased, the data were showing that people were not isolating “to the extent
          required”.100 Mr Hancock also considered the decision to impose a legal duty to

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




                comply with self-isolation to be a “necessary and proportionate” step in the fight
                against the virus.101

      4.52. The Inquiry accepts that there was a need to address low levels of adherence to the
            self-isolation measures. However, as noted in the Inquiry’s combined Module 2ABC
            Report, the complexity and frequency of changes to the legislative response to the
            pandemic, including new laws on self-isolation, caused confusion among members
            of the public.102 In a 2022 lessons learned report produced by NHS Test and Trace
            in England, it was noted that the aim of the legal duty to provide clarity about the
            public’s obligations under the self-isolation regulations in England and Wales “may
            have been undermined at times” by their complexity and that better planning might
            have helped simplify the regulations.103


      Enforcing the law on self-isolation
      4.53. Police forces were relied upon to ensure compliance with the self-isolation
            regulations in England and Wales. The police promoted voluntary compliance with
            self-isolation requirements before moving to enforcement through their four-step
            escalation policy – engage, explain, encourage and enforce (known as the ‘four Es’).
            However, problems with the enforcement stage of this approach soon emerged after
            the legal duty to self-isolate came into force.104

      4.54. Underlying this was a structural difficulty. Data-sharing between the police and
            the Department of Health and Social Care was inadequate, restricting the flow of
            information that effective enforcement required. In England, NHS Test and Trace
            shared with the police the name, address, telephone number, period of self-isolation
            and the date a notification to self-isolate was received by an individual.105 However,
            in November 2020, the Crown Prosecution Service advised the Department of
            Health and Social Care that the information provided regarding the identity of people
            required to self-isolate did not meet the evidential standards to enable the police to
            issue fixed penalty notices (fines issued for certain offences, allowing the recipient to
            avoid prosecution by paying a set amount) or to prosecute people for failing to pay
            fines in court.

      4.55. Martin Hewitt, Chair of the National Police Chiefs’ Council from March 2019 to April
            2023, told the Inquiry that, despite frequent engagement with the Department
            of Health and Social Care, the system “never got itself into a place” where police
            could verify the identity of a person alleged to have breached the regulations.106

                                                                           Chapter 4: Isolation




         As such, in “the vast majority” of cases, no enforcement action was taken.107 Further
         challenges included ensuring that people were notified correctly of the legal
         requirement to self-isolate.108

4.56. Mr Hancock was aware by November 2020 that “the data systems used by NHS
      Test and Trace were not designed with enforcement and robust identity-checking
      in mind”.109 Despite this, the Department of Health and Social Care planned to
      rely on data derived from NHS Test and Trace to support enforcement action.
      It is concerning that the enforcement framework was being developed by the
      Department of Health and Social Care on the basis of data infrastructure known
      to be ill-suited for that purpose.

4.57.    A subsequent UK Health Security Agency lessons learned report (dated April 2022)
         suggested that the introduction of new laws requiring self-isolation may not have
         been the only way to increase understanding and compliance – noting that, in future,
         “non-legislative options could be explored again”.110

4.58. The challenge of enforcing the self-isolation regulations highlights a critical need
      for improved planning and coordination between governments, police forces and
      enforcement bodies, specifically regarding the way data are used and shared.

The effectiveness of enforcement

4.59. As early as May 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care was made aware
      that evidence showed that people are more likely to comply with something “they
      choose to do, rather than something they are instructed to do”.111 Indeed, Mr Hewitt
      emphasised that collaboration and encouragement with members of the public
      resulted in a higher chance of compliance.112

4.60. Professor Susan Michie (Director of the Centre for Behaviour Change at
      University College London and member of SPI-B from March 2020 to February
      2022) believes that enforcement measures, particularly financial penalties, were
      “counterproductive”.113 She explained that the decision to issue substantial fines
      showed a lack of understanding of “how communities were thinking and behaving”.114
      People who lacked the financial means to self-isolate were less likely to seek testing,
      given their concerns about being unable to comply with isolation requirements –
      particularly where this risked income loss or conflicted with caring responsibilities.
      Professor Michie further observed: “SPI-B were not consulted on the consequences
      of issuing fines and how that would impact on adherence.”115

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      4.61.     The Inquiry understands the reasoning underpinning the introduction of mandatory
                requirements. However, introducing self-isolation requirements as “guidance only”
                may have resulted in similar compliance levels and would have given ministers the
                flexibility needed to consider changes in the fast-moving context of the pandemic.116


      Support to self-isolate
      4.62. The importance of providing support to people required to stay at home was
            recognised at an early stage of the pandemic. During March 2020, a series of cross-
            government meetings took place to consider how those most at risk from Covid-19
            could be supported through self-isolation at home.117

      4.63. On 22 March 2020, the UK government advised clinically extremely vulnerable
            people (those identified by the UK government as having medical conditions that
            meant they were particularly likely to develop severe illness or to die from Covid-19)
            to stay at home and ‘shield’ for a period of time to reduce their risk of infection. The
            UK-wide shielding programme was launched the same day.118 A press release issued
            by the UK government announced measures including a helpline, local authority
            hubs for those without a support network of friends and family, and partnerships
            with the food industry to deliver basic food parcels and other essential items.119

      4.64. This early focus on the provision of support for people required to remain at home
            for prolonged periods indicated that it was reasonably foreseeable that larger
            numbers of people asked to self-isolate would encounter similar challenges.
            These included reduced access to essential services, difficulties in obtaining
            food, medication and other basic necessities, and increased social isolation.120

      4.65. Professor Madelynne Arden, expert witness on adherence to behaviours associated
            with test, trace and isolate, described how the feasibility of self-isolation differed
            between demographic groups:

                    “[F]or some people, self-isolation merely means working from home, in a nice
                      house with a garden. For other people, self-isolating is incredibly hard, they
                      haven’t got the support, they haven’t got the housing to self-isolate, they haven’t
                      got the finances to enable them to do that if they’re in precarious employment.
                      So we have a kind of huge difference in terms of how difficult self-isolation is.”121

=== PAGE 147 ===
                                                                                                    Chapter 4: Isolation




4.66. Self-isolation was especially difficult for many people living with a disability. Being
      required to stay at home significantly affects the ability to access care from others,
      both inside and outside the home. Kamran Mallick, who gave evidence on behalf of
      Disability Rights UK and Disability Action Northern Ireland, told the Inquiry that the
      negative impacts of self-isolation are felt much more acutely by disabled people.122

4.67.    Two months after the introduction of the first self-isolation measures, the impact of
         self-isolating was becoming increasingly apparent. Caroline Abrahams, who gave
         evidence on behalf of Age UK, told the Inquiry that many older people living on their
         own were unable to access food and essential care. In May 2020, Age UK and the
         Malnutrition Task Force published an announcement on the Age UK website warning
         that self-isolation and loneliness, together with restricted access to shopping and
         support, would leave many older people at risk of malnutrition.123

4.68. Although SPI-B provided information to SAGE on the need for both financial and
      practical support as early as March 2020, no detailed policy or delivery plan for
      providing widespread practical and financial support across the UK was in place
      at that time.124 While initiatives across the four nations sought to address specific
      challenges as they arose by drawing on existing support mechanisms and by new
      sources of funding, the Inquiry heard from a number of witnesses who considered
      that the provision of financial and practical support was delayed and inadequate.125
      Lara Wong, founder and leader of Clinically Vulnerable Families, told the Inquiry
      that workers often felt “compelled to continue working”, despite requirements to
      self-isolate, due to the inadequacy and insufficiency of financial support during the
      pandemic.126


Practical support
4.69. At the outset of the pandemic, the UK government lacked detailed arrangements
      for supporting the needs of people required to self-isolate at home. Isolation policy
      during the earliest weeks of the pandemic focused on isolation within dedicated
      quarantine facilities, rather than self-isolation at home.127 Key operational elements
      – such as systems for identifying needs and funding arrangements – emerged
      progressively over time.

4.70.    While a support programme for clinically extremely vulnerable people was
         implemented before the announcement of the first national lockdown on 23 March
         2020, no equivalent programme existed for the wider population following the
         announcement of the requirement to self-isolate. In England, the NHS Volunteer
         Responders programme, launched in March 2020, was designed to support the

                NHS and people who were shielding or self-isolating by collecting prescriptions,

      4.71.     Mr Hancock told the Inquiry that the nature of support measures for those
                self-isolating required “a major coordination effort across national and local
                government”.129 However, a study carried out by the London School of Economics
                and Political Science on the test, trace and isolate strategies in England found that
                “it took many months of quite fierce lobbying to get any meaningful contact and
                especially resourcing” for local authorities to supplement the NHS Test and Trace
                effort at a local level.130

      4.72. By the summer of 2020, the UK government had allocated £300 million to local
            authorities in England to develop plans to implement measures to identify and
            contain outbreaks.131 However, it took a further month for the Department of Health
            and Social Care to establish a policy framework for how it would work in partnership
            with local authorities to support them with local outbreak response, known as the
            ‘COVID-19 Contain Framework’.132

      4.73.     The COVID-19 Contain Framework provided detail on the funding available to local
                authorities to enable them to provide the non-financial support and resources
                needed for people required to self-isolate.133 In its guidance paper to local
                authorities, the UK government described the framework as a means of ensuring that
                local authorities could provide the most appropriate forms of practical, social and
                emotional support to those who have no other means of getting that support.134

      4.74.     In practice, the UK government’s approach to funding and guidance for local
                authority-led practical support was developed incrementally. For example, certain
                initiatives, such as the Medicines Delivery Service, were not rolled out until early
                2021.135

      4.75. On 19 January 2021, the Department of Health and Social Care identified that only
            17% of people with symptoms were coming forward for testing and that avoiding

        self-isolation was the “single biggest reported barrier to requesting a test”.136
        The Department of Health and Social Care acknowledged that the main barriers to
        self-isolation related to the need to access essential supplies, caring responsibilities
        or other practical tasks such as dog walking. Consequently, the Department of
        Health and Social Care suggested working with local authorities to provide:

            “a more consistent, visible and accessible framework of practical, social and
             emotional support for people self-isolating, modelled on the Shielding support
             framework”.137

4.76.   In Wales, coordinated engagement between the Welsh Government and local
        government regarding support for self-isolation took place much earlier in the
        pandemic. The Covid-19 Core Group, established by the Welsh Government in March
        2020, included ministers (such as the Minister for Health and Social Services and the
        Minister for Housing and Local Government) in the development of the pandemic
        response. By 25 March 2020, the Core Group membership was widened to include
        the Leader of the Welsh Local Government Association.138

4.77.   Welsh Government ministerial advice submitted on 29 June 2020 to the Minister
        for Housing and Local Government and the Minister for Health and Social Services
        set out the “core support and the more complex areas of support that we would
        expect each local authority to be able to provide”.139 Suggestions included picking
        up shopping and prescriptions and help understanding advice and mental health
        services.140 There were also more involved forms of support such as provision of
        temporary accommodation for those who could not self-isolate safely with others
        (eg if a person with whom they lived was clinically extremely vulnerable) and
        substance misuse support.141

4.78. Vaughan Gething (Minister for Health and Social Services in the Welsh Government
      from May 2016 to May 2021) thought that local authorities were best placed to deal
      with the provision of support and had the infrastructure to provide that support.142
      Jo-Anne Daniels (Interim Director General, Education, Social Justice and Welsh
      Language in the Welsh Government from April 2022 to April 2024) told the Inquiry
      that the provision of practical support “evolved throughout the pandemic” to support
      and encourage people to self-isolate.143 She said that support was adapted to
      respond to feedback from local authorities and third-sector organisations.144

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      4.79.     The Scottish Government also reacted quickly, establishing schemes to provide
                forms of practical support.145 Ms Lamb recalled that the Scottish Government’s
                approach from the outset “recognised that key to compliance” was “creating
                the conditions” for people to follow self-isolation requirements.146 The National
                Assistance Helpline was in operation from 14 April 2020 and provided a single
                point of contact for requests for support relating to self-isolation, such as food and
                essential medication.147 The Local Self-Isolation Assistance Service also provided
                people with access to a range of practical support, including a local government
                hardship fund, food fund and business support schemes.148

      4.80. In Northern Ireland, the Covid-19 Test, Trace and Protect Strategy was published on
            27 May 2020 and was intended to “better reflect the need to provide people who
            had been asked to isolate with access to appropriate support mechanisms to protect
            others in the community”.149

      4.81.     The Department for Communities (Northern Ireland) played a lead role in the
                provision of practical support and established a number of initiatives to help those
                self-isolating. Professor McBride told the Inquiry that the programmes “relied upon
                and utilised the wide network of relations, including with community and voluntary
                groups”, established and maintained by the Department for Communities (Northern
                Ireland).150

      4.82. Working alongside the Department of Health (Northern Ireland), the Department for
            Communities (Northern Ireland) put arrangements in place early on in the pandemic
            to ensure food deliveries to those who were unable to access food through online
            shopping, family, friends or local support networks.151 For example, the Department
            for Communities (Northern Ireland) established the Covid-19 Community Help Line,
            a freephone helpline operated by Advice NI, which began operation on 27 March
            2020. The helpline was available seven days a week.152

      4.83. The Northern Ireland Executive also ensured that communications about Test,
            Trace and Protect indicated the availability of forms of support for self-isolation.
            In the Department of Health (Northern Ireland)’s strategy document dated 27 May
            2020, the public was informed of the importance of “early, effective and supported
            isolation” to prevent onward transmission of infection.153 Recognising the need for
            support for those self-isolating was a central part of Northern Ireland’s Test, Trace
            and Protect strategy.

Coordinating practical support to self-isolate
4.84. Structures and mechanisms for coordinated working and collaboration between
      national and local governments were already in place before the Covid-19 pandemic.
      The Civil Contingencies Act 2004 provides a structure for local response planning
      and multi-agency collaboration. In England and Wales, local resilience forums
      (composed of representatives from local public services, including local authorities,
      emergency services, the NHS and others) are responsible for coordinating local
      emergency preparedness and response and recovery activities.154 This role is carried
      out by regional resilience partnerships in Scotland and emergency preparedness
      groups in Northern Ireland.155 These bodies supported the implementation of the
      shielding programmes for clinically extremely vulnerable people across the UK as
      early as March 2020, providing a key coordination and oversight role. However,
      there was considerable variation in the use of these local forums when it came to
      implementing wider practical support for self-isolation.156

4.85. Local authorities are often best placed to coordinate and deliver support given
      their existing relationships with communities, links with voluntary and community
      organisations, and knowledge of vulnerable population groups. The importance
      of coordinated collaboration between central governments and local authorities
      during a public health emergency should not be underestimated.


               “In March 2020 religious leaders, parish councillors, local GPs and business
                 owners met to discuss how our isolated rural community could support the
                 residents in the village and outlying farms and homes. Over 100 volunteers
                 came forward and each was given 20 homes within a specific postcode to
                 support. They delivered postcards with their names and contact details to
                 each of those 20 homes and delivered medication and shopping, took dogs
                 for walks, chatted from a safe distance and in some cases befriended those
                 living alone who would otherwise have had no contact with anyone for
                 months.”157

                Contributor, Every Story Matters

      4.86. Will Garton (Director General for Local Government, Growth and Communities at
            the Ministry of Housing, Communities and Local Government from March 2022)
            did not disagree and told the Inquiry that the department had a “pre-established”
            engagement framework to communicate with local government and that insight
            from local authorities was critical in developing the local support offer to those
            shielding.159 However, when it came to practical support for self-isolation, England
            took longer than the devolved administrations to coordinate with its local partners
            in rolling out timely and dedicated support.

      4.87.    Joanna Killian (Chief Executive of the Local Government Association from March
               2024 to July 2026) told the Inquiry that there was a “regrettable delay” in the UK
               government’s engagement with local government on key issues and decisions,
               which affected the design of the test and trace measures.160 Greg Fell (President from
               November 2023 of the Association of Directors of Public Health, the representative
               body for directors of public health across the UK) told the Inquiry that the early phase
               of NHS Test and Trace had been designed using a “one size fits all” approach.161
               He explained that directors of public health bring extensive knowledge of their
               local communities and of local health and social care systems. As a result, local
               teams had to:

                    “fill the gaps. For example by providing a wrap-around service, directing people
                      to local self-isolation payment systems and other support, such as food bank
                      provision and voluntary sector support.”162

      4.88. Mr Dyson acknowledged that local authorities worked with a range of voluntary
            and community organisations to provide practical support to people self-isolating.
            However, there was “considerable local variation in the extent, visibility, and
            timeliness of support available” across local communities.163 It took until January
            2021 for the Department of Health and Social Care to develop proposals for
            working with local authorities to provide a “more consistent, visible and accessible

4.89. Mr Dyson accepted, with the benefit of hindsight, that directors of public health had
      not been engaged at an early enough stage by the Department of Health and Social
      Care. He explained:

            “It may seem a very obvious point but having local authority Directors of Public
             Health and their team, able to work with those voluntary organisations, faith
             groups, Community Champions and so forth, is probably the most important
             thing when it comes to cutting through and making sure that people not only
             have got the support they need, but also that they understand the importance
             of engaging with testing and self-isolation.”165

4.90. Professor Carolyn Wilkins (Divisional Director of Contain at NHS Test and Trace from
      July 2020 to May 2021) told the Inquiry that there was a lack of understanding in the
      UK government of the role, capacity and capabilities of local authorities and other
      local bodies in relation to health protection. Pre-existing reductions in local authority
      funding had had a significant impact on their ability to respond. While local resilience
      forums had pandemic influenza plans in place, Professor Wilkins described there
      being “significant challenges” in implementing effective responses to Covid-19 on
      the ground.166 James Bethell, Lord Bethell (Parliamentary Under Secretary of State
      for Technology, Innovation and Life Sciences at the Department of Health and Social
      Care from March 2020 to September 2021), told the Inquiry that local resilience
      forums may have been “better placed” to tailor support for individuals and their
      contacts, but “lacked the power and resources to coordinate any targeted local
      response”.167

4.91.   The delay in collaborative working across national and local government in England
        contrasted with Wales, where existing public sector structures were used from the
        start of the pandemic. In Wales, there was a focus on “joint local-regional-national
        working” across the Welsh Government, local authorities, Public Health Wales, health
        boards and the NHS Wales Informatics Service.168 At the start of the pandemic, the
        Welsh Government, Public Health Wales and four local resilience forums agreed to
        establish a Public Health Strategic Coordination Support Group to ensure regular
        communication between strategic partner organisations. This group met for the first
        time on 23 March 2020.169

      4.92. The Convention of Scottish Local Authorities played a key role in liaising with local
            government and regional resilience partnerships on test, trace and isolate, ensuring
            that regional resilience partnerships facilitated links to relevant local partners.170

      4.93. In Northern Ireland, the Civil Contingencies Group (Northern Ireland) was the
            principal strategic civil contingencies preparedness body, together with a number
            of sub-groups, including emergency preparedness groups, divided into regions.171
            All local councils are members of their local emergency preparedness groups.172
            A report for the Northern Ireland Emergency Preparedness Group for the period
            2020 to 2025, which included a review of the response to the pandemic by
            emergency preparedness groups, stated that the resilience team was “utilised to
            its fullest capacity during all stages of the response”, including working with local
            government and other organisations to co-design a model to ensure the effective
            distribution of food and medicines during Covid-19.173

      4.94. A debrief exercise, conducted in January 2023 by the Department for Levelling Up,
            Housing and Communities (which was named the Ministry of Housing, Communities
            and Local Government from January 2018 to September 2021 and from July 2024),
            identified the need to improve understanding in the UK government of the role of
            local resilience forums. It also highlighted the importance of closer collaboration
            between the Department of Health and Social Care, the Department for Levelling
            Up, Housing and Communities, and local resilience forums in the development of
            a new pandemic preparedness strategy, to ensure that the local resilience forum
            perspective is “adequately reflected”.174

      4.95. Mr Dyson told the Inquiry that having protocols in place to work with local
            communities at the earliest stages of a pandemic response is a “critical part” of
            future preparedness for a pandemic.175 The Inquiry agrees and notes the Department
            of Health and Social Care’s proposals within its 2026 Pandemic Preparedness
            Strategy, which sets out that the UK government will define clearer expectations
            and guidance for local responders and determine the appropriate support for local
            planning.176

      4.96. Local resilience forums, regional resilience partnerships and emergency
            preparedness groups should be involved in the development of self-isolation
            policy – as part of a wider test, trace and isolate strategy – well in advance of the
            next pandemic. For this to happen, there must be clear responsibilities for local
            resilience forums, regional resilience partnerships and emergency preparedness
            groups to plan for and respond to pandemics – and they must be supported by
            the UK government and devolved administrations. The UK government said that
