4.97.     The Inquiry therefore recommends that local resilience forums, regional resilience
          partnerships and emergency preparedness groups should prepare plans now for
          practical support for people asked or directed to self-isolate during a pandemic.
          This support should be tailored to the needs of their communities and plans should
          take account of the full range of practical support that may be required, including
          emotional and psychological support.


          Recommendation 4: Local support for self-isolation
          Local resilience forums, regional resilience partnerships and emergency
          preparedness groups should, as part of the planning required by the Civil
          Contingencies Act 2004, prepare plans now for the scale-up of practical
          support for their community if self-isolation is required during a future pandemic.
          Plans should be tailored to the needs of local communities, including groups
          that may face challenges in adhering to self-isolation measures.
          The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
          Ireland Executive should review and monitor progress of the development of
          these local support plans, as well as monitor their delivery in a future pandemic.



Financial support
4.98. The packages of economic support for adult social care workers were considered
      in the Inquiry’s Module 6 Report.178 Other forms of financial support provided during
      the pandemic for people, including those receiving benefits, and for businesses
      are being considered in Module 9: Economic response. For the purposes of this
      Module 7 Report, the Inquiry is focusing solely on financial support for those self-
      isolating.

4.99. Early advice from SAGE and SPI-B provided to the Department of Health and

      highlighted that self-isolation measures may have implications for people who are
      unable to work or who are on zero-hours contracts.179 However, the response was
      not uniform across all four governments. Ms Killian told the Inquiry that it was “not
      immediately recognised” by the UK government how many households would be

      Existing financial support

      4.100. Throughout March 2020, the Department for Work and Pensions made a number
             of temporary changes to Statutory Sick Pay.181 Statutory Sick Pay is a payment made
             by employers to employees if they are too ill to work, or are incapable of work, due
             to a specific disease or disability. These amendments applied to England, Wales and
             Scotland to ensure that eligible people could access financial support to comply with
             self-isolation measures.182

      4.101. The amendments came into force on 28 March 2020 and temporarily expanded the
             circumstances in which Statutory Sick Pay could be paid to include people who were
             self-isolating due to Covid-19. Statutory Sick Pay was widened again, in May 2020
             and in July 2020, to reflect changes to public health advice at the time.183

      4.102. The changes to Statutory Sick Pay in Northern Ireland were largely aligned with
             those in England, Wales and Scotland, although they were implemented via separate
             legislation, under the Statutory Sick Pay (General) (Coronavirus Amendment)
             Regulations (Northern Ireland).184 As discussed below, in contrast to England,
             Scotland and Wales, Northern Ireland introduced a self-isolation payment via its
             existing Discretionary Support scheme – alongside the amendments to Statutory
             Sick Pay – as early as March 2020.185

      4.103. In Wales, in May 2020, Mr Gething attended a Shadow Social Partnership Council
             meeting to highlight work being done with Test, Trace, Protect in Wales and heard
             from trade union representatives that greater clarity was needed on pay support for
             employees during the pandemic. Mr Gething told the Inquiry that these concerns
             were fed back to the UK government.186

      4.104. By June 2020, it had become clear to Mr Johnson and decision-makers in the
             Cabinet Office, including Rishi Sunak MP (Chancellor of the Exchequer from February
             2020 to July 2022), that further policy options for financial support for people self-

         isolating were required.187 On 19 June 2020, the Cabinet Office commissioned the
         Treasury to provide options to assist people required to self-isolate on a regular
         basis due to compliance with NHS Test and Trace, people classed as clinically
         extremely vulnerable who were unable to work in a ‘safe workplace’, and population
         groups impacted by local lockdowns.188

4.105. In May and early June 2020, research carried out by NHS Test and Trace in England
       revealed that levels of compliance with the programme were considered low among
       people from deprived postcodes, specific ethnic minority communities, people with
       low literacy rates and asylum seekers. The research showed that one of the major
       factors that prevented compliance with NHS Test and Trace was the financial cost to
       individuals and their families of complying with self-isolation measures.189

4.106. As a consequence, Baroness Harding requested financial support for those required
       to self-isolate to boost overall compliance with the programme, funded by the
       existing NHS Test and Trace budget. However, Baroness Harding identified a lack of
       willingness to address this proactively in the UK government.190 She said there was:

              “money in the budget, you know, we didn’t spend all of our budget, and I also
               think that spending more on self-isolation would have reduced the need for
               testing. But I wasn’t the decision maker. The decision maker in this was the
               Chancellor and, at every opportunity from June onwards, the Chancellor
               rejected the proposals.”191

4.107. Baroness Harding told the Inquiry that a system that made it materially easier for
       the most disengaged and vulnerable communities to self-isolate was “fundamental”
       and would likely have achieved better results.192

4.108. Analysis carried out by the Covid-O Covid-19 Taskforce in September 2020
       corroborated the growing consensus in the Cabinet Office that further financial
       assistance for self-isolation was needed. The taskforce found that many workers
       faced significant financial pressures, resulting in a “substantial reduction in income
       with an administrative delay before money reaches the claimant”.193

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      Dedicated self-isolation payment schemes

      4.109. SAGE highlighted the likely impact on poorer people and those on zero-hours
             contracts in early March 2020, SPI-B having noted:

                  “For single parents, adherence may become impossible. For poorer families, loss
                   of income and increased household bills (heating, electricity, food delivery, etc.)
                   will occur concurrently with loss of social services provided through schools (free
                   school meals, after school clubs, etc).”194

      4.110. In June 2020, NHS Test and Trace suggested different forms of support mechanisms
             – including options to isolate outside the home in a hotel, financial compensation for
             lost earnings and the option of making self-isolation mandatory.195

      4.111.   However, it took until September 2020 for England, Scotland and Wales to
               introduce dedicated self-isolation support payment schemes. By the time they
               were established, Northern Ireland’s Discretionary Support Self-Isolation Grant
               had already been in place for six months.196

      4.112. Given that there were already a range of new and existing financial support
             packages available to people during the pandemic (which are being considered
             in Module 9: Economic response), the growing demand for a more clearly defined
             and targeted self-isolation support payment generated “significant debate” within
             the UK government.197 Dan York-Smith (Director General for Tax and Welfare at the
             Treasury from March 2023 to August 2025) told the Inquiry that Mr Sunak recognised
             the importance of the test, trace and isolate system, but raised concerns about
             additional support payments creating an incentive not to work. In addition, Mr Sunak
             was concerned about the risk of fraudulent claims for self-isolation support.198

      4.113. By July 2020, various options for dedicated financial support for self-isolation
             were being considered by the Treasury, including ring-fenced grant funding to all
             local authorities in England.199 A month later, Mr Sunak shared the proposals with
             Mr Johnson. Although Mr Sunak reiterated his concerns regarding the introduction
             of further financial support, he suggested that, if implemented, the payment scheme
             must be targeted to minimise the risk of the scheme becoming an unaffordable
             national programme.200 A pilot payment scheme was subsequently introduced
             in Blackburn, Pendle and Oldham on 23 August 2020, which offered a payment
             of £13 per day for eligible people.201

                                                                                     Chapter 4: Isolation




4.114. Work continued on an England-wide scheme involving a flat payment of £500 for
       the required number of days of self-isolation.202 The Department of Health and
       Social Care led on the development of the scheme, with input from the Treasury
       regarding funding, and the Ministry of Housing, Communities and Local Government
       regarding its operational delivery.203 The Test and Trace Support Payment scheme
       was subsequently announced on 20 September 2020 and was in operation from
       28 September 2020.204

4.115. Discussions between the UK government and devolved administrations on further
       financial support for self-isolation were ongoing during the summer of 2020.205
       Shirley-Anne Somerville MSP (Cabinet Secretary for Social Security and Older
       People from June 2018 to May 2021) recalled receiving information from the UK
       government on the pilot scheme, which informed early discussions on a similar
       scheme in Scotland.206

4.116. Once the UK government confirmed its intention to develop a dedicated self-
       isolation support payment scheme, the Welsh Government also began work
       implementing a similar scheme.207 The Self-Isolation Support Scheme was introduced
       on 16 November 2020 and provided a £500 payment (to align with the payment
       scheme in England) for people who were employed or self-employed, had been told
       to self-isolate and were in receipt of Universal Credit or another benefit. Payments
       were backdated to 23 October 2020, where appropriate.208 As in England, the
       scheme also included discretionary payments, funded from a local government
       hardship fund, to provide support to people who did not meet the criteria for the self-
       isolation payment but who would suffer financial hardship as a result of being asked
       to self-isolate.209 This included people with no recourse to public funds, people with
       a total income below £500 a week (net) and people in receipt of Statutory Sick Pay.210

4.117.   The Scottish Government adopted the same approach.211 The Scottish Government
         utilised its existing Scottish Welfare Fund to deliver the Self-Isolation Support Grant,
         so that the payment could be administered by local authorities at speed.212 The Self-
         Isolation Support Grant was launched on 12 October 2020, with eligibility backdated
         to 28 September 2020. Eligibility and payment value of £500 were matched with
         England’s scheme, to ensure equality of treatment for low-income workers required
         to self-isolate.213

=== PAGE 160 ===
      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      4.118. Northern Ireland utilised its existing Discretionary Support scheme to deliver support
             payments to eligible people self-isolating, calculated on an individual basis. Unique
             to Northern Ireland, the Department for Communities (Northern Ireland) had sole
             responsibility for developing the policy, legislation and guidance relating to the
             Discretionary Support Self-Isolation Grant, which had been introduced on 25 March
             2020.214

      4.119. Professor Richard Machin, who provided expert evidence to the Inquiry on the
             impact of financial support on test, trace and isolate, concluded that common
             issues emerged across all payment schemes, relating to eligibility and adequacy of
             payments, accessibility and awareness of the schemes, and challenging decisions.215

      Eligibility for financial support

      4.120. The Inquiry heard evidence that the Test and Trace Support Payment in England
             was criticised for the “highly varied eligibility criteria between councils” operating the
             discretionary element of the scheme.216 A briefing produced by the Nuffield Trust and
             Resolution Foundation found that nearly two-thirds of applications were rejected in
             England due to difficult application requirements (such as proving financial hardship)
             together with lack of funding for discretionary payments.217

      4.121. In the months following the rollout of the scheme, the Department of Health and
             Social Care and NHS Test and Trace put forward proposals for self-isolation pilots to
             better understand the needs of disproportionately impacted communities and the
             factors that may improve adherence to self-isolation measures.218 Up to this point, the
             eligibility criteria for the Test and Trace Support Payment excluded many people on
             low incomes from claiming under the scheme.219 However, the Treasury considered
             that there was limited evidence regarding the impact of financial support and did not
             agree to sign off on the funding for the pilots until April 2021.220

      4.122. The schemes in Northern Ireland and Wales were broader in scope.221 Professor
             Machin observed that Welsh authorities were able to deliver “more ‘generous’
             discretionary self-isolation support” thanks to higher levels of resource.222 Refused
             applications were primarily due to claimants being unable to demonstrate that they
             met the eligibility criteria.223

      222 INQ000575999_0051 para 196
      223 INQ000575999_0051 para 197

=== PAGE 161 ===
                                                                                                      Chapter 4: Isolation




Awareness of financial support for self-isolation

4.123. Professor Machin noted that, in England and Wales, a Trades Union Congress
       survey of more than 2,000 workers in May 2021 found that, overall, only 21% of
       workers were aware of the financial support schemes for self-isolation. Further, that
       awareness dropped to 18% for people in less secure work and to only 16% for both
       low-paid workers and those not eligible for sick pay.224

4.124. In Wales, regular monitoring of the Self-Isolation Support Scheme included data on
       the number of applications, rejection rates and overall awareness of the scheme.
       However, by February 2021, ministers in the Welsh Government identified that more
       work was required with local authorities and across the voluntary sector to improve
       awareness of the payment scheme and to clarify eligibility criteria. Professor Machin
       found that it was common for people to become aware of the Self-Isolation Support
       Scheme only after they had begun self-isolating.225 It is clear that those who might
       need financial support for self-isolation the most – such as low-income groups –
       were also the most likely to be unaware that there was a financial support scheme
       to help them.226

4.125. Similar issues arose in Scotland. The number of awards being made was low
       during the first few months of the scheme, “believed to be connected to low public
       awareness”.227 In its review of the Scottish Welfare Fund, which administered
       the Self-Isolation Support Grant, the Scottish Government found that there was
       “considerable variation” in the promotion of the scheme, and a degree of “reticence”
       among some local authorities about how much promotion of the scheme was
       appropriate, given it had a fixed eligibility criteria and limited resources.228

4.126. Northern Ireland’s Discretionary Support scheme was distinct from schemes in other
       parts of the UK. Introducing a new payment within the existing Discretionary Support
       scheme proved beneficial because it enabled immediate support to be delivered to
       people self-isolating, using established systems.229

4.127. Professor Machin observed that particular consideration should be given to groups
       of people who have no previous experience of claiming financial support from
       government schemes.230 It should be a fundamental principle that those requiring
       assistance are not prevented from accessing it due to complex application processes
       or inadequate telephone support.

      4.128. Measures to improve awareness of financial support for self-isolation by improved
             communications are considered in Chapter 6: Communications and data.

      Challenging decisions

      4.129. Not all of the payment schemes included a mechanism for review of decisions.
             As the Self-Isolation Support Grant in Scotland was administered through the
             existing Scottish Welfare Fund, it was subject to existing appeal processes.
             Applicants who were unhappy with a decision could appeal to the Scottish Public
             Services Ombudsman.231 In Northern Ireland, the administrative process for the
             Discretionary Support Self-Isolation Grant scheme meant that a review could be
             requested within 28 days of a decision.232 Neither of the schemes in England or
             Wales included a mechanism for review of decisions.

      4.130. Future self-isolation payment schemes should include clear guidance on eligibility
             criteria, how to make a claim and the process for requesting a review of claim
             decisions.

      Financial support for self-isolation in the future

      4.131. The Inquiry acknowledges that government decision-making about the provision
             of financial support to those needing to self-isolate involves a complex interplay
             of central and local authority powers, budgetary considerations and the need to
             minimise the risk of misuse or fraud.

      4.132. However, it should have been foreseen that adherence to self-isolation guidance
             – and the subsequent legal requirements in England and Wales – would be linked
             to the degree of financial impact a person experienced as a result of lost income.
             Those in financially secure situations were more likely to be able to afford to self-
             isolate and thereby protect their health and the health of those around them,
             whereas those in financial poverty and without prior experience of claiming financial
             support would be more likely to become infected with, and potentially spread,
             Covid-19 because they could not afford to give up work. These issues should
             have been factored into pandemic planning (see Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to
             implement a test, trace and isolate system).

      4.133. The Inquiry has already recommended, in its Module 6 Report, that financial support
             should be provided to protect adult social care staff from financial loss as a result of
             compliance with mandatory self-isolation regulations.233 This issue was not unique to

=== PAGE 163 ===
                                                                             Chapter 4: Isolation




         the adult social care sector; therefore, protection must also be offered to the public
         more broadly.

4.134. Given the scale of the sacrifices that were asked of the public, this lack of forward
       planning and preparedness to support people to self-isolate is unacceptable. As
       Baroness Harding explained, isolation support must be at the “forefront” of a test,
       trace and isolate system.234 The Inquiry recommends that future governments should
       ensure that a strategy for effective self-isolation includes the provision of financial
       support from the outset. This financial support should be available to the public in
       circumstances where a novel pathogen poses such a danger that the government
       asks people to self-isolate. In those instances, whether a test is available and
       indicates a positive case, or a test is unavailable but close contact has occurred,
       adequate support to offset the burden of time away from work should be provided.
       Clear, accessible guidance on available support should be issued and this should be
       accompanied by targeted and effective communications that ensure that the public
       is aware of the financial support schemes and how to access them. There should be
       mechanisms in place to monitor the effectiveness of those schemes, so that changes
       can be made as needed.


         Recommendation 5: Financial support to self-isolate
         The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
         Ireland Executive should put in place financial support schemes for individuals
         complying with self-isolation rules during a pandemic.
         The following principles should apply:

         • There should be clear and straightforward guidance on eligibility criteria,
            application processes and payment arrangements for the support schemes.

         • Information about the introduction of support schemes and how to access them
            should be communicated clearly and made available in accessible formats.

         • Mechanisms should be in place to monitor the effectiveness of support
            schemes.
         Plans should also be developed and maintained to enable these financial support
         schemes to be scaled up rapidly during a pandemic.

=== PAGE 164 ===
Chapter 5: Border control
measures

=== PAGE 165 ===
                                                                                Chapter 5: Border control measures




Introduction
5.1.      Border control measures have a different purpose from other non-pharmaceutical
          interventions. Rather than limiting the spread of an infection within a country, they
          are primarily designed to prevent or at least reduce the risk of infection (or a new
          variant of the infection) entering a country.1 Possible border control measures include
          screening travellers for symptoms, requiring or advising a person to self-isolate on
          arrival, requiring medical proof that a person does not have the virus, and part or full
          closure of the borders to all travellers.2 The impact on the spread of infection can be
          particularly significant when restrictions are applied to travellers from a jurisdiction
          with a higher incidence of the infection than the country to which they are travelling.3

5.2.      International border control therefore has an important and distinct role in
          pandemic response. Yet deciding how to exercise that control is not a simple matter.
          The closure or restriction of borders is immensely complex in a world increasingly
          reliant on international trade and travel, as the Inquiry noted in its combined Report
          relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C.4

5.3.      As with testing, tracing and isolation, there was no pre-pandemic UK-wide strategy
          for international border control measures. Implementation of international border
          control on the scale seen during the Covid-19 pandemic was unprecedented
          in recent times. Prior to 2020, the most recent UK border control measures
          were implemented during the 2013 to 2016 Ebola virus disease outbreak, when
          approximately 11,400 incoming travellers at four airports and one international train
          station were screened on entry to the UK for symptoms.5 By contrast, the Covid-19
          response saw 1,260,400 air travellers subjected to controls in July 2020 alone
          (widespread border control measures having been imposed for the first time on
          8 June 2020).6

5.4.      Border control measures may also serve as an important tool to reassure the public
          and increase confidence in the response to a pandemic.7 The perception that a virus
          is allowed to cross the UK’s borders could impact public confidence in government
          decision-making more generally. For example, during the Covid-19 pandemic, a large
          proportion of the population believed that full border closure was required to protect
          the public.8 While the scientific advice did not support full border closure and the
          general public may not have fully appreciated the complications of complete closure,

          effective communication was key in ensuring that the public understood this and
          recognised the value of complying with the restrictions that were in place.

  5.5.    This chapter examines the use of different border control measures during the
          Covid-19 pandemic and considers what improvements might be made in advance
          of the next public health emergency.


  International good practice during the Covid-19
  pandemic
  5.6.    During the Covid-19 pandemic, the World Health Organization listed management
          of the risk of exporting and importing cases from communities with high risks of
          transmission as one of six criteria for controlling the spread of infection.9

  5.7.    As noted earlier in this Report, Singapore, South Korea, Germany, Hong Kong and
          Taiwan were internationally recognised for their effective responses to Covid-19.
          They implemented early measures to control their borders. Some of these measures
          included testing, tracing or isolating at the border. Other countries and territories also
          implemented border control measures (see Table 1).

5.8.     By April 2020, 93% of countries and territories had put measures in place – such
         as flight suspensions, border closures and travel restrictions – to control their
         international borders.10 By contrast, the UK government only requested (rather than
         directed) travellers from Wuhan, China, to self-isolate in January 2020 and travellers
         from some other high-risk areas to self-isolate in February 2020. This did not prevent
         a coachload of tourists from Wuhan entering the UK and travelling from England to
         Scotland and then to Northern Ireland and the Republic of Ireland in February 2020.11
         The UK government implemented no further border control measures until June
         2020.12

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      5.9.     In April 2020, the International Comparators Joint Unit was established by the
               Foreign and Commonwealth Office and the Cabinet Office’s Joint Intelligence
               Organisation.13 Its purpose was to “provide timely, relevant and objective analysis of
               different countries’ responses to the crisis”.14 The unit produced briefings on specific
               Covid-19-related issues at the request of UK government departments, which were
               used to inform advice to ministers and for discussion at meetings of the Covid-19
               Operations Committee (Covid-O, a Cabinet sub-committee that drove the policy
               and operational aspects of the Covid-19 response).15

      5.10.    From 23 April to 9 October 2020, the International Comparators Joint Unit produced
               49 analyses.16 For example, on 4 May 2020, it produced a presentation entitled
               ‘Borders’ that compared border controls in different countries around the world.17
               In a letter dated 16 June 2020, 10 Downing Street said that the unit was critical
               in providing an improved basis of information for decision-making on everything
               related to Covid-19 and was valued by “everyone involved from the Prime Minister
               down”.18 On 29 September 2020, Simon Case (later Lord Case), Cabinet Secretary
               from September 2020 to December 2024, said that he had never seen examples
               of international assessment that had as much impact as the unit’s work – it was part
               of almost every decision.19

      5.11.    The establishment of the International Comparators Joint Unit in April 2020 was
               a positive step, providing a structured means of analysing international responses
               and informing decision-making.

      5.12.    The Inquiry considers that there would have been value in having the kind of
               formal, detailed international comparative analysis conducted by the International
               Comparators Joint Unit from the earliest stages of the pandemic, particularly given
               the speed with which many countries had implemented border control measures
               by the spring of 2020. James Bethell, Lord Bethell (Parliamentary Under Secretary
               of State for Technology, Innovation and Life Sciences at the Department of Health
               and Social Care from March 2020 to September 2021), told the Inquiry about the
               importance of early international comparative analysis:

                   “A key element of Taiwan’s successful strategy was the early closing of their
                     borders which meant that even in the early days, by the time we were live to the
                     potency of their contact tracing, it may have already been too late to replicate it
                     – it is almost impossible to have an effective track-and-trace system with open
                     borders.”20

5.13.     Each country, territory or region may have unique geographical, societal and legal
          characteristics, but meaningful comparisons can be made. There is clear value in
          studying international border control measures and analysing whether there are
          lessons that can be applied in the UK. A future pandemic strategy should include
          evaluation of previous international good practice and a plan for ongoing analysis
          of international border control measures during a pandemic in order to inform the
          UK’s approach.


Border control in the UK during the Covid-19
pandemic
5.14.     In its Module 1 Report, having considered previous health emergencies
          and pandemic exercises in relation to border control measures, the Inquiry
          concluded that there was no framework in place as at January 2020 to allow the
          UK government or the devolved administrations to weigh up the relative costs and
          benefits of the many different border interventions open to them.21 As noted in the
          Inquiry’s combined Module 2ABC Report, in order to be effective, border restrictions
          must be stringent and accompanied by a system of quarantine.22

5.15.     In January and February 2020, travellers from specified countries and regions were
          asked to self-isolate for 14 days.23 From June 2020, the UK introduced a series of
          progressively more stringent border controls, including self-isolation requirements,
          passenger information collection, risk-based ‘travel corridors’,24 and pre-departure
          and post-arrival testing regimes.25 These were subsequently broadened in response
          to variants of the virus – most notably through the suspension of travel corridors and
          the introduction of a ‘traffic light’ system and hotel quarantine for high-risk countries
          – before being eased for vaccinated passengers and ultimately lifted in March 2022
          (see Figure 7).26

      Challenges in maintaining an aligned four-nations approach
      5.16.         Management of the UK border is a reserved matter and therefore remains the
                    responsibility of the UK government.27 Responsibility during the pandemic was split
                    across multiple departments. The Home Office is the lead department for border
                    security, but its role was primarily to implement and enforce policy decisions made
                    by other departments. The Cabinet Office acted as the central coordinator for
                    decision-making, hosting and facilitating the Covid-O meetings. The Department of
                    Health and Social Care was responsible for health measures, including quarantine
                    and testing rules. The Department for Transport led the Global Travel Taskforce,
                    which developed the traffic light policy, while the Foreign and Commonwealth
                    Office (the Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office from September 2020)
                    provided guidance to British nationals.28 The Home Affairs Committee in August
                    2020 found that there was a “lack of clarity over which Government department or
                    agency [was] ultimately responsible for coordinating Covid-19 border policy”.29

5.17.    This position was further complicated because health is a devolved matter
         (or a ‘transferred’ matter in Northern Ireland) and so falls within the purview
         of the devolved administrations. Each government is responsible for measures
         at its borders to prevent danger to public health and the spread of infection.30

5.18.    A report in 2013 about the response to the outbreak of Ebola that year (jointly
         produced by the Department of Health and Social Care, NHS England and Public
         Health England) noted the different disease management controls and powers
         in relation to ports of entry and raised the possibility of a review to align those
         powers.31 There is no evidence that a review was carried out.

5.19.    The desirability of alignment between the four nations of the UK was apparent during
         the Covid-19 pandemic.32 Boris Johnson (Prime Minister from July 2019 to September
         2022) told the Inquiry:

              “Because of our shared island status, I always felt it was critical to have
               agreement on border-related issues with the DAs [devolved administrations]
               and that encompassed the ensuing TTI [test, trace and isolate] policies.”33

         Ministers from the devolved administrations were also committed to alignment.
         For example, Nicola Sturgeon (First Minister of Scotland from November 2014 to
         March 2023) told the Inquiry that the Scottish Government supported four-nations
         alignment on international border control requirements, where possible.34 Similarly,
         a briefing from the Welsh Government’s Covid-19 Recovery and Restart Directorate
         in January 2021 noted that Wales had long been in favour of a four-nations
         approach.35

5.20. Mr Johnson told the Inquiry that the “valuable perspectives” of the devolved
      administrations were “always sought out when a UK-wide approach was needed,
      including on border measures”.36 He said that representatives from the devolved
      administrations were invited to relevant Covid-O meetings, such as those relating
      to managed quarantine.37 The Four Nations Health Protection Oversight Group
      (originally called the UK Health Protection Oversight Group) was also set up from
      28 April 2021 as a mechanism for coordination and collaboration between the
      four governments about broader health protection matters, though not specifically
      focused on border control measures.38 The UK Health Protection Committee was
      established at the same time, with terms of reference similar to those of the Four
      Nations Health Protection Oversight Group.39 The oversight group was intended for

      5.21.     Public Health Scotland told the Inquiry that, in the main, collaboration arrangements
                between the four nations worked well. However, there were occasions when
                changes to border measures were communicated to Public Health Scotland through
                the media rather than via established intergovernmental channels (although the
                Home Office began to communicate changes on a weekly basis as processes
                improved).41 A Scottish Government review of international travel dated 20 May 2021
                also noted that the UK government made decisions without consulting devolved
                administrations.42 However, as set out below, the Scottish Government itself
                imposed border controls for travellers from England without consultation with the
                UK government.

      5.22. The review also stated that the UK government had suspended the four-nations
            decision-making process (which by that stage appears to have largely consisted
            of Covid-O meetings) and that ministers from the devolved administrations
            had not attended Covid-O meetings about international travel decisions since
            12 March 2021.43

      5.23. In a letter dated 26 May 2021, Robin Swann (Minister of Health for Northern Ireland
            from January 2020 to October 2022 and from February to May 2024) noted that
            there had been a lack of devolved administration involvement in the Covid-O
            meetings about international travel requirements since April 2021. Instead, UK
            government decision-making took place at closed Covid-O meetings, with decisions
            being communicated to devolved administrations through updates at officials’
            meetings.44

      5.24. The Inquiry is not in a position to determine whether the UK government consistently
            sought the views of the devolved administrations when a UK-wide approach was
            required.45 However, while arrangements for collaboration generally functioned
            well, relations between the UK government and devolved administrations also
            broke down at times. This is concerning given the critical importance of effective
            cooperation between all four governments during the pandemic. The devolved
            administrations should have been fully involved in the decision-making process
            and not left with the perception that they had unequal – and at times unsatisfactory
            – influence in decisions about the pandemic response that affected the whole of

                                                                                 Chapter 5: Border control measures




          the UK.46 The Inquiry, in its combined Module 2ABC Report, noted similar concerns
          about effective collaboration.47

5.25. The Inquiry recognises, however, that, even when the input of all four nations was
      sought, alignment could not be assumed. Professor Sir Christopher Whitty (Chief
      Medical Officer for England from October 2019) told the Inquiry:

              “Debates between the four nations of the UK on border measures were one of
               the few areas which proved quite difficult even at a technical level. The starting
               point for political leaders in the four nations on border issues was different,
               perhaps unsurprisingly as attitudes to borders are essentially a political issue.”48

5.26. Consequently, there were times during the Covid-19 pandemic when the four nations’
      approaches differed. Professor Sir Gregor Smith (Chief Medical Officer for Scotland
      from December 2020) told the Inquiry that Scotland had a lower risk appetite than
      the UK government about border control measures: “This occasionally led to some
      disagreement about the timing and extent to which additional border controls were
      implemented for some countries.”49

5.27.     One such disagreement arose in July 2020, when the four governments were
          considering which countries to put on a list allowing travellers from that country
          to enter the UK without having to self-isolate. The Scottish Government initially
          disagreed with the other three on whether Spain should be included on the list.
          However, the risk from Spain increased within days and all the nations of the UK
          removed Spain from the list.50 This particular divergence was therefore relatively
          short-lived. Had it lasted longer, however, it could have caused significant problems
          in enforcing international border controls in the UK.

5.28. The approach to the Delta variant of Covid-19 provides another example of the
      risks caused by different approaches across the UK’s international border. The
      Delta variant was first identified in India and cases emerged in the UK in early
      April 2021.51 All direct international arrivals into Scotland at that time had to enter
      managed quarantine. By contrast, direct international arrivals into England only had
      to quarantine if travelling from a specific list of countries (called a ‘red list’, as further
      described below). India was not added to England’s red list until the end of April
      2021, in part due to operational challenges because a substantial number of British
      nationals were in India for a festival at that time.52 A Scottish Government review of

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




               international travel restrictions dated 20 May 2021 linked the Delta variant’s entry
               into Scotland with the differences in approach between England and Scotland:
               “The [Delta] variant entered Scotland while our restrictions were at their highest
               level because [the] UK Government would not engage with our concerns.”53

      5.29. Difficulty in collaboration and cooperation could also, at times, be exacerbated
            by measures to stop movement across internal borders within the UK, as illustrated
            by the following examples:

               • In May 2021, Ms Sturgeon imposed restrictions on travel into Scotland from Bolton
                   for public health concerns, based on Covid-19 levels.54
               • A ban on non-essential travel into Scotland from Manchester and Salford was
                   announced for the same reasons on 21 June 2021.55 This was publicly opposed
                   by Andy Burnham (Mayor of Greater Manchester from May 2017 to June 2026).56
                   He was concerned about the lack of prior warning from the Scottish Government,
                   the lack of clarity about the criteria being used to determine that Manchester and
                   Salford posed a risk (given that they had similar Covid-19 rates to Dundee), and
                   the financial impact on individuals because they had to cancel holidays to
                   Scotland.57 The ban was lifted just over a week later.58 This was following a review
                   of data in relation to the spread of Covid-19 variants of concern.59
               • Similarly, in November 2020, the Welsh Government banned travel outside
                   Wales without a reasonable excuse, because England was imposing a national
                   lockdown.60

      5.30. Alignment between all four nations should always be sought. Where there are
            differing approaches that cannot be reconciled, serious consideration should be
            given to ways in which the risks of travellers crossing internal borders once they
            have entered the UK can be mitigated.

      5.31.    Northern Ireland had an additional issue to consider: its border with the Republic
               of Ireland. During the pandemic, restrictions on international travel did not apply
               between Northern Ireland and the Republic of Ireland.61 This represented a challenge
               in terms of controlling transmission when in-country restrictions were different.62

                                                                             Chapter 5: Border control measures




         Northern Ireland chose formally to align its international border control measures
         with the UK rather than with the Republic of Ireland.63 However, there was close
         liaison between the UK government, the devolved administrations and the Irish
         government in relation to border health measures.64 The Northern Ireland Executive
         entered into a Memorandum of Understanding with the Irish government on 7 April
         2020 to allow regular data-sharing and the sharing of information on international
         travel policies.65 This included weekly meetings between the two governments’
         health departments and the sharing of data in relation to the trajectory of the
         pandemic and information about international travel policy, as well as working closely
         together in order to inform policies about border control.66 Professor Sir Michael
         McBride (Chief Medical Officer for Northern Ireland from September 2006) told the
         Inquiry that this close joint working was “key in managing the pandemic response
         across many areas”.67

5.32. The complexity of relations between the four nations, together with the additional
      complexity arising from the land border between Northern Ireland and the Republic
      of Ireland, must be carefully examined and addressed in the development of any
      future border control approach.

5.33. The UK government’s March 2026 Pandemic Preparedness Strategy: Building Our
      Capabilities included the development and maintenance of a four-nations approach
      to pandemic response, including in relation to ports and borders.68 This is a positive
      step. Cooperation and coordination between the four nations and the Republic of
      Ireland should be pursued wherever possible to ensure that border control measures
      are effective and operable in practice.


Delay in implementing border control measures: January to
May 2020
5.34. At its first meeting about Covid-19 on 22 January 2020, the Scientific Advisory
      Group for Emergencies (SAGE) supported the position of the Department of Health
      and Social Care’s New and Emerging Respiratory Virus Threats Advisory Group
      (NERVTAG), which advised that the benefits of port-of-entry screening were likely to
      be low.69 SAGE said that it would review its position on screening travellers at ports
      of entry only if a simple, specific and rapid test was available and deployable at scale
      across the UK. Temperature checking and other forms of screening were unlikely to
      be of value and were likely to have high false positive and false negative rates.

=== PAGE 176 ===
  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




  5.35. As the Inquiry noted in its combined Module 2ABC Report, temperature screening
        was not always effective because not everyone with Covid-19 would have a
        temperature.70 SAGE considered and discounted the utility of temperature screening
        at its meeting on 22 January 2020.71 Requiring a certificate of medical health
        with test results also had its limitations, because it would require the country of
        departure to have tests available that could confirm that a person did not have the
        virus.72 Therefore, people without ready access to those tests prior to travel could
        be adversely affected, including British nationals seeking repatriation.73 However,
        testing at the border was not viable due to the UK’s lack of mass testing capability
        in early 2020 (see Chapter 2: Testing). Testing at the border was therefore another
        casualty of the UK’s lack of readiness to test at scale in early 2020.

  5.36. A further possible border control measure was isolation on arrival in the UK, and this
        was the first border control measure to be introduced on 27 January 2020, albeit
        only for certain travellers. Travellers arriving into the UK from Wuhan province in
        China were asked (not required) to self-isolate for 14 days.74 Three days later, the UK
        risk level was raised from low to moderate.75 By the end of January 2020, the first
        case of Covid-19 had been confirmed in the UK.76

  5.37.     A paper by the Scientific Pandemic Influenza Group on Modelling (SPI-M), dated
            3 February 2020, stated that restricting travel from countries with outbreaks and
            quarantining all travellers from China could potentially delay the spread of Covid-19
            in the UK. However, it concluded that the impact of any intervention or combination
            of interventions was hard to determine because the transmissibility of Covid-19 was
            poorly understood.77

  5.38. At its meeting that same day, SAGE discussed its modelling of the impact of travel
        restrictions. SAGE estimated, albeit with limited data, that, if the UK reduced
        imported infections by 50%, that might delay the onset of an epidemic in the UK
        by about five days.78 If imported infections were reduced by 75%, that might produce
        a delay of 10 days, and 90% might result in a delay of 15 days. SAGE concluded that
        a reduction in imported infections of 95% or more “maybe buys a month”.79 While

         noting that a month of additional preparation time for the healthcare system would
         offer a significant advantage, SAGE also warned:

              “To prevent imported infections along these lines would require draconian and
               coordinated measures, because direct flights from China are not the only route
               for infected individuals to enter the UK.”80

5.39. No specific border control measures were agreed at that meeting. The Department
      for Transport and the Home Office were given the task of producing more wide-
      ranging estimates about the number of people entering the UK from China for the
      next SAGE meeting the following day.81 At that meeting, on 4 February 2020, SAGE
      confirmed that it remained content with its conclusions the previous day about
      the impact of international travel restrictions. Other measures to limit spread were
      discussed, such as wearing face masks, shutting down public transport and regional
      closing of schools.82 At its meeting on 5 February 2020, COBR decided not to close
      the borders for arrivals from China.83

5.40. On 14 February 2020, the Cabinet discussed whether all flights from China to the
      UK should be suspended. Professor Whitty repeated the advice from SAGE that
      suspending flights from China would only slow the spread by a maximum of five
      days.84 Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health and Social Care from July 2018
      to June 2021) said that it would be challenging to convince people that flights from
      China should continue.85 Flights from China were not suspended.

5.41.    There were only nine confirmed cases of Covid-19 in the UK by 18 February
         2020.86 This would have been an ideal time to implement at least some travel
         restrictions. The Technical Report on the COVID-19 Pandemic in the UK (published
         on 1 December 2022) concluded that measures to prevent importation of cases
         generally mattered most when there were very low levels of domestic infection but
         higher rates elsewhere. Measures could then delay establishment of the infection at
         the outset of the pandemic, allowing time to assess other potential measures.87

5.42. Some border control measures were implemented on 25 February 2020. However,
      these only amounted to the UK government telling travellers from certain specified
      countries or regions – such as China, Iran and Italian cities under containment

  5.43. International travel continued throughout the pandemic, although, following a sharp
        decline from the spring of 2020, rates of travel remained below pre-pandemic levels.
        UK government statistics record that approximately 6.8 million people arrived in the
        UK by air in February 2020 and approximately 3.8 million people in March 2020.89
        UK government figures indicate that the recorded daily death count from Covid-19
        in England began to increase rapidly from March, rising from one death by 5 March
        2020 to 2,698 deaths by 1 April 2020.90 In April 2020, 112,300 people arrived in the
        UK by air, representing a 99% reduction compared with April 2019.91

  5.44. However, border control measures were eased in March 2020, at a time when
        international arrivals continued and the number of daily deaths was increasing.
        On 13 March 2020, guidance for international travellers from specific countries
        was superseded by guidance about staying at home, linked to the move from the
        ‘contain’ phase to the ‘delay’ phase of the pandemic response (see Chapter 2:
        Testing and Chapter 3: Contact tracing).92 However, that ‘stay at home’ guidance was
        significantly less stringent than the guidance it replaced for travellers from certain
        high-risk areas.93 It required people to stay at home for at least seven days only if
        they had a new continuous cough or a high temperature, rather than because they
        had travelled from a high-risk area (like the measures implemented on 25 February
        2020).94

  5.45. This decision was taken one day after the decision to stop community testing and
        contact tracing in the UK (see Chapter 2: Testing and Chapter 3: Contact tracing).95
        Accordingly, if an infected traveller without a cough or high temperature arrived in
        the UK at this time, they would have been able to pass through the border and to
        avoid any testing or tracing once in the country. There was nothing to stop them
        infecting others.

        home in France. Ms de Prudhoe talked about the lack of border controls at that time:

5.47.     On 23 March 2020, Dominic Raab (Secretary of State for Foreign and
          Commonwealth Affairs and First Secretary of State from July 2019 to September
          2021) and Grant Shapps (Secretary of State for Transport from July 2019 to
          September 2022) advised all British tourists and short-stay travellers to return to
          the UK if commercial flights were still available.97 There was still no requirement for
          incoming travellers to self-isolate on arrival, aside from the general ‘stay at home’
          guidance for symptomatic people across the UK.

5.48. Professor Whitty told the Inquiry that the economic, trade, diplomatic, consular
      and legal implications of full border closure are “profound”.98 The scientific advice in
      early 2020 was that the benefits of full border closure would be relatively minimal.99
      There would also be negative consequences for the economy and for public health
      from interruptions of food and medicine supplies.100

5.49. However, the complexities of full border closure did not mean that other options
      could not have been effective in the first six months of the pandemic. Each may
      have had its limitations (as set out above) but they could have included:

          • restricting flights from certain countries (as discussed by the UK government
              and SAGE in February 2020);101
          • imposing mandatory isolation on travellers from ‘hotspot’ countries;
          • taking the temperature of arriving travellers;                     102


          • requiring travellers to provide a certificate of medical health obtained before they
              started their journey;103

  5.50. Even if the suspension of all flights from China to the UK in February 2020 might
        have only led to a brief delay in the spread of Covid-19 – as was advised by SAGE
        and Professor Whitty at the time – such a delay might nonetheless have provided
        valuable additional time to prepare healthcare systems for the impact of the
        pandemic. As noted by the Inquiry in its Module 3 Report, there was a significant
        lack of critical care capacity in hospitals across the UK by March 2020. The UK
        government and devolved administrations had to work urgently to increase
        their critical care capacity and maximise the size of the healthcare workforce.106
        Any additional time would likely have assisted those efforts.

  5.51.    A 14-day self-isolation requirement for all incoming travellers could have had a
           significant impact. In its November 2020 analysis of border control measures,
           the Animal and Plant Health Agency concluded that, from a public health
           perspective, this measure was the most effective of the border control scenarios
           considered.107 Its modelling suggested that this could have reduced the number
           of infected travellers entering the UK in June 2020 from 377 people per week to
           about 83 people per week.108 The agency found a similar estimated impact from its
           modelling relating to August 2020.109

  5.52. An August 2020 Home Affairs Committee report concluded that the lack of a
        requirement to quarantine (ie isolate) in March 2020 worsened the UK’s experience
        of Covid-19.110 It was particularly critical of the decision to remove guidance to self-
        isolate from 13 March 2020, at a time when other countries were strengthening their
        border measures and when hundreds of new Covid-19 cases were arriving in the
        UK every day. It described this as an “inexplicable” decision and said that the failure
        to have any special border measures in place in mid-March was a serious error.111
        Professor Whitty also told the Inquiry about border control measures:

                “This is one area however where we should not allow hindsight to give a
                 favourable gloss. Were we running things again earlier intervention would have
                 in my view been prudent and I would have advised measures more rapidly.”112

=== PAGE 181 ===
                                                                               Chapter 5: Border control measures




5.53. The decision not to implement widespread border control measures from the outset
      of the Covid-19 pandemic represented a missed opportunity by the UK government
      to try to slow the spread of infection by reducing the risk of imported cases.

5.54. As noted in the Inquiry’s combined Module 2ABC Report, there were no detailed
      plans in place, let alone developed procedures, for border control measures early in
      the pandemic.113 Advance planning (as discussed in Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness
      to implement a test, trace and isolate system) places governments in a far better
      position to evaluate pandemic responses.

5.55. The decision to allow virtually unrestricted international travel in the first six months
      of 2020 came at a time when the UK had no mass testing or widespread contact
      tracing and its healthcare resources were stretched thin.114 Overall, the UK was left in
      an extremely weak position from which to face the rapidly rising rates of infection.


Travel corridors with exempt countries: June to November 2020
5.56. Dame Priti Patel (Secretary of State for the Home Department from July 2019 to
      September 2022) told the Inquiry that, by 22 May 2020, air passenger arrivals into
      the UK were reduced by 99% compared with the previous year.115 This was as a
      consequence of the Covid-19 outbreak itself, rather than because of concerted
      action on the part of the UK government or devolved administrations.116 In May 2020,
      the Cabinet considered proposals for new border control measures and ways in
      which those might be enforced.117

5.57.    A package of new measures came into force across the UK on 8 June 2020.118
         These included:

         • a “ramping up” of communications in the UK and overseas about the new
             measures;119
         • requiring all passengers arriving in the UK to provide their contact details digitally
             through a ‘passenger locator form’;
         • advising all passengers to download and use the NHS COVID-19 app (although
             the app was in advanced development by May 2020, it was not actually launched
             until September 2020 – see Chapter 3: Contact tracing); and

            • requiring all individuals (except those importing goods or travelling within the

                to self-isolate for 14 days on arrival either in their own accommodation or in

  5.58. The new package of measures was introduced because Covid-19 transmission rates
        had reduced in the UK, and international travel presented a higher risk of importing
        cases or strains and triggering a new wave of the pandemic.121

  5.59. On 10 July 2020, all countries were categorised in accordance with the level of
        risk that a traveller from that country would import Covid-19. Those considered
        sufficiently low risk would fall into the ‘exempt’ category. Any traveller from one of
        those countries would no longer be required to self-isolate on arrival in England,
        Scotland, Wales or Northern Ireland unless they had visited or transited through a
        non-exempt country or territory in the preceding 14 days.122 Travelling from any of
        the exempt countries was often referred to as using a ‘travel corridor’. The list of
        countries in the exempt category was reviewed regularly throughout the summer
        of 2020.123 The categorisation system was a risk-based alternative to blanket self-
        isolation requirements.124

  5.60. On 7 October 2020, the UK government launched the Global Travel Taskforce, which
        was to be led by the Department for Transport.125 It was to work with representatives
        from the aviation, travel, healthcare and testing sectors, as well as the devolved
        administrations, “to support the recovery of the travel sector”.126 That included
        considering the feasibility of passengers taking a Covid-19 test in order to reduce the
        length of their self-isolation.127 Just over two months later, that particular possibility
        was realised in England through the Test to Release scheme, discussed below.

                                                                               Chapter 5: Border control measures




The Test to Release scheme and pre-departure testing:
December 2020
5.61.    As set out in Chapter 2: Testing, it was not until November and December 2020
         that lateral flow testing was trialled throughout all four nations, with certain groups
         and settings prioritised.

5.62. Border control measures subsequently changed to take account of the new
      availability of tests. On 15 December 2020, the Test to Release scheme was
      launched across England. This meant that travellers arriving in England other than
      by a travel corridor (ie those arriving from a country that was not on the exempt list)
      could shorten their period of self-isolation by paying for a private test five full days
      after leaving the country of departure. If the result was negative, they could end self-
      isolation.128 The five-day period was based on modelling by SAGE, SPI-M and Public
      Health England that suggested that a test taken on day five had an 88% success rate
      in identifying cases, rising to 94% by day eight.129 In other words, a person receiving
      a negative test result after the five-day period could be reasonably confident that
      they did not have the infection when leaving the country of departure.

5.63. The devolved administrations indicated in November 2020 that they would follow
      a different timetable, subject to scrutiny of the clinical and behavioural science
      underpinning the scheme.130 However, the scheme was not subsequently adopted in
      Scotland, Wales or Northern Ireland.131 The Scottish Government publicly indicated in
      December 2020 that it was not satisfied that moving from isolation to testing would
      provide enough protection.132 The Welsh Government considered that there was an
      inherent risk with the scheme in that advice from Public Health Wales indicated that
      it would fail to identify approximately 30% of individuals who would develop Covid-
      19.133 In December 2020, the Department of Health (Northern Ireland) also chose not
      to adopt the Test to Release scheme.134 The research for the scheme had suggested
      that about 30% of passengers would use the scheme.135 That research was based
      on a less expensive test (which was not available in Northern Ireland), so it could
      be reasonably assumed that uptake in Northern Ireland would be even lower. In
      addition, there would have been significant resourcing pressures for public sector
      bodies and on the testing capacity of the healthcare system as a whole.136

5.64. At this time, a new variant of concern began to emerge in South Africa.
      On 24 December 2020, new travel restrictions were implemented that meant

            arrivals, regardless of country of departure, would be required both to take a
            pre‑departure test and to self-isolate for 10 days on arrival into the UK.143

                                                                              Chapter 5: Border control measures




5.68. Mandatory managed quarantine was implemented in the UK from 15 February 2021.
      This meant that anyone travelling to the UK from specific countries (those considered
      to be the most high risk, also referred to as ‘red list’ countries) would be required to
      quarantine in a government-approved facility for 10 days.146 Travellers who fell under
      the new managed quarantine provisions were required to purchase a ‘quarantine
      package’, which covered accommodation and testing.147

5.69. In total, more than 247,000 people arriving from red list countries stayed in managed
      quarantine hotels.148 Of those, more than 3,000 were identified as having Covid-19;
      the quarantine may well have prevented them from transmitting the infection
      further.149

5.70. Managed quarantine placed an economic burden on travellers, costing £1,750
      for an adult, although there was the option to defer payments for those receiving
      income-related benefits.150 People with legitimate reasons to travel, such as to attend
      a funeral or visit a seriously ill family member, may therefore have been prevented
      from doing so due to the cost.151 In June 2021, a SAGE report raised a further
      concern that some travellers with complex psychological needs were being refused
      medical exemption from the requirement to quarantine in a hotel.152 In addition,
      there was a significant cost to the UK government; in total, the managed quarantine
      service cost £757 million, with the taxpayer subsidising £329 million, despite the
      scheme being intended to be self-funding.153 On top of that, as at 1 March 2022,
      the Department of Health and Social Care was owed £74 million by people who had
      not paid for their stay in the managed quarantine system or had not paid for their
      polymerase chain reaction (PCR) tests (see Chapter 2: Testing).154

5.71.    The managed quarantine service also placed burdens on those responsible for
         ensuring that people were using it. Border Force was responsible for processing
         inbound travellers required to enter managed quarantine and for implementing fixed
         penalty notices (a fine allowing the recipient to avoid prosecution by paying a set
         amount) for those who did not comply with the international travel requirements.155
         Yet, as Dame Priti Patel told the Inquiry, it did not have legal powers or the capacity
         to escort people to the managed quarantine hotels after they had been admitted

  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




            to the UK.156 She was advised in February 2021 that an additional 3,050 Border
            Force officers would be required to handle the operational impact.157 However, the
            only evidence of additional recruitment that the Inquiry has seen related to efforts
            to increase staffing levels by about 500 additional staff in May 2021.158 In theory, a
            requirement to self-isolate at home or in a hotel organised by the traveller, which is
            less expensive and does not put operational pressures on Border Force in the same
            way, should achieve the same goal as managed quarantine (although this has its own
            challenges – see Chapter 4: Isolation).

  5.72. In addition, the evidence suggests that managed quarantine requirements were
        not always implemented when international arrivals were transiting from one UK
        nation to another. Caroline Lamb (Director General for Health and Social Care in the
        Scottish Government and Chief Executive of NHS Scotland from January 2021 to
        August 2026) told the Inquiry:

                 “Managed isolation applied at the point of entry into the UK regardless of final
                  destination. Border Force should have stopped such passengers on their arrival
                  at an English airport and sent them to the nearest quarantine hotel, but this did
                  not always happen in practice where individuals had onward connections within
                  the UK.”159

            In other words, some passengers who were potentially highly infectious were
            allowed to enter the UK and travel freely to their destination without quarantining if,
            for example, they had arrived in England but were then travelling to Scotland.

  5.73. Managed quarantine is likely to be a proportionate solution during periods of high
        risk, when immediate and readily enforceable measures are needed to stem a high
        influx of infection. However, to ensure adherence, it requires those charged with
        enforcement to be properly resourced and for the complications of internal borders
        to be given greater consideration.


  The traffic light system: From the spring of 2021
  5.74.     On 9 April 2021, the Global Travel Taskforce published a framework designed to
            enable the safe return of international travel based on the level of risk of importation
            of infection posed by travellers coming from a specific country. It was a traffic light
            system, categorising countries as ‘red’ (the most high risk), ‘amber’ and ‘green’
            (the lowest risk).160 It was introduced across all four nations on 17 May 2021.161 This

                                                                                   Chapter 5: Border control measures




          sustainable reopening of international travel was described by Mr Shapps as “vital”
          to the UK economy and in connecting families who had been kept apart.162

5.75. As set out above, this was not the first time a risk-based approach had been used
      for border control during the pandemic. In July 2020, all countries were categorised
      in accordance with the risk they posed in terms of importation of infection, and the
      lowest-risk countries were placed on an exempt list (a travel corridor).

5.76. However, the traffic light system was more complex. The 2020 categorisation was a
      binary approach: either a country was on the travel corridor list – allowing travellers
      passage into the UK without self-isolating – or it was not. The traffic light system
      created three different categories of travellers and restrictions:

          • Travellers from a country on the green list would need to take a test before they
              left that country and a further test (a PCR test) on or before the second day after
              their arrival.163
          • Travellers from a country on the amber list needed to self-isolate on arrival for a
              period of 10 days. They also had to take a test before they left the amber country
              and a test on the second and eighth day after their arrival. They could take a test
              to release them from self-isolation.164 The tests after arrival needed to be PCR
              tests.165
          • Travellers from a country on the red list were subject to strict requirements,
              including a 10-day quarantine in a managed quarantine hotel, testing before they
              left the red country, and further tests on the second and eighth day after their
              arrival. They were also required to be either a British or Irish citizen, or to have
              residence rights in the UK.166 The tests after arrival had to be PCR tests.167

5.77.     Changes were made as the pandemic response progressed – in particular,
          as vaccinations became a central part of the pandemic response. By the end of
          October 2021, 85% of adults in England had received two doses of the vaccine.168

  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




  5.78. On 4 October 2021, across all four nations, a new, simplified system was launched
        as a result of the success of the UK’s domestic vaccine rollout.169 The original traffic
        light system was replaced by a red list and non-red list system.170 This more closely
        resembled the July 2020 binary categorisation system. Fully vaccinated travellers
        from a non-red list country no longer needed to take a pre-departure test, and, in
        terms of testing on the second day after arrival, fully vaccinated travellers from a
        select group of non-red list countries could substitute the cheaper lateral flow test for
        the PCR test previously required.171 Those provisions applied across all four nations.172

  5.79. On 22 November 2021, measures were changed again so that, in respect of more
        than 135 approved countries, travellers who were fully vaccinated were only required
        to take a lateral flow test before the end of their second day after arrival. All previous
        testing and isolation requirements were removed for those passengers.173

  5.80. However, within six days, new border control measures were agreed at a Covid-O
        meeting in response to an increasing number of Omicron variant cases across the
        world.174 There were changes to testing and isolation regimes for both vaccinated
        and unvaccinated arrivals.175 For example, some countries were added to the UK’s
        red list. Ministers also agreed to require all arrivals into the UK to take a reverse
        transcription polymerase chain reaction (RT-PCR) test (see Chapter 2: Testing).176

  5.81.     The swift change in approach between 22 and 28 November 2021 indicates that
            the UK had by this point developed a system that was able to monitor and rapidly
            respond to emerging new threats. That was a positive development. In a future
            pandemic, however, the UK should be prepared and able to implement new border
            measures at speed from the outset.

  5.82. On 15 December 2021, the UK government and devolved administrations removed
        all countries from the red list.177 From 11 February 2022, fully vaccinated international
        arrivals into any of the four nations were no longer required to take tests on arrival or
        self-isolate.178 On 18 March 2022, all Covid-19 travel restrictions were lifted, including
        for passengers who did not qualify as fully vaccinated.179 This marked the end of
        border control measures.
