=== PAGE 189 ===
                                                                                  Chapter 5: Border control measures




5.83. The National Audit Office estimated that the UK government spent £486 million on
      the traffic light system in 2021/22.180 Although significant resources were dedicated
      to the system, the rules for travellers changed frequently and these changes were
      not sufficiently communicated to the public, which may have affected compliance.
      A survey by the Office for National Statistics in July 2021 found that about two in five
      respondents misunderstood, or were unsure about, quarantine requirements.181 The
      Inquiry recognises that frequent changes to rules may be required in the context of
      a rapidly evolving pandemic, but rules are of little use if people do not understand
      them. It is therefore imperative that changes are communicated clearly and promptly
      to the general public if compliance is to be assured. The communications strategies
      during the pandemic, and the Inquiry’s recommendations for future strategies, are
      considered in detail in Chapter 6: Communications and data.


Enforcement of and adherence to border control
measures
5.84. The four nations imposed rules about international travel during the pandemic
      through health protection regulations.182 Border Force was then responsible for
      monitoring and enforcing the rules at the international borders. To assist with this,
      ‘interim operational instructions’ (documents explaining the new rules and their
      rationale) were issued to Border Force by the Home Office.183

5.85. Border Force’s duties included conducting passenger spot checks to ensure that
      passengers had complied with pre-departure testing requirements, processing
      travellers required to enter managed quarantine facilities and implementing a fixed
      penalty notice regime.184 Fixed penalty notices could be issued to people who did
      not comply with the travel requirements. There were 1,114 fixed penalty notices
      issued in connection with travel restrictions in 2020; in 2021 there were 5,894 fixed
      penalty notices and in 2022 there were 4,698.185


Public trust in border control decisions
5.86. There was a disconnect between public perceptions of the most effective measures
      to control the influx of the virus and the UK government’s decisions. For example,
      a survey by Ipsos MORI (an established market research and polling company) from
      October 2020 indicated that 61% of the public supported banning all travel into

           and out of the country.186 As noted in the Inquiry’s combined Module 2ABC Report,

  5.87.    However, the Ipsos MORI results suggest that the rationale for not imposing full
           border closure (eg the interruption in the flow of food and vital materials into the
           UK) was not effectively communicated to the public. If members of the public do
           not trust that measures being taken are in their best interest, then adherence
           is likely to be low. It is imperative that those responsible for making decisions
           effectively communicate their reasoning to the public (as discussed in Chapter 6:
           Communications and data).


  Awareness of border control measures
  5.88. Passenger locator forms were introduced on 8 June 2020.188 The form collected a
        passenger’s contact information so that they could easily be traced if it transpired
        that they had come into contact with an infected person.189 It was therefore a
        relatively simple, yet important, part of infection control.

  5.89. In August 2020, Border Force estimated that approximately one-third of passengers
        arriving in the UK had not completed a passenger locator form.190 Of those who had
        not, 90% reported that they were unaware of the requirement.191 On 17 August 2020,
        a weekly meeting of the Visitor Economy Working Group (which included bodies
        such as Airlines UK, the official body and association for UK-registered airlines, and
        UKinbound, the trade association dedicated to the UK’s inbound tourism sector)
        noted increasing levels of abuse towards Border Force officers from returning British
        nationals who did not seem to understand that they needed to complete the form
        regardless of the country from which they had arrived, rather than to complete it only
        if they were arriving from a country subject to quarantine.192

  5.90. Dame Priti Patel was in favour of introducing legislation to compel carriers to check
        whether a passenger locator form had been completed and to deny boarding to
        passengers who had not completed one.193 Work was undertaken to implement this
        over the following few months.194 Dame Priti Patel also advised that communications
        from various UK government departments and the devolved administrations should
        be improved to ensure that all travellers were aware of the requirement to complete
        a passenger locator form.195 In August 2020, for example, the Department for
        Digital, Culture, Media and Sport undertook to “push out” the requirement to comply

                                                                                 Chapter 5: Border control measures




          through the Tourism Industry Emergency Response Group (a group convened by
          the British Tourist Authority, comprising key tourism industry organisations and
          government bodies that developed plans for crisis scenarios and managed the
          industry’s response to specific emergencies) and the Visitor Economy Working
          Group once again.196

5.91.     Non-compliant arrivals continued, and, on 20 October 2020, Border Force was
          advised by the Home Office to begin issuing fixed penalty notices to those who had
          failed to complete a passenger locator form on arrival, even if they subsequently did
          complete one.197 A meeting of the Tourism Industry Emergency Response Group on
          10 December 2020 noted that, although millions of people had completed passenger
          locator forms, traveller awareness of the forms remained low and completion was still
          inconsistent.198 Accordingly, UK government departments developed a pack of the
          key messages around health measures at the border for industry representatives to
          give to inbound travellers, in order to obtain help from the travel industry to ensure
          compliance.199

5.92. Given that the purpose of the passenger locator form was to trace potentially
      infected people, it is concerning that a large number of people did not comply.
      Mr Johnson told the Inquiry that “the public did not find [it] easy to understand”
      the public messaging on border policy.200 This, along with the continued number
      of arrivals not complying with the form’s requirements, indicates that communications
      about border control measures may not have consistently achieved sufficient levels
      of awareness and clarity among travellers.201 The World Health Organization, in an
      article dated 16 May 2020, advised that decisions should be communicated to the
      public:

              “using simple, clear and precise messages. For example, ‘Based on A, B and C,
                we will do X, Y and Z. We will reassess the measures after N number of weeks.’
                This communication strategy aims to provide accurate information while also
                encouraging behaviours that will reduce the spread of infection.”202

5.93. As discussed further in Chapter 6: Communications and data, communication is key
      to compliance. In advance of a future pandemic, strategies should be developed to
      ensure that border control measures are communicated effectively to the public.
      This should include people situated outside the UK who are planning to travel into
      the UK, who should be made aware of requirements before they arrive at a UK port
      of entry.

  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




  Rapid changes in border control measures
  5.94. Border Force officers were often not aware of changes to border control measures
        until an extremely late stage. Paul Lincoln (Director General of Border Force from
        September 2017 to September 2021) told the Inquiry of one example where revised
        operational instructions were issued by the Home Secretary at 02:21 on 7 November
        2020, to refuse entry from 04:00 that same day.203

  5.95. Dame Priti Patel told the Inquiry that she had been:

              “concerned that decisions on health measures at the borders were not being
               taken with sufficient regard of the operational impacts for Border Force officers
               and the travelling public”.204

          She also said that the “frequency and timing of changes to the public health
          regulations was unhelpful for policing” and that, in order to build public confidence
          in the regulations, it was “essential that our officers understood the regulations”.205
          She told the Inquiry that she sought to advocate on behalf of enforcement officers
          at meetings with the Prime Minister, and at Cabinet and Covid-O meetings.206
          However, in Dame Priti Patel’s evidence to the Inquiry she did not specifically
          address the provision of operational instructions to Border Force within tight
          timeframes, the impact that had on Border Force officers or any specific measures
          she took to mitigate that impact.

  5.96. Issuing updated instructions about regulation changes to officers close to the
        time of their implementation may on occasion be necessary. However, it risks
        misinterpretation and a lack of understanding by those responsible for ensuring
        adherence. If – as Dame Priti Patel told the Inquiry – maintaining public confidence
        required police officers to understand the regulations they were enforcing, then
        the same must be true in relation to Border Force officers’ understanding of border
        control measures.207 Those responsible for enforcement of border control measures
        should be involved in the development of new regulations at the earliest stages as
        far as possible.


  A future border control strategy
  5.97.   Appropriate international border control measures should be in place at the earliest
          stages of a pandemic to attempt to control the spread of infection into the country

5.98. In its March 2026 Pandemic Preparedness Strategy: Building Our Capabilities, the
      UK government committed the UK Health Security Agency, the Home Office and
      the Department for Transport to work together to strengthen their capabilities for
      implementing and operationalising public health measures at points of entry to the
      UK.209 The UK government and devolved administrations should also, in consultation
      with Border Force, produce a strategy for international border control in a future
      public health emergency. This should include:

          • a plan for rapid, coordinated action across all four nations;
          • analysis of international good practice (from previous pandemics, but also to
             include a plan to analyse international good practice in real time as the relevant
             pandemic progresses); and
          • measures to encourage trust, awareness and adherence, including strong
             communications strategies.


          Recommendation 6: A strategy for international border control
          measures during a pandemic
          The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
          Ireland Executive should, in consultation with Border Force, produce a UK-wide
          strategy for the use of international border control measures during a future
          pandemic. This strategy should be informed by the experience of the Covid-19
          pandemic, including international examples of good practice.
          The strategy should include:

          • an analysis of different border control measures and the scenarios in which
             they will be effective and proportionate;

          • mechanisms to support early decision-making (including cooperation between
             the four nations of the UK) when border control measures may be required;

          • mechanisms to support the early implementation of border control measures
             when they are required;

Chapter 6: Communications
and data

=== PAGE 196 ===
  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




  Introduction
  6.1.      Testing, tracing and isolation were key pillars of the UK government and devolved
            administrations’ response to the pandemic. It was therefore vital that members of the
            public understood both the purpose of the measures and what was required of them.

  6.2.      Governments also required reliable data to assess whether test, trace and isolate
            measures were working as intended, including the extent of public compliance.
            Although a range of data was collected by all four governments in the UK, problems
            with the quality of data – particularly on levels of adherence to test, trace and isolate
            measures – persisted throughout the Covid-19 pandemic.1

  6.3.      The Inquiry has already considered public health communications in its combined
            Report relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C.2 This chapter considers the approach
            of the UK government and devolved administrations to communications about
            testing, tracing and isolation. Given the breadth of public communications activity
            across the four nations of the UK and the duration of the pandemic, this chapter
            focuses on illustrative examples to highlight key themes and issues that emerged in
            relation to communications about test, trace and isolate measures. It also addresses
            the use of data and monitoring to evaluate test, trace and isolate policy.


  Test, trace and isolate communications across the
  four nations
  6.4.      The UK-wide Coronavirus: Action Plan, published at the start of March 2020,
            explained to the public that testing, tracing and isolation were likely to be introduced
            to slow the transmission of the virus and to protect public health services.3

  6.5.      Two months later, in May 2020, the UK government and devolved administrations
            announced their own test, trace and isolate systems. They were:

            • in England: Test, Trace, Isolate, and later NHS Test and Trace;                         4


            • in Northern Ireland: Test, Trace and Protect;                  5

=== PAGE 197 ===
                                                                            Chapter 6: Communications and data




         • in Scotland: Test and Protect; and         6


         • in Wales: Test, Trace, Protect.            7



         Different terminology across the UK causes confusion for members of the public
         and should be avoided where possible. Greater consideration should be given in
         the future to the use of consistent terminology in a UK-wide emergency.

6.6.     The introduction of different test, trace and isolate schemes across the UK marked
         the start of further targeted public health communications, designed to encourage
         widespread compliance with the measures across the UK. It was essential that these
         communications explained the importance of the test, trace and isolate measures,
         but also set out in clear terms how to follow them in practice.

6.7.     By the time the test, trace and isolate schemes were announced, the public
         had been exposed to a large volume of information about Covid-19, including daily
         press conferences, published guidance and targeted communications campaigns.8
         In contrast to the first lockdown restrictions – which could be communicated through
         simple, straightforward messaging, such as ‘stay at home’ – test, trace and isolate
         measures required people to take more complex actions.

6.8.     As early as January 2020, scientific advisers to the UK government and devolved
         administrations recognised the need to consult with behavioural science experts
         on how to communicate self-isolation measures to the public.9 By March 2020,
         the Scientific Advisory Group for Emergencies (SAGE), supported by advice from
         the Scientific Pandemic Insights Group on Behaviours (SPI-B), advised the UK
         government and devolved administrations that public health messaging must
         communicate the rationale for introducing self-isolation measures, along with
         practical advice on how to comply.10 Professor Lucy Yardley, Professor of Health
         Psychology at the University of Bristol and the University of Southampton and
         Co-Chair of SPI-B from April 2020, told the Inquiry that SPI-B emphasised that
         communications should be based on the assumption that people wanted to work
         collectively to manage the pandemic, but that they needed to be given clear,
         consistent and detailed guidance for how to do so.11

6.9.     Public health communications played a central role in ensuring that guidance
         on testing, tracing and isolation was clear and accessible. However, this role was
         made significantly more challenging by the scale and pace at which information
         about these measures developed. These challenges were particularly pronounced

           during the early months of the pandemic, when testing capacity and contact tracing
           arrangements were being scaled up (see Chapter 3: Contact tracing).12

  6.10.    Later, in September 2020, when both the UK government and Welsh Government
           introduced a legal duty to self-isolate, further complexity was added to the existing
           requirements. In England, the self-isolation regulations were subsequently amended
           nine times, with some amendments implementing multiple changes to the rules.13

  6.11.    However, the complexities do not explain why the content of some public health
           communications regarding test, trace and isolate did not include key information,
           such as the main symptoms of Covid-19, when people should seek a test, how digital
           contact tracing safeguarded personal data, and how practical and financial support
           could be accessed during periods of self-isolation. Effective communication of this
           information was critical to enabling public understanding and to building confidence
           in a system intended to protect individuals and reduce transmission.

  6.12.    As the volume of public health guidance increased over the course of the pandemic,
           the UK government and devolved administrations recognised that unclear or
           incomplete messaging was an important factor in the reduced uptake of test, trace
           and isolate measures and likely contributed to the erosion of public trust during
           the pandemic.14 Some contributors told the Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story
           Matters, that they found the information about contact tracing unclear.


                 “The self-isolation, everyone understands. Testing, everyone understands.
                   The tracing bit, whether it was maybe not explained why it’s important or how
                   it works, or how the technology works, or what are the very clear one, two,
                   three steps you need to take. I think that bit for me, there was a confusion.”15

                 Contributor, Every Story Matters



  Symptom recognition
  6.13.    Symptom recognition as a key trigger for taking a test was being considered by
           SAGE as early as May 2020.16 By November 2020, SPI-B published a detailed paper
           on the importance of symptom recognition in leading people to seek a test for
           Covid-19, in which it found that the interpretation of symptoms:

               “is likely hampered by low levels of knowledge among the UK population that
                 the symptoms to be aware of are cough, fever, loss of sense of taste and loss
                 of smell”.17

6.14.     A number of studies carried out in 2020 and 2021 confirmed that knowledge of
          symptoms plateaued rather than improved over time. For example, the COVID-19
          Rapid Survey of Adherence to Interventions and Responses (CORSAIR) study,
          discussed in Chapter 2: Testing, indicated that, between May and August 2020,
          only 48.9% of respondents living in the UK could identify the three main symptoms
          of Covid-19.18 According to the study:

               “There were initial increases at the start of data collection and when loss of sense
                of smell or taste was introduced into the guidance, but some decline since April/
                early May.”19

6.15.     The percentage of people surveyed between March and August 2020 who could
          name two of the three main symptoms did not exceed 70%.20 Based on polling over
          the same period, SPI-B described recognition of the symptoms of Covid-19 across
          the UK as “sub-optimal”.21

6.16.     A further study, published by the British Medical Journal, concluded that only
          51.5% of participants surveyed between May 2020 and January 2021 could identify
          cough, high temperature or fever and loss of sense of smell or taste as symptoms
          of Covid‑19.22 It proposed that policies providing financial and practical support,
          together with improvements in communication about the testing system, would be
          “key to increasing uptake both in the UK and internationally”.23

6.17.     However, some communications campaigns promoting testing did not include
          any reference to the three key symptoms of the virus. For example, the ‘Let’s get
          back’ campaign (see Figure 8; launched by the Department of Health and Social
          Care and NHS Test and Trace on 30 July 2020) was designed to increase public
          understanding of the role of NHS Test and Trace in England and to encourage

6.19.     The absence of information about the specific symptoms of Covid-19 in public-facing
          communications also extended to the UK government’s press conferences. A study
          published by the Royal Society of Medicine in February 2022, which examined
          the public’s understanding of the symptoms of Covid-19, concluded that the UK
          government’s press conferences, delivered from the early stages of the pandemic
          in March 2020 through to February 2022, “rarely described specific symptoms that
          the public needed to be aware of to seek a test or isolate”.29

6.20. Professor James Rubin, Professor of Psychology and Emerging Health Risks at
      King’s College London and Chair of SPI-B from February 2020 to June 2021, told the
      Inquiry that public communications related to Covid-19 “often assumed that people
      knew” what symptoms to look out for.30

6.21.     Symptom recognition for much of the pandemic was an integral first step to
          enabling people to adhere to the test, trace and isolate system. Professor Madelynne
          Arden (expert witness on adherence to behaviours associated with test, trace and
          isolate) analysed the range of test, trace and isolate communications throughout
          the pandemic and agreed that campaigns should ensure “widespread knowledge
          and understanding about what symptoms to look out for”.31


Contact tracing apps and the protection of privacy
6.22. In the early stages of a potential outbreak of a novel disease, contact tracing has
      significant potential to reduce transmission, particularly when used alongside other
      public health measures such as mass testing.32 During the first year of the pandemic,
      three digital contact tracing apps were launched in the UK.

6.25. In Scotland, the communications campaign for the Protect Scotland app sought
      to address users’ concerns about data security and the provision of personal
      information by using language such as ‘private’ and ‘anonymous’. It also emphasised
      that the app was ‘easy’ to use.37 A televised campaign told users how to use the app
      and confirmed that it was “private and anonymous”.38

6.26. In Northern Ireland, an announcement by the Department of Health (Northern
      Ireland) about the launch of the StopCOVID NI app said that the app used
      anonymised information to safeguard privacy.39 The announcement also emphasised
      the app’s ease of use (“It will only take a few short minutes to download and
      activate”) and its utility in fighting Covid-19 (“This could be the most important thing
      you do all year”).40

6.27.     As discussed in Chapter 3: Contact tracing, privacy concerns were considered
          during the design and development stage of all three apps. However, this did not
          in itself guarantee public confidence in the security of personal data or in how that
          data would be used. A number of people across the UK expressed mistrust about
          the privacy protection offered by the apps, did not understand how they operated
          and doubted their effectiveness.41


               “[A] lot of people I know either didn’t download it or removed it from their
                 smart phones because they thought the government was monitoring their
                 movements and there was some issue with the app development.”42

                Contributor, Every Story Matters


          The NHS COVID-19 app campaign did not explain to members of the public how
          the app would protect their loved ones or how it would safeguard users’ privacy.
          While the campaigns in Scotland and Northern Ireland did refer to the use of
          anonymised data to protect privacy, they did not provide detailed explanations
          of how users’ privacy was protected in practice.

6.28. These issues were recognised by the Northern Ireland Human Rights Commission,
      which stated that privacy issues relating to the app should be “set out in a clear,
      accessible and succinct way for those individuals who download the app”.43

           The Scottish Government’s Equality and Fairer Scotland Duty Impact Assessment,
           published on 17 March 2021, also found that public health messaging and guidance
           must be accessible for people who have lower levels of digital literacy.44

  6.29. Public communications about any digital tool should include a full explanation about
        how privacy and confidentiality concerns are addressed. Low levels of uptake of the
        apps suggest that more could have been done by the Department of Health and
        Social Care (NHS Test and Trace), the Scottish Government (health and social care
        directorates) and the Department of Health (Northern Ireland) to build trust through
        communications.

  6.30. Launch campaigns can provide an important opportunity to engage the public
        and explain the purpose and safeguards of a new digital tool. However, a single
        campaign may not be sufficient to establish or sustain public confidence, particularly
        where concerns about data and privacy are likely to evolve over time. Decision-
        makers must continue to monitor privacy concerns and develop measures to build
        and sustain public trust and confidence in the ongoing use of personal data.45
        The public needs clear information about what personal data are collected,
        how they are used, who has access to them and for what purpose.

  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




  Inclusive approaches to test, trace and isolate messages
  6.35. Where governments seek to implement widespread restrictions on behaviour as
        a means of reducing the transmission of a virus and protecting the health of the
        population, they need to ensure that their instructions for how to comply with those
        restrictions are received, understood and trusted by all sections of the population.52

  6.36. At a SAGE meeting on 23 July 2020, SPI-B recommended that communication about
        risk that was “culturally appropriate” might promote increased uptake of test, trace
        and isolate measures.53

  6.37.    Despite this advice, there were blind spots. As described by James Bethell, Lord
           Bethell (Parliamentary Under Secretary of State for Technology, Innovation and
           Life Sciences at the Department of Health and Social Care from March 2020 to
           September 2021), there were:

                “communities, ethnic groups, geographies and certain areas of poverty – where,
                 really, government on the whole, and particularly the health service, has very,
                 very little trust or reach”.54

  6.38. Lord Bethell also explained that feedback from roundtables with communities
        consistently indicated that people who were most likely to be adversely affected
        by the pandemic did not trust the NHS and that they “weren’t really hearing the
        message”.55

  6.39. Feedback from a series of ‘discovery exercises’ carried out by the NHS Test and
        Trace Strategy Unit in June 2020, with members of different population groups,
        also identified distrust in the UK government, the need to use trusted sources to
        communicate guidance on test, trace and isolate, and the need for greater diversity
        when delivering communications.56 In response, the UK government sought to
        improve its public health communications to increase engagement with test, trace
        and isolate within ethnic minority communities – including through the development
        of a cross-government communications strategy intended to reach the whole
        population.57

  6.40. By July 2020, the cross-government Covid-19 communications and engagement
        activity (which was concerned with communicating Covid-19 messaging and
        guidance to different communities) had directed a number of resources towards

         key population groups, including young people (18 to 24 years old), young workers,
         older people, single mothers with young children and people from Black, Asian and
         other ethnic minority groups. The strategy also focused on ‘at risk’ regions, including
         the north-east, the Midlands and the north-west. Communications approaches
         included press, radio, digital and social media messaging, and tailored messaging
         delivered in multiple languages.58

6.41.    Despite these efforts, some witnesses felt that the Department of Health and Social
         Care’s communications on test, trace and isolate were not always appropriately
         tailored to, or accessible for, all population groups.59

6.42. Ade Adeyemi (on behalf of the Federation of Ethnic Minority Healthcare
      Organisations) said that there was a “lack of culturally competent approaches and
      trust-building measures” in the UK government’s test, trace and isolate messaging.60
      Mr Adeyemi explained that members of the federation relied on their professional
      skills and personal resources to assist in the reach of messaging across ethnic
      minority groups.61 Online discussions and webinars were arranged to bridge the gap
      in understanding, despite limited resources and the personal and financial cost.62
      According to Mr Adeyemi, the absence of meaningful engagement and culturally
      appropriate messaging “significantly hampered participation” in testing, tracing
      and isolation.63

6.43. Professor Kevin Fenton (President of the UK Faculty of Public Health from July
      2022) expressed a similar view. As the pandemic had a disproportionate impact
      on Black, Asian and ethnic minority groups, community-centred approaches were
      required to build trust and deeper engagement.64 According to Professor Fenton,
      local authorities and voluntary and community sector partners were best placed
      to manage these concerns.65

6.44. However, during the early months of the pandemic, the Department of Health
      and Social Care’s approach to test, trace and isolate did not adequately take into
      account the role of local authorities and directors of public health in supporting the
      communication of the NHS Test and Trace system.

6.45. Greg Fell (President from November 2023 of the Association of Directors of Public
      Health, the representative body for directors of public health across the UK) told the
      Inquiry that the lack of early engagement with local authorities during the design
      and implementation of the test, trace and isolate programme in England resulted in
      avoidable delays in protecting communities. Mr Fell emphasised that these issues

           could have been mitigated if local knowledge and expertise had been integrated into
           the planning process earlier.66

  6.46. The devolved administrations engaged with local authorities and local communities
        at an earlier stage in the pandemic, in order to build trust in communications.
        For example, in June 2020, the Welsh Government developed a communications
        strategy for its Test, Trace, Protect system, which included targeted communications
        for Black, Asian and ethnic minority communities that were identified as needing
        additional support.67 The Scottish Government also worked with local partners
        to develop communications on test, trace and isolate measures, including a
        ‘self-isolation fact sheet’, which was translated into 26 languages.68 During the
        development of its contact tracing model, the Public Health Agency (Northern
        Ireland) carried out a wide-ranging programme of engagement with representatives
        from population groups, including the Northern Ireland Commissioner for Children
        and Young People, the Commissioner for Older People for Northern Ireland and
        representatives from the homeless population and the Roma community. This
        identified the need to assist further with adherence to the test, trace and isolate
        guidance and the importance of effective communications targeted to specific
        vulnerable groups.69

  6.47.    In any future pandemic, it will be essential that public health messaging is inclusive,
           culturally sensitive and tailored to the diverse needs of different population groups.70


  Inconsistent messages
  6.48. The UK government and devolved administrations received regular advice on
        the importance of monitoring and gathering feedback on test, trace and isolate
        interventions.71 Some mechanisms for monitoring the effectiveness of test, trace
        and isolate communications were set up. For example, the Scottish Government
        established a Covid-19 campaign tracker in April 2020, designed to evaluate the
        performance of marketing campaigns.72 Public Health Wales also attempted to
        monitor and evaluate the effect of its communications, but noted that the rapidly
        changing policy context meant that measuring the impact of its communications
        was difficult.73 Overall, there was a lack of reliable, high-quality data on levels of
        adherence to the test, trace and isolate systems across the UK. This made it difficult
        for governments to assess how effectively communications were driving behaviour.74

6.49. Differences between the test, trace and isolate communications campaigns across
      the UK contributed to public confusion.75 This was particularly the case for people
      who were exposed to UK-wide media or social media and for people who moved
      between nations of the UK.76


               “I think you have to have a standard over the UK. You can’t have something
                 different in [each UK country]. When you live so close to a border, you could
                 be working in England and living in Wales, or vice versa, and you’ve got two
                 different guidelines.”77

                Contributor, Every Story Matters


6.50. Concerns that inconsistencies in public health messaging had the capacity to
      confuse the public were also raised by the devolved administrations. Michelle
      O’Neill MLA (First Minister of Northern Ireland from February 2024 and deputy
      First Minister of Northern Ireland from January 2020 to February 2022) told the
      Inquiry that different approaches across different administrations “hampered the
      Executive’s ability to provide clear and consistent messages to the public”.78 The
      Welsh Local Government Association told the Inquiry that the public in Wales had the
      tendency to be “confused by separate communications on test, trace and protect/
      isolate programmes from UK and Welsh Governments” as the announcements and
      communications from the UK government had “far greater profile and traction”
      across the media.79 According to Dr Chris Llewelyn (Chief Executive of the Welsh
      Local Government Association from January 2019), the conflicting messages for
      Wales and England also impacted on public confidence in the wider Test, Trace,
      Protect system in Wales.80

6.51.     Public health communications play a critical role as tools for encouraging behaviour
          change, which in turn influence overall levels of compliance with public health
          measures.81 The extent to which people engaged with the various test, trace and
          isolate measures across the UK therefore depended significantly on how clearly,
          consistently and persuasively the measures were communicated.82

6.52. The Inquiry is therefore recommending the alignment of test, trace and isolate
      communications in any future pandemic, based on shared principles. While decision-
      making, funding and delivery will rightly remain distinct under the devolution

  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




           arrangements, this should not prevent collaboration between the UK government
           and devolved administrations or a more coordinated approach to how policies are
           presented to the public.83

  6.53. Communications campaigns should explain that test, trace and isolate operates as
        an interconnected system. They should set out the rationale for these measures as
        critical interventions in responding to a pandemic, explain how compliance benefits
        the public and clearly signpost to the support available.84 The campaigns should
        be developed with appropriate input from behavioural scientists and public health
        experts, taking into account public understanding of what is required in a global
        emergency as well as a person’s capacity, opportunity and motivation to comply.85
        In essence, test, trace and isolate communications should not assume that “‘telling
        people what to do’ is sufficient”.86

  6.54. Once developed, communications related to test, trace and isolate should be
        tested through UK-wide pandemic response exercises. Mechanisms should also
        be established to monitor and evaluate their effectiveness and impact. Where such
        measures form a central component of the response to a future pandemic, it will be
        critical that communications are as clear and effective as possible.


           Recommendation 7: Aligned test, trace and isolate communications
           The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
           Ireland Executive should develop aligned communications to support adherence
           to test, trace and isolate measures to control disease spread in a public health
           emergency.
           The four nations of the UK should coordinate as far as possible while considering
           any region-specific challenges or differences in approach to test, trace and isolate.
           Communications should be informed by behavioural science principles and
           tested as part of UK-wide pandemic response exercises (as recommended in the
           Inquiry’s Module 1 Report). The effectiveness of communications during a public
           health emergency should be monitored.



  Data and evaluation
  6.55. The Covid-19 pandemic highlighted the critical role of data and modelling in
        informing the policy and delivery of test, trace and isolate. A novel pathogen is,
        by its nature, unfamiliar. The collection and analysis of data – including infection
        rates and levels of adherence to measures such as test, trace and isolate – are
        therefore essential.

  86   INQ000587239_0113 para 382

=== PAGE 211 ===
                                                                             Chapter 6: Communications and data




6.56. From the earliest stages of the response to Covid-19, SAGE and its sub-groups relied
      on epidemiological models, surveillance data and forecasting tools to understand
      transmission dynamics, assess the risks posed by the virus and guide public health
      interventions.87 As the pandemic progressed, the devolved administrations also
      established dedicated teams to provide a localised focus on the modelling and
      epidemiology of the virus.88

6.57.    However, this work was constrained by the quality and timeliness of the underlying
         data. In the early phases of the pandemic, limited testing capacity, inconsistent
         reporting practices, data lags and gaps in population-level information introduced
         further significant uncertainty into inherently uncertain model outputs.89

6.58. Boris Johnson (Prime Minister from July 2019 to September 2022) agreed that
      collection and use of data should be improved:

              “We need to have much better data and more of it, as well as a better
                understanding of that data – about our healthcare system and about the
                vulnerabilities of the population. We need to be much better at sharing the
                available data across government.”90

         Paul Givan MLA (First Minister of Northern Ireland from June 2021 to February 2022)
         also told the Inquiry that he had concerns that there were insufficient data and
         modelling informing the impact of measures being introduced to contain the spread
         of the virus, particularly on the economy, education and the consequential effects
         on people’s health and wellbeing.91

6.59. There were clearly specific limitations in the data available in relation to adherence to
      the test, trace and isolate measures. On 20 March 2020, SAGE commissioned SPI-B
      to review levels of adherence to behavioural and social interventions and the options
      to increase adherence.92 In that review, SPI-B concluded that it did not know the
      levels of adherence among children, adherence to household isolation or adherence
      to shielding. It noted that the data reviewed “were not ideal”.93 These limitations
      arose for three reasons. Firstly, the data were derived from participants who had
      signed up to take part in surveys and were paid for doing so. Secondly, the response

               options did not reflect changes to household isolation policy.94 Thirdly, self-reported
               behaviour may not accurately reflect actual behaviour, as people may seek to

      6.60. By the summer of 2020, the Scientific Pandemic Influenza Group on Modelling,
            Operational sub-group (SPI-M-O) had identified that the evaluation of the impact of
            test, trace and isolate had not been fully considered at its “design stage”.96 Professor
            Graham Medley (Co‑Chair of SPI-M-O from January 2020 to February 2022)
            told the Inquiry that this “will have hampered the improvement of the system”.97
            The quality of adherence data remained inadequate towards the end of 2020.
            On 4 November 2020, SPI-B noted at the beginning of its analysis of self-isolation
            adherence: “[C]urrently we do not have good quality data re: rates of adherence
            to isolation.”98

      6.61.    Problems with a lack of quality data persisted into 2021. On 11 March 2021, just over
               a year after the pandemic hit the UK, SAGE noted that there were no published data
               which would allow the Scientific Pandemic Influenza Group on Modelling (SPI-M) or
               other relevant sub-groups to assess the impact of test, trace and isolate systems on
               wider transmission.99

      6.62. High-quality data are essential to understanding why people comply with public
            health guidance or fail to comply with it, and how adherence can be improved.
            The Inquiry heard evidence from a number of witnesses about the lack of high-
            quality data, fragmented data systems and the lack of monitoring and evaluation
            of test, trace and isolate.

      6.63. The British Medical Association told the Inquiry that the NHS Test and Trace system
            was not well integrated within the NHS and local public health infrastructure due
            to the UK government’s reliance on private contracts.100 This fragmentation led
            to delays in delivering test results and significant amounts of testing data going
            missing, further compromising the system’s effectiveness.101

      6.64. Professor David Halpern, Chief Executive Officer of the Cabinet Office Behavioural
            Insights Team from 2014, said that test, trace and isolate was a “glaring example”
            of a recurring failure by government to develop systems designed to “test,
            learn and adapt” or to incorporate basic impact evaluation into the design

         of operational and delivery systems and in policy.102 As a result, the monitoring and
         evaluation of test, trace and isolate measures were constrained by inadequate data.
         Andrew Garrett, President of the Royal Statistical Society, told the Inquiry:

             “[T]here was a missed opportunity for statistical methods and data to have been
               used in the TTI [test, trace and isolate] programme to gain information about the

         of any infection control operation” – such as learning about who was infected, how
         early they became positive and the symptoms they exhibited.104

6.65. The proper collection and analysis of high-quality data should therefore be central
      to any future pandemic response involving testing, tracing and isolation. The Inquiry
      agrees that systems for monitoring and evaluating the impact and effectiveness of
      NHS Test and Trace should have been considered by the Department of Health and
      Social Care at the outset. Without these systems, there were limited ways in which
      the UK government could analyse and understand the impact of its test, trace and
      isolate policies on different population groups and to make the changes needed to
      improve adherence and uptake.

6.66. Since the Covid-19 pandemic, the devolved administrations in Wales and Scotland
      have taken important steps to improve the use of data during a pandemic, as well as
      pandemic preparedness generally. Dr Rob Orford (Chief Scientific Adviser (Health)
      for Wales from January 2017 to April 2024) told the Inquiry that Wales is undertaking
      work to improve access to reliable data for civil emergencies, including pandemics.
      This includes improving how data are collected, analysed and shared in response to
      both operational and pandemic emergencies.105 In Scotland, the Standing Committee
      on Pandemic Preparedness has been established to advise the Scottish Government
      on future pandemic risks.106 Its remit includes providing advice on the strategic
      approach to identifying, accessing and using data to understand future pandemics.107
      It has been meeting regularly since 19 August 2021.108

6.67.    In March 2026, the UK government said that it would review the data, analysis
         and modelling capabilities needed across health and other areas of government to
         support decision-making and evaluate the impact of measures. This work will include
         workstreams on public health and social measures, which will use “new approaches

      6.68. These initiatives represent positive steps. All four governments of the UK should
            improve data collection and analysis in relation to testing, tracing and isolation. As
            health is a devolved (or, in Northern Ireland, ‘transferred’) matter in the UK, alignment
            on data and technology requirements between the four nations is also essential.110

            develop a digital data presentation dashboard to support government decision-

            regularly updated data about test, trace and isolate measures.

      6.69. There should be improved systems for the collection of both qualitative and
            quantitative data on public awareness of test, trace and isolate measures, levels

            support the development of more-targeted interventions and communications.113

      6.70. Data collection and analysis should form an integral part of any future test, trace
            and isolate system from the earliest stages of implementation and throughout

            of international good practice as examined in Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to
            implement a test, trace and isolate system, should also inform the development
            of a future test, trace and isolate system.


               Recommendation 8: Improved data collection and evaluation of test,
               trace and isolate measures
               The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
               Ireland Executive should collect robust data to monitor and evaluate test, trace
               and isolate measures introduced during a future pandemic.
               Qualitative and quantitative data relating to test, trace and isolate measures
               should be collected on public awareness, adherence and the impact of those
               measures on the public.
               The data collected should allow governments to assess differing impacts across
               groups to improve test, trace and isolate measures.
               Data collection should be aligned as far as possible across the four nations of the
               UK and should be shared within and across governments.

=== PAGE 215 ===
Chapter 7: An effective future
test, trace and isolate system

=== PAGE 216 ===
  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




  Introduction
  7.1.     At the start of the Covid-19 pandemic, the World Health Organization stated that
           “isolating, testing and treating every suspected case, and tracing every contact,
           must be the backbone of the response in every country. This is the best hope of
           preventing community transmission.”1 As the Director General of the World Health
           Organization explained: “You can’t fight a virus if you don’t know where it is. Find,
           isolate, test and treat every case to break the chains of transmission.”2

  7.2.     When cases of Covid-19 reached the UK, testing to identify infected individuals and
           isolating positive cases to break the chain of transmission quickly became the UK’s
           primary defence against the spread of the virus. However, as cases began to rise
           sharply from February 2020 and the urgency of responding to the threat of Covid-19
           intensified, the UK’s test, trace and isolate capabilities could not be scaled up at the
           point when they were most needed.3 The governments of the UK could not monitor
           effectively the spread of the disease or ensure that those infected – or their close
           contacts – self-isolated. Consequently, early attempts to contain the virus were
           doomed to fail, and the virus spread to an unknown extent.

  7.3.     This chapter examines the overall effectiveness of the UK’s test, trace and isolate
           system. It considers both its strengths and its failures, highlighting where the system
           proved resilient and where significant weaknesses emerged. It recommends a UK-
           wide accessible and adaptable test, trace and isolate system in order to avoid, as
           Professor Sir Christopher Whitty (Chief Medical Officer for England from October
           2019) described, the inability to scale up this system being “a weakness for future
           pandemics and other health emergencies on this scale”.4


  The lessons from Covid-19 for test, trace and isolate
  7.4.     The costs of developing and delivering functioning and scalable test, trace and
           isolate systems across the UK were unprecedented. Such systems had to be
           constructed from the ground up, at speed, and under conditions that no plan
           had fully anticipated. This was always going to come at a significant cost, but the
           amount spent was, by any measure, extraordinary. For example, in England, the
           UK government spent in excess of £25 billion on NHS Test and Trace during the
           pandemic.5 The cost must have been significantly greater than it would have been
           had there been better planning.

  covid-19---18-march-2020; INQ000587312)

                                              Chapter 7: An effective future test, trace and isolate system




7.5.     The Inquiry has identified a number of weaknesses and some strengths in the test,
         trace and isolate systems developed during the Covid-19 pandemic.


Lack of planning
7.6.     Pandemics, like other major emergencies, rarely unfold in a single predictable way.
         Pandemic planning is more than just considering the science of how a virus spreads
         and what measures are needed to fight it.

7.7.     As the Inquiry concluded in its Module 1 Report, the UK prepared for the wrong
         pandemic. The UK government’s sole pandemic strategy prior to the onset of
         Covid-19, the UK Influenza Pandemic Preparedness Strategy 2011, did not address
         adequately how the spread of a novel virus could be disrupted or prevented in the
         absence of clinical measures such as therapeutics and vaccines. Non-pharmaceutical
         measures such as testing and contact tracing were not considered relevant for a
         pandemic influenza.6

7.8.     The March 2020 Coronavirus: Action Plan – intended to be “a coronavirus version”
         of the UK Influenza Pandemic Preparedness Strategy 2011 – was commissioned
         as a public-facing document setting out what the UK government and devolved
         administrations planned to do in response to the virus.7 Although it sought to
         reassure the public that there were “tried and tested procedures for rapid tracing,
         monitoring and isolation of close contacts” used by public health specialists across
         the UK, there was no plan for a test, trace and isolate system at the scale necessary
         in a pandemic.8 The lack of planning had several consequences, as noted in earlier
         chapters of this Report.

7.9.     Existing private and academic laboratories were a valuable but under-utilised
         resource in the early months of the pandemic. Their services could have been better
         used to fill critical gaps in testing capacity while a larger, centralised system was
         being developed.

7.10.    There was no strategy for international border control measures – a first and obvious
         line of defence during the early stages of a novel pandemic. Some countries and
         territories such as Singapore and Taiwan (later independently assessed as having
         responded well to the pandemic) put in place border controls far earlier than the
         UK and had far greater success in controlling the virus. Widespread border control
         measures were not in place in the UK until June 2020. Border control measures
         short of full border closure should have been implemented at an earlier stage of the
         pandemic.

public-inquiry.uk/documents/module-1-full-report)

=== PAGE 218 ===
      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      7.11.    Systems had to be developed in the middle of a crisis. While some aspects of the
               test, trace and isolate systems proved effective, particularly where there was strong
               coordination across the four nations of the UK, other elements were affected by
               delays or were hampered by an ongoing lack of capacity and infrastructure. Policy-
               makers and organisations were also operating with limited information and under
               significant pressure.9

      7.12.    The need for urgent action meant that there was insufficient time to consider fully
               how each test, trace and isolate measure would interact with other public health
               measures, such as social distancing and national lockdowns. Ministers had not given
               themselves adequate opportunity to assess the complexity of balancing public
               health benefits with societal and economic costs.


      Pausing community testing and tracing
      7.13.    Most importantly, the lack of planning resulted in the decision by the UK government
               and the devolved administrations to pause community testing and contact tracing in
               March 2020. They did not have sufficient diagnostic capacity just when numbers of
               infections were rising exponentially (see Chapter 2: Testing).

      7.14.    This weakened the ability of the UK government and devolved administrations to
               monitor the spread of the virus.10 Without widespread community testing and contact
               tracing, the UK lacked essential information needed to manage the response to
               Covid-19, including data on the number of cases, routes of transmission and the
               range of symptoms.11 This likely limited the effectiveness of the overall response.


      Support for isolation
      7.15.    Although the UK government and devolved administrations acted quickly to
               introduce financial support measures at the start of the pandemic (being considered
               in Module 9: Economic response), it should have been foreseen that not everyone
               required to self-isolate would be eligible for this support. This recognition came
               too late. It took several months before England, Scotland and Wales introduced
               dedicated payment schemes to help people comply with self-isolation. Only
               Northern Ireland established a support payment shortly after the first widespread
               self-isolation measures were announced.


      Inadequate data
      7.16.    The inadequacy of available data across the UK – from infection rates to levels of
               adherence to self-isolation – is a recurring theme in this Inquiry.

7.17.     Several data gaps were also identified in the test, trace and isolate system in England
          that raised concerns about the effectiveness of the system and the justification for
          the substantial public spending allocated to it. The lack of good-quality, reliable
          data on various aspects of the test, trace and isolate system inevitably created the
          conditions for poor decision-making (see Chapter 6: Communications and data).


Inconsistent messaging
7.18.     The UK’s test, trace and isolate response did not speak with one voice.
          Divergence between the four governments of the UK generated a large volume of
          communications, and consequently public messaging and campaigns about the
          importance of testing and tracing were inconsistent. Even when their approaches
          were aligned, the terminology for the system in each country was different. It was
          the right of each government to choose its own terminology but it caused confusion
          across the UK and undermined compliance with the guidance and regulations.


Lack of accessibility
7.19.     Contributors told the Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story Matters, about
          problems with the accessibility of the test, trace and isolate system for people with
          physical disabilities and those who were digitally excluded.


               “[The test centre] had loads of stairs and really narrow cubicles. [A wheelchair
                 user] wouldn’t even be able to sit in there. And then the next one available
                 to me was up near the airport, and that was a drive-through, but that’s an
                 hour-and-a-half journey [away].”12

                Contributor, Every Story Matters



               “Older people […] really struggled with the technical side of […] booking their
                 PCR [polymerase chain reaction] test […] we often had older patients phoning
                 in asking us questions about booking […] nobody took that into consideration
                 that a lot of elderly people, they don’t have mobile phones.”13

                Contributor, Every Story Matters


7.20.     The UK government initially prioritised the rapid establishment of a basic testing
          system, only considering at a later stage how certain groups would be able to access
          it (see Chapter 2: Testing).14

      7.21.     The Inquiry also heard evidence about the accessibility of the contact tracing app
                used in England and Wales (see Chapter 3: Contact tracing). Although the app was
                available in multiple languages, there was evidence that language remained a barrier
                for some users.15 Those who were unable or unwilling to use mobile phone apps
                were unable to benefit from digital contact tracing.


      Lack of trust
      7.22.     People will not comply with test, trace and isolate measures if they do not trust
                the government or the government department trying to encourage them to do
                so. Contributors to Every Story Matters described a lack of trust in what were
                fundamental components of the UK’s test, trace and isolate systems.


                     “The initial ones [tests] were really uncomfortable … but I think it was more
                       a total distrust of the system [… and] of the NHS generally, this element
                       of people who just thought, ‘No, I don’t trust this. I’m not going to [test].’”16

                      Community member who supported people to self-isolate, Every Story
                      Matters

                       and produce the associated hysteria […] I did not have any test (as a matter
                       of principle).”17

                      Contributor, Every Story Matters


      7.23.     Public trust in the UK’s Covid-19 response was also affected by its lack of
                preparedness. One study found that the UK’s lack of readiness for mass testing
                contributed to a perception of government incompetence.18

      7.24.     While the UK’s test, trace and isolate response included commendable efforts to
                address privacy concerns and engage diverse communities – such as targeted
                initiatives in Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland – significant mistrust and
                perceptions of government incompetence persisted.19 This will require considerable
                work with the communities themselves to change minds.

An aligned approach
7.25.     Announced in April 2020, the National Testing Programme operated throughout
          the pandemic and involved all four governments working together to deliver
          Covid-19 testing.20 This included efforts to expand laboratory capacity and sampling
          infrastructure, develop novel testing technologies and procure tests.21 As set out
          in Chapter 2: Testing, it included the creation of the Lighthouse laboratories, a
          network of dedicated Covid-19 diagnostic facilities established across the UK to
          process Covid-19 tests at scale.22 Within six months, they were processing more than
          1 million tests per week.23 Professor Dame Anna Dominiczak (Director of Laboratories
          at the Department of Health and Social Care from August 2020 to June 2022)
          characterised this collective effort as “absolutely incredible”.24 This achievement
          was the result of extensive collaboration between the four governments, volunteers
          and private sector organisations.

7.26.     Robin Swann (Minister of Health for Northern Ireland from January 2020 to
          October 2022 and from February to May 2024) told the Inquiry that Northern
          Ireland’s membership of the National Testing Programme “was key to our being
          able to expand the testing capacity the way in which we did and I do not consider
          that we could have done so as quickly on our own”.25

7.27.     Against a backdrop of several significant weaknesses, the National Testing
          Programme demonstrated what was achievable when the four governments
          worked together towards a common goal.


Developing a UK-wide system for the future
7.28.     Despite initial challenges, by the summer of 2020, the test, trace and isolate systems
          were producing and delivering testing and tracing at the scale required.

7.29.     According to modelling estimates produced by NHS Test and Trace (the Canna
          model), the NHS Test and Trace system may have prevented between 1.2 and
          2 million secondary Covid-19 infections in England between June 2020 and April
          2021.26 While these figures are subject to the inherent uncertainties associated with
          statistical modelling and were set against a hypothetical scenario in which it was
          assumed that individuals with Covid-19-like symptoms would self-isolate together
          with their household contacts, the Canna model suggests the extent of the impact

      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




                that an effective test, trace and isolate system may have during a pandemic
                response.27

      7.30.     However, James Bethell, Lord Bethell (Parliamentary Under Secretary of State
                for Technology, Innovation and Life Sciences at the Department of Health and
                Social Care from March 2020 to September 2021) told the Inquiry that “we have
                gone backwards” since Covid-19, with diagnostic and public health infrastructure
                dismantled and “weaker than ever”.28 If correct, this puts lives at risk.

      7.31.     The lessons from Module 7 are clear: many of the flaws in the UK’s test, trace and
                isolate system stemmed from a lack of pre-planning and preparedness. Weaknesses
                in these systems ultimately came at a high cost, with many individuals unable to
                access or comply with the very measures designed to protect them.

      7.32.     There are also valuable lessons to be learned from countries and territories such
                as Taiwan, Singapore, Australia and Germany, which had plans in place at the start
                of the pandemic and were widely praised for their effectiveness in controlling the
                spread of Covid-19 when it first struck. The Inquiry recognises that a response that is
                appropriate for one country or territory may not be appropriate for another and that
                an overall appraisal of the response of a country or territory may not be as positive.
                However, as set out in Chapter 1: The UK’s readiness to implement a test, trace and
                isolate system, they drew on lessons from previous public health emergencies to
                implement proactive and comprehensive test, trace and isolate systems from an
                early stage. For example, Taiwan took significant action following the severe acute
                respiratory syndrome (SARS) outbreak in 2003, such as conducting regular audits
                of hospitals to ensure that all necessary infection control measures were in place.29
                By April 2021, it had among the lowest per capita incidence and mortality rates
                of Covid-19 in the world.30 Understanding why – what they did differently, what
                structures they had in place and what decisions they made – should form part of the
                UK’s plans for implementing a test, trace and isolate system for a future pandemic.


      Expert advice
      7.33.     Future plans should also better harness expert advice and expertise. For example,
                behavioural science can help governments understand practical and financial
                barriers to adherence to self-isolation requirements and inform measures to address
                them (see Chapter 4: Isolation).31 It might also help to commission qualitative and

      assumptions used in the models and are subject to uncertainty.

         comparative studies to understand challenges in implementation, to establish
         support mechanisms and to prepare for multiple scenarios. While the March
         2026 Pandemic Preparedness Strategy: Building Our Capabilities suggests that
         governments will capitalise on existing scientific advisory structures and industry
         expertise, governments must consult a diverse range of disciplines and effectively
         integrate this advice into decision-making.32


Adaptability
7.34.    The Inquiry’s combined Report relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C identified
         the flaws in the UK’s ‘ready for flu, ready for anything’ approach to pandemic
         preparedness. The next pandemic may well present a different scenario from
         Covid‑19.33 Planning for a future test, trace and isolate system must take into account
         the unpredictable nature of pandemic pathogens.

7.35.    Strategies must first account for potential delays in test development and what
         support may be required for periods of self-isolation while a suitable test is being
         developed. When testing is available, its deployment will need to adapt to the
         type of pathogen and the extent of infection spread. During the early stages of
         an outbreak, there may be a need for focused testing of symptomatic people to
         contain and prevent widespread transmission. Where infection does not always
         result in symptoms, mass testing of the population may be required to identify both
         symptomatic and asymptomatic infections. If individuals – for example, care home
         residents – are particularly vulnerable to a novel pathogen, regular testing of staff
         and residents will be necessary to reduce transmission to people most at risk of
         severe outcomes.34

7.36.    The UK must also be adaptable in its approach to contact tracing. A 2021 study
         of contact tracing during the Covid-19 pandemic found that infection control was
         improved when both manual and digital methods were used together, as compared
         to digital tracing alone.35 A future test, trace and isolate system should integrate
         manual and digital methods, utilising both backward and forward contact tracing,
         and take account of technological developments (see Chapter 3: Contact tracing).
         Future digital contact tracing tools, potentially used alongside wearable devices,
         may be able to detect early symptoms and indicate infection, providing an alternative
         to testing to confirm infection.36

=== PAGE 224 ===
      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      7.37.    For the system to work as it should, the UK government and devolved
               administrations must put in place mechanisms to monitor and evaluate the
               effectiveness and impact of any future test, trace and isolate system, and ensure that
               there is better integration between statistical and policy functions.37 This will enable
               decision-makers to better understand virus transmission or levels of adherence to
               measures such as self-isolation, and adapt accordingly.38

      7.38.    As Sir Matthew Rycroft (Permanent Secretary to the Home Office from March 2020
               to March 2025) emphasised, agility in policy, legislation and digital solutions is vital:

                    “There should be greater resilience and agility in our structures, policy, legislation,
                     digital solutions, and public communications to respond to future threats and
                     support the needs of everyone in society when implementing test, trace, and
                     isolate strategies.”39

               Embedding flexibility into preparedness frameworks for a UK-wide test, trace
               and isolate system will be essential to ensuring that responses remain effective
               in dynamic and uncertain situations.


      Accessibility
      7.39.    Test, trace and isolate systems must be informed by the realities of the lives of the
               people they are designed to protect.

      7.40.    Future test, trace and isolate systems must prioritise accessibility by embedding
               sustained engagement with vulnerable groups into the planning phase. The Scottish
               Government’s proactive work with the Expert Reference Group on COVID-19 and
               Ethnicity serves as a model for identifying barriers to compliance within migrant
               and ethnic minority communities.40

      7.41.    The UK government and devolved administrations must apply these lessons to
               ensure that future pandemic responses account for shifting vulnerabilities across
               different populations.


      Building trust
      7.42.    Test, trace and isolate measures are essential pandemic tools – particularly
               when vaccines are unavailable or when public concerns about vaccines lead




      37   INQ000475156_0013 para 3.2.6
      38   INQ000475156_0013 para 3.2.6
      39   INQ000587248_0058 para 223
      40   INQ000475071_0106-0108, 0124-0125 paras 314, 370-373


210

=== PAGE 225 ===
                                                   Chapter 7: An effective future test, trace and isolate system




          to reduced uptake – yet their success depends heavily on clear communication
          and public trust.41

7.43.     Studies have repeatedly identified trust as a key factor in public compliance during a
          pandemic.42 As one study concluded: “In a crisis, almost-instant choices about who
          to trust or distrust could make a difference between life and death.”43

7.44.     To ensure compliance in future pandemics, the UK government and devolved
          administrations must prioritise building trust from the outset. This requires
          developing comprehensive pre-pandemic plans, addressing specific privacy
          concerns regarding contact tracing apps, and delivering accessible, culturally tailored
          communications through trusted sources.

7.45.     To protect public health in a future pandemic, the four governments of the UK must
          act decisively and plan for a robust, adaptable, accessible and – most importantly
          – scalable test, trace and isolate system. The Inquiry is therefore recommending
          the development of a UK-wide test, trace and isolate system in advance of the
          next pandemic. It will be fundamental to protecting public health and saving lives.
          One system would ensure greater clarity and consistency for the public, inspire
          confidence and ensure greater compliance. As the National Testing Programme
          showed, when the four governments work successfully together, the people they
          serve – the UK public – benefit.


          Recommendation 9: A new UK-wide test, trace and isolate system
          The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
          Ireland Executive should work together to develop one system to find, test, trace,
          isolate and support cases of infection during a public health emergency.
          The system should be accessible to all population groups and adaptable
          to account for a range of pathogen types and the different stages of response
          to a public health emergency. It should be developed in consultation with local
          authorities, health boards and trusts, as well as experts in modelling, behavioural
          science and public health.
          This should form part of the civil emergency strategy recommended in the
          Inquiry’s Module 1 Report and the system should be tested during UK-wide
          pandemic response exercises.
