=== PAGE 227 ===
                          Appendix 1: The background to this module and the Inquiry’s methodology




Background
A1.1.     The Right Honourable Boris Johnson MP, Prime Minister from July 2019 to
          September 2022, formally established the UK Covid-19 Inquiry in June 2022 to
          examine the preparations for and response to the Covid-19 pandemic in the UK
          and to learn lessons for the future. In December 2021, he appointed The Right
          Honourable the Baroness Hallett DBE, a retired judge of the Court of Appeal,
          as its Chair.

A1.2.     On 28 June 2022, the Prime Minister issued the final Terms of Reference for the
          Inquiry, establishing it under the Inquiries Act 2005.1 The Inquiry formally opened
          on 21 July 2022 to:

               “examine, consider and report on preparations and the response to the
                pandemic in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland, up to and
                including the Inquiry’s formal setting-up date, 28 June 2022”.2

A1.3.     To ensure a full and focused examination of the wide range of issues covered in the
          Terms of Reference and to produce regular reports, the Inquiry’s investigation has
          been divided into sections or ‘modules’. Each module gathers evidence, designates
          Core Participants and has both preliminary hearings (at which decisions about the
          procedure for the conduct of its investigations and public hearings are made) and full
          public hearings where evidence is heard. Details of public hearings are published by
          the Inquiry.3

A1.4.     The Inquiry’s public hearings concluded on 5 March 2026. Six investigation reports
          have already been published (see Table 2).

terms-of-reference), which includes translations of the Inquiry’s Terms of Reference; for the Inquiries Act 2005, see https://www.
legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2005/12/contents. A separate inquiry is taking place in Scotland, which will evaluate areas where policy was
devolved to the Scottish Government, as set out in its Terms of Reference. The UK Covid-19 Inquiry works with the Scottish Covid-19
Inquiry to avoid duplication of work where possible.

          Northern Ireland.7 The Inquiry uses the phrase ‘test, trace and isolate’ to refer to

Outline of Scope
A1.8.     In order to reflect the unique issues arising within each jurisdiction as a result of the
          pandemic, each module adopted its own Outline of Scope.8 In particular, the matters
          examined in Module 7 encompassed:

          • the test, trace and isolate policies and strategies developed and deployed,
              including modelling, system capacity, rationale for decisions and wider border
              policy considerations;
          • the availability, use and effectiveness of different test, trace and isolate
              technologies and policies, such as lateral flow tests, polymerase chain reaction
              (PCR) tests, testing for variants and digital contact tracing;
          • the structure of the test, trace and isolate system, the key decision-making bodies,
              the effectiveness and impact of adopted models, the use of the private sector and
              the associated costs;
          • enforcement of procedures and factors influencing public compliance, including
              the adequacy of messaging, trust, financial and practical support for isolation,
              and the use of data in decision-making; and
          • the preservation of infrastructure, capacity and research to improve and develop
              test, trace and isolate schemes for future pandemics.

A1.9.     Module 7 prepared a List of Issues to supplement the Provisional Outline of
          Scope, intended to provide greater detail about the matters which might warrant
          investigation. The List of Issues was intended as a guide and not to be prescriptive
          about the issues which would be addressed.


Core Participants
A1.10. In accordance with rule 5 of the Inquiry Rules 2006 and the Inquiry’s Core
       Participant Protocol, the Chair designates a number of Core Participants –
       individuals, organisations or institutions with a specific interest – in each module.9
       Core Participants have enhanced rights in the Inquiry process, including receiving
       disclosure of documents, being represented, making legal submissions and
       suggesting lines of enquiry. They are also able to apply to the Inquiry for funding
       to cover legal and other costs.

Evidence-gathering
A1.13. In Module 7, the Inquiry issued 233 requests for evidence, pursuant to rule 9 of the
       Inquiry Rules 2006, to organisations and individuals. It received and considered 200
       witness statements, which included 37 impact statements. In addition, in Module 7
       the Inquiry received and reviewed more than 23,000 documents, comprising
       exhibits, statements and documents forming part of general disclosure. These
       amounted to 159,497 pages. A total of more than 210,000 pages (which included
       documents provided to earlier modules and considered relevant to Module 7’s
       scope) were disclosed to Core Participants.

A1.14. In Module 7, the Inquiry sent requests to a number of individuals from Core
       Participant groups who were particularly affected by the Covid-19 pandemic for
       evidence about the impact it had on them. Those contacted included bereaved
       families, the Trades Union Congress and the Federation of Ethnic Minority
       Healthcare Organisations. A total of six witness statements were received from
       these groups and disclosed to Core Participants. Additional evidence regarding the
       impact of the pandemic on particular groups was obtained from Clinically Vulnerable
       Families, the Long Covid Groups and two disabled people’s organisations: Disability
       Rights UK and Disability Action Northern Ireland.


Every Story Matters
A1.15. As set out in the Terms of Reference, the Inquiry regards it as critical to its work to
       listen to and consider carefully the experiences of bereaved families and others who
       have suffered hardship or loss as a result of the pandemic. The Inquiry has done this
       in a number of different ways, including through its listening exercise, Every Story
       Matters, the largest public engagement exercise by a UK public inquiry. It heard
       from thousands of people about their experiences of the pandemic.

A1.16. Stories were shared with the Inquiry through a webform (including accessible
       versions), a series of events across the UK and interviews with under-represented
       groups.12 Those aged 18 or older were invited to share as much or as little of their
       pandemic experience as they felt able, without the formality of giving evidence or
       attending a public hearing. More than 58,000 stories were shared with the Inquiry.
       The sharing of these experiences has helped the Inquiry to understand events
       and their impact and has aided the development of recommendations that could
       reduce suffering in the future. Experiences shared with the Inquiry were analysed

      Disclosure to Core Participants and publication of materials
      A1.17. The Inquiry’s approach to documents is set out in its Protocol on Documents,
             which explains key principles for the delivery of documents to the Inquiry, including
             requests for documents or witness statements pursuant to rule 9 of the Inquiry
             Rules 2006.14 This should be read with the Inquiry’s Protocol on the Redaction
             of Documents, which details the approach to the redaction of documents for the
             purposes of both disclosure to Core Participants and publication.15

      A1.18. The Inquiry discloses all witness statements and documents it considers relevant
             to Core Participants in full, subject to any redactions applied in accordance with the
             Inquiry’s Protocol on the Redaction of Documents.16 To comply with section 18 of the
             Inquiries Act 2005, the Chair is taking reasonable steps to ensure that members of
             the public are able to view documents provided to the Inquiry and attend Inquiry
             hearings.17 All documents shown on screen during the course of the hearings appear
             in the YouTube recording of the evidence and are published on the Inquiry’s website
             at the end of each day. The witness statements of those who give evidence each day
             are also published in full. Since the conclusion of the hearings, the Chair has granted
             permission for the publication of further materials, including those referenced within
             this Report, where she has been satisfied it is necessary to do so.18

      A1.19. In the event of an objection to the disclosure or publication of relevant material,
             an application must be made to the Chair for a restriction order in accordance with
             section 19 of the Inquiries Act 2005, following the Inquiry’s Protocol on Applications
             for Restriction Orders.19 In Module 7, the Chair issued a restriction order dated 15 May
             2025 concerning the publication of sensitive and redacted material and a general
             restriction order dated 13 March 2026.20

Terminology and references
A1.26. The nature of the subject matter means that the evidence considered by the
       Inquiry contains technical and specialist language, which the Inquiry has tried to
       minimise in this Report. A number of witnesses and documents also used a range
       of abbreviations and acronyms. To avoid any confusion and to assist the reader, the
       Inquiry has set out names and other key phrases in full in this Report. A detailed
       glossary is also included at Appendix 2.

A1.27. Some terminology that is particularly key to understanding this Report is listed below
       for ease of reference.


Key terminology
A1.28. The virus that causes the coronavirus disease known as Covid-19 is SARS-CoV-2.
       However, where this specificity is not necessary, in accordance with the practice of
       the World Health Organization, the Inquiry uses ‘Covid-19’ to refer to both the virus
       and the disease.

A1.29. Although the first Covid-19 patients in the UK were announced on 31 January
       2020 and the outbreak was not characterised by the World Health Organization
       as a pandemic until 11 March 2020, for clarity this Report refers to the time period
       beginning with the arrival of Covid-19 in the UK as the ‘Covid-19 pandemic’.

A1.30. The Covid-19 pandemic required action by both the UK government and the
       devolved administrations. Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland each have a
       legislature and executive elected by their own electorates (referred to in this Report
       as the ‘devolved administrations’). Although each devolution settlement is different,
       each administration is responsible for a range of matters, including health, education
       and transport. England has no legislature of its own – instead, the UK Parliament
       legislates on both UK-wide, ‘reserved’ (ie not devolved) issues such as defence and
       foreign affairs and legislates for England on issues devolved to other nations. The UK
       Parliament also, at times, legislates for other groupings – for example, in England and
       Wales on issues of justice.


                                                                                                 223

=== PAGE 238 ===
      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      A1.31. The UK government is responsible for all aspects of government policy in England.
             At the time of the pandemic, the Department of Health and Social Care was
             responsible for policy on health and adult social care matters in England (and on
             a UK-wide basis for a few issues that are not otherwise devolved). Prior to January
             2018, the Department of Health and Social Care was known as the Department of
             Health – for clarity, the department is referred to throughout this Report using its
             current name.

      A1.32. ‘The NHS’ is the term used to refer collectively to the publicly funded healthcare
             systems in England, Scotland and Wales, comprising NHS England, NHS Scotland
             and the NHS in Wales (also known as NHS Wales). In Northern Ireland, the publicly
             funded healthcare system is Health and Social Care (Northern Ireland), with health
             and social care integrated under a single framework.

      A1.33. Prior to the pandemic, responsibility for responding to public health challenges in
             Scotland was divided between a number of different bodies. As part of the Public
             Health Reform programme, Public Health Scotland was launched on 1 April 2020.
             It brought together three legacy bodies: NHS Health Scotland, Health Protection
             Scotland (formerly part of NHS National Services Scotland) and the Information
             Services Division (also formerly part of NHS National Services Scotland). As a result,
             at an early stage of the pandemic, all staff and functions of those legacy bodies
             transferred to Public Health Scotland, with limited exceptions (the Antimicrobial
             Resistance and Healthcare Associated Infection function and a number of corporate
             services staff from NHS Health Scotland which remained within NHS National
             Services Scotland). Accordingly, questions relating to the public health response
             in Scotland were directed to both Public Health Scotland and NHS National
             Services Scotland.


      References
      A1.34. References such as ‘Alan McNally 15 May 2025 111/6-17’ or ‘INQ000587251_0077
             para 5.5.4’ in the footnotes of this Report relate to material that is available on the
             Inquiry’s website.

      A1.35. The transcripts of the Inquiry’s hearings are referenced by person, hearing date
             and internal page and line numbers. For example, ‘Alan McNally 15 May 2025
             111/6‑17’ refers to the evidence of Professor Alan McNally on 15 May 2025, page 111,
             lines 6 to 17.

      A1.36. Documentary evidence is referenced by the document’s number and, where
             relevant, page and paragraph numbers. For example, ‘INQ000587251_0077
             para 5.5.4’ refers to document INQ000587251, page 77, paragraph 5.5.4.

Term                    Description
Advisory Committee on   A scientific advisory committee of the Department of Health
Dangerous Pathogens     and Social Care. Its work includes providing the UK government
                        with independent scientific advice on the risks of exposure
                        to pathogens.
Airborne transmission   Transmission of a pathogen occurring across short or long
                        distances through the air from very small virus-containing
                        particles produced by an infected individual.
Antibody                A component of the immune system that is produced after
                        infection or vaccination with a pathogen and which identifies and
                        helps to neutralise future infections from the same pathogen.
Antibody (or            Detects antibodies to SARS-CoV-2 virus from a current or
serology) test          previous infection by means of a blood test.
Antigen                 A foreign substance which induces an immune response in the
                        body, especially the production of antibodies. Antigens are
                        unique to each pathogen. In this case, the foreign object is the
                        spike protein found on the surface of the SARS-CoV-2 virus.
Antigen test            Detects viral material indicating a current infection. Tests for
                        Covid-19 detect whether viral antigens found on the surface
                        of SARS-CoV-2 are present in a sample.
Application             A set of rules or protocols that enable software applications
programming             to communicate with each other to exchange data, features
interface (API)         and functionality.
Assay                   The analytical procedure used to qualitatively assess
                        or quantitatively measure the presence or amount of the
                        target entity.
Asymptomatic            Having an infection but not showing any symptoms.
Asymptomatic            Mass testing of individuals within a community with no symptoms
community testing       or those not showing or producing recognised symptoms.
Asymptomatic testing    The testing of individuals with no symptoms or those not
                        showing or producing recognised symptoms.
Behavioural science     The scientific study of human and animal behaviour, including
                        disciplines such as psychology, anthropology and sociology.
Cabinet                 A team of the most senior government ministers who are chosen
                        to lead on specific policy areas.
Cabinet Office          A ministerial UK government department, supported by 29
                        agencies and public bodies. Its role is to support the Prime
                        Minister, ensure the effective running of government and take
                        the lead in certain critical policy areas.
Centralised model       A system where contact data collected by an app are stored on
                        a central server.

      Term                       Description
      Chief Medical Officer      A qualified medical practitioner, the most senior government
                                 adviser on health matters, and the professional head of all
                                 directors of public health in local government and the medical
                                 profession in government. There is a separate Chief Medical
                                 Officer for England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland.
                                 The Chief Medical Officer for England is the UK government’s
                                 chief medical adviser.
      Chief Scientific Advisers Senior science advisers, working in most government
                                departments, who provide oversight and assurance of science
                                capability and activities.
      Clinically extremely       People identified by the UK government in March 2020 as
      vulnerable                 having medical conditions that meant they were particularly
                                 likely to develop severe illness or to die from Covid-19 infection.
      Clinically vulnerable      People identified by the UK government in March 2020
                                 as having medical conditions that might make them likely to
                                 develop severe illness from Covid-19 infection, although not
                                 with the same predictability or to the same extent as the
                                 clinically extremely vulnerable.
      Close contact              A person who was within two metres of an infected person for at
                                 least 15 minutes, or within one metre for a shorter period of time,
                                 within a specified window of time.
      COBR                       The UK government’s national crisis management centre for
                                 responding to whole-system civil emergencies. It provides the
                                 coordination mechanism through which the UK government
                                 responds to emergencies that require urgent decision-making.
                                 Its name was originally derived from its location in the Cabinet
                                 Office Briefing Rooms.
      Community transmission When a disease is spreading in the community and the specific
                             source is unknown (for instance, where it cannot be linked to a
                             traveller from abroad).
      Contact                    A person who has been within a specified distance of someone
      (see also close contact)   who has tested positive for an infection and so is at increased
                                 risk of having contracted that infection. The particular distance
                                 is dependent on the type of infection.

=== PAGE 243 ===
                                                                        Appendix 2: Glossary




Term                      Description
Contact tracing           Identifying the source and contacts linked to a confirmed case
                          of an infectious disease. A public health measure to contain the
                          spread of an infection. This includes:
                          • manual contact tracing – the use of trained human contact
                             tracers to speak with infected people to identify their
                             contacts;
                          • digital contact tracing – the use of a digital tool, such as a
                             mobile phone application (app), to trace contacts;
                          • backward contact tracing – seeking to identify the original
                             source of an infection by identifying the activities and
                             interactions of an infected person around the time they
                             are likely to have been infected; and
                          • forward contact tracing – identifying the contacts of an
                             infected person whom they are likely to have infected.
Containment level         Biological agents are categorised based on how hazardous they
                          are to people and the environment. Laboratories which handle
                          biological agents must meet minimum safety requirements to
                          work with them. Different containment levels require different
                          levels of safety and control.
Coronaviruses             A family of viruses that cause respiratory illnesses in people.
Covid-19                  The disease caused by the coronavirus SARS-CoV-2.
Covid-19 Advisory Group A group of experts that provided the Scottish Government with
                        scientific and technical advice on the Covid-19 response.
Covid-19 Bereaved         A group dedicated to campaigning for and giving a voice to
Families for Justice      those bereaved by Covid-19 in Wales.
Cymru
Covid-19 Bereaved         A UK-wide campaign group representing the interests of
Families for Justice UK   bereaved family members of individuals who died from Covid-19.
Covid-19 Operations       A Cabinet sub-committee that met from May 2020 to drive the
Committee (Covid-O)       policy and operational aspects of the Covid-19 response.
COVID-19 Rapid            A study carried out to investigate rates of adherence to the
Survey of Adherence       UK’s test, trace and isolate system between 2 March 2020
to Interventions and      and 5 August 2020.
Responses (CORSAIR)
Dashboard                 A visual display of data.
Decentralised model       A system where data collected by an app are stored on the
                          individual users’ devices rather than on a central server.

      administrations

      public health           public health responsibilities. They have primary responsibility

      acid)                   functioning of all known organisms and many viruses.

                              fluids. It causes a severe and often fatal haemorrhagic fever.

                              and isolate measures, effectiveness refers to how well the
                              intervention achieves the intended effect of reduced viral
                              transmission and controlling outbreaks. It takes into account
                              factors such as public compliance, test availability and accuracy,
                              and communication barriers.
      Emergency               Multi-agency groups in Northern Ireland set up to ensure
      preparedness groups     appropriate preparedness to enable an effective response

      Epidemic                A sudden increase in incidence of a disease that is higher than

      Term                       Description
      Health and Social Care     The publicly funded healthcare system in Northern Ireland, with
      (Northern Ireland)         health and social care integrated under a single framework.
      Healthcare systems         The system of healthcare in each of the four nations of the UK,
                                 comprising the NHS in England, the NHS in Wales (NHS Wales),
                                 NHS Scotland, and Health and Social Care (Northern Ireland).
      Health protection teams A part of the UK Health Security Agency. They consist of
                              professionally qualified consultants in health protection who
                              provide specialist public health advice and operational support
                              to the NHS, local authorities and other agencies.
      HM Treasury                A ministerial UK government department that acts as the
                                 economic and finance ministry, maintaining control over public
                                 spending and setting the direction of UK economic policy.
      Home Office                A UK government department, supported by 30 agencies and
                                 public bodies. The lead government department for immigration
                                 and passports, drugs policy, crime, counter-terrorism and police.
      Immunity                   The ability to defend the body from a pathogen’s infection.
                                 Acquired immunity describes how the body builds immunological
                                 memory – so that if the person is exposed to the same infection
                                 again, the body’s response is enhanced. This is the basis for
                                 immunisation with vaccines.
      Impact assessment          A process of assessment designed to ensure that the most
      (eg equality, children’s   significant risks are identified and taken into account when
      rights)                    making decisions.
      Incidence                  The number of new cases of a disease in a population during a
                                 particular time period. Calculating incidence rates can indicate
                                 how quickly an infectious disease is occurring in a population.
      Influenza (flu)            A viral respiratory infection that infects humans globally and
                                 several other host species. Causes both seasonal endemic
                                 waves of infection and, when new strains emerge against which
                                 the population has less immunity, causes more severe epidemics
                                 or pandemics.
      Interventions              Any activity undertaken with the objective of improving human
                                 health by preventing disease, by curing or reducing the severity
                                 or duration of an existing disease, or by restoring function lost
                                 through disease or injury.
      Key worker                 Someone whose work was critical to the Covid-19 response.
                                 This was relevant to testing eligibility and the ability of the
                                 children of such workers to attend early years and education
                                 settings during school closures.
      Lateral flow test (LFT)    A type of molecular test to detect an active infection. These tests
                                 do not require processing in a laboratory and results are
                                 available within minutes.

                                                                       Appendix 2: Glossary




Term                      Description
Lighthouse laboratories   A nationwide network of laboratories set up by the UK
                          government that specifically focused on Covid-19 testing
                          for NHS Test and Trace.
Local authority           An administrative body in local government that provides public
                          services in a local area.
Local Government          A national membership body for local authorities in England
Association               and Wales.
Lockdown                  A mandatory stay-at-home order, a legal prohibition placing
                          blanket restrictions on the whole population (apart from
                          specified activities) for the purpose of limiting the spread
                          of a disease.
Loop-mediated            A scientific technique to detect and increase the amount of viral
isothermal amplification genetic material – DNA. Equipment using LAMP technology can
(LAMP) testing           be sited near to the person being tested and can give results in
                         minutes instead of having to send samples for laboratory
                         processing.
Mass testing              Using tests in a large number of asymptomatic people to detect
                          those who are infected.
Medicines and             An executive agency of the Department of Health and Social
Healthcare products       Care. It regulates medicines, medical devices, including Covid-19
Regulatory Agency         testing kits, and blood components used in transfusions in the
(MHRA)                    UK. It decides whether to approve new medicines such as
                          vaccines.
Middle East respiratory   A serious illness caused by the Middle East respiratory virus
syndrome (MERS)           (MERS-CoV), a coronavirus first identified in 2012 in Saudi Arabia.
                          Largely zoological (camel to human transmission), limited human-
                          to-human transmission has been noted.
Mitigation                A strategy aiming to use limited but effective interventions
                          to delay and minimise the peak of waves of infections and to
                          reduce their size. The goal is primarily to spread pressure on
                          the healthcare system over a longer time period, while accepting
                          that a similar number of people will become infected eventually.
Modelling                 Statistical models are tools for analysing complex data,
                          identifying patterns, and informing evidence-based decision-
                          making where direct measurement is not possible. Figures from
                          modelling are estimates only, and different statistical models will
                          produce different outcomes. These estimates are not based on
                          direct observations. They are therefore limited by the underlying
                          assumptions used in the models and are subject to uncertainty.
Molecular test            A test that detects viral genetic material through PCR or RT-PCR
                          or newer laboratory techniques.
Mortality rate            An expression of the number of deaths for a given cause divided
                          by the whole population.

Term                     Description
Non-pharmaceutical       Non-drug measures to limit the transmission of an infectious
interventions            disease. These can be measures at the individual level, such as

                         improved hygiene measures. They can also be measures to
                         constrain activities, such as the closure of various premises,

                         environment and regional development.
Office for National      The UK’s largest independent producer of official statistics
Statistics               and the recognised national statistical institute of the UK.
Outbreak                 The occurrence of cases of disease in excess of what would
                         normally be expected in a defined community, geographical
                         area or season.
Pandemic                 An epidemic occurring worldwide, or over a very wide area,
                         crossing international boundaries and usually affecting a large
                         number of people.
Pathogens                Infectious organisms, such as viruses, bacteria or parasites,
                         that can produce a disease.
Polymerase chain         A specialised laboratory method used to increase the amount
reaction (PCR) test      of DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) or RNA (ribonucleic acid) in a
                         sample so that there is enough to test it. PCR tests are used
                         to detect RNA in samples from people to see if the samples
                         contain SARS-CoV-2 virus.
Prevalence               A measurement that expresses the proportion of people who
                         have a disease at or during a given time period. Prevalence rates
                         for disease are calculated by dividing the number of cases by
                         the total number of people in the sample.
Prime Minister of the UK The leader of the UK government, ultimately responsible for the
                         policy and decisions of the UK government.
Public health            The science and art of preventing disease, prolonging life
                         and promoting health through organised efforts of society.
Public Health Agency     Established under the Health and Social Care (Reform) Act
(Northern Ireland)       (Northern Ireland) 2009. Responsible for the improvement
                         of health and social wellbeing, reducing health inequalities in
                         Northern Ireland, health protection and service development.

      Term                     Description

                               wellbeing and reduce health inequalities in Wales.
      Reproduction number,     A measure of the reproductive rate of the virus: the average
      ‘R number’, ‘R’          number of people that a single infected person passes a virus
                               on to.
                               Generally refers to Rt (RT): the average number of people that a
                               single infected person passes the virus on to at a particular point
                               in time, taking into account current levels of immunity and the
                               extent of social mixing.
                               May alternatively refer to R0 (R0): the average number of people
                               that one infected person would pass the virus to in a population
                               with normal mixing behaviour and no prior immunity.
      Reverse transcription    Similar to loop-mediated isothermal amplification (LAMP) testing.
      loop-mediated            RT-LAMP is a scientific technique used to detect and increase
      isothermal amplification the amount of viral genetic material – RNA – for testing.
      (RT-LAMP) testing
      Reverse transcription    Similar to polymerase chain reaction (PCR), RT-PCR is a
      polymerase chain         molecular test. It detects RNA (ribonucleic acid) with high
      reaction (RT-PCR) test   accuracy and detects infection.
      RNA (ribonucleic acid)   A molecule with many biological functions, including carrying
                               a genetic template encoded by DNA so that it can be used to
                               make proteins. It is also used to encode genetic information
                               itself in many viruses, including SARS-CoV-2.
      Scientific Advisory      An advisory group convened to provide independent scientific
      Group for Emergencies    advice to support decision-making in COBR in the event of a
      (SAGE)                   national emergency.

      Modelling, Operational   up again during the Covid-19 pandemic.

                         for a person with a disease.

                         non-essential public transport, or recommending more home
                         working.

                         for a person who does not have a disease.

                         unpredictable and sudden increases in demand for healthcare
                         services.

=== PAGE 252 ===
  Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      Term                      Description
      Test, trace and isolate   Test, trace and isolate programmes identify infected people
                                and their contacts so that they can be advised to self-isolate.
                                Different models and names were adopted across the four
                                nations: Test and Protect (Scotland); NHS Test and Trace
                                (England); Test, Trace, Protect (Wales); and Test, Trace and
                                Protect (Northern Ireland). The Inquiry has adopted ‘test,
                                trace and isolate’ as shorthand for all of the models across
                                the four nations.
      Test, Trace and Protect   Northern Ireland’s programme for testing, tracing and isolation
                                during the Covid-19 pandemic. Led by the Department of Health
                                (Northern Ireland) and the Public Health Agency (Northern
                                Ireland). It was operational from 18 May 2020 and the
                                programme was scaled back from late April 2022.
      Test, Trace, Protect      The Welsh Government’s strategy for testing, tracing and
                                isolation during the Covid-19 pandemic. Launched by the
                                Welsh Government on 1 June 2020 and delivered by partners
                                including Public Health Wales. Operations were scaled back
                                from February 2022 and routine contact tracing stopped at the
                                end of June 2022.
      Transmission              The process by which a pathogen, such as a virus, spreads from
                                one infected person to another.
      UK government             The central government for the UK, headed by the Prime
                                Minister. The UK government is responsible for reserved
                                and excepted policy matters across the UK.
      UK Health Security        An executive agency, established in April 2021 and sponsored
      Agency (UKHSA)            by the Department of Health and Social Care, responsible for
                                public health protection and infectious disease capability.
      Vaccination               Protecting individuals from a disease by treating them with
                                a vaccine.
      Vaccine                   Vaccines train the immune system to recognise a pathogen
                                and to produce antibodies to defend the body from it at the
                                next encounter.
      Variant                   As a virus replicates, it can accumulate mutations. A version
                                of the virus with these mutations is called a ‘variant’. Mutations
                                may facilitate transmission or impact the effectiveness of
                                vaccines, treatments or immunity resulting from previous
                                infection. Notable Covid-19 variants during the pandemic
                                included Alpha, Delta and Omicron.
      Virus                     A parasitic infectious agent that replicates only inside the cells
                                of an organism.

=== PAGE 254 ===
Appendix 3: List of
recommendations

=== PAGE 255 ===
                                                    Appendix 3: List of recommendations




Chapter 2: Testing
    Recommendation 1: Monitor testing capabilities and prepare plans
    for rapid scale-up of test processing
    The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and the Northern
    Ireland Executive should compile information about the testing capabilities of
    academic, clinical and research laboratories, including the types of diagnostics
    they process and the equipment they use.
    This information should be updated regularly and shared between the four
    governments of the UK.
    Each government should also develop a plan, as part of their wider planning
    for pandemics, to enable the test processing capacity in these laboratories
    to be increased quickly in a public health emergency.



Chapter 3: Contact tracing
    Recommendation 2: Manual contact tracing during a public
    health emergency
    The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
    Ireland Executive should maintain the capability to deliver manual contact tracing
    using local public health expertise.
    Each government should also prepare plans to scale up manual contact tracing
    during a public health emergency. Plans should include:

    • a contact tracing model that incorporates local public health expertise;
    • the recruitment of a trained and appropriately sized workforce; and
    • a framework agreement for contact tracing services so that providers and staff
      can be engaged to deliver those services quickly.




                                                                                         241

=== PAGE 256 ===
      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




             Recommendation 3: Develop a digital contact tracing tool
             The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
             Ireland Executive should develop a single digital tool to support forward and
             backward contact tracing across the UK in a public health emergency.
             The tool should include:

             • mechanisms to record contacts;
             • mechanisms to receive automatic contact tracing notifications; and
             • wider test, trace and isolate system features such as a symptom
               tracking feature.
             The tool should be developed in consultation with regional, local, community
             and voluntary bodies to ensure that it is accessible. It should be regularly tested
             and updated.



      Chapter 4: Isolation
             Recommendation 4: Local support for self-isolation
             Local resilience forums, regional resilience partnerships and emergency
             preparedness groups should, as part of the planning required by the Civil
             Contingencies Act 2004, prepare plans now for the scale-up of practical
             support for their community if self-isolation is required during a future pandemic.
             Plans should be tailored to the needs of local communities, including groups
             that may face challenges in adhering to self-isolation measures.
             The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
             Ireland Executive should review and monitor progress of the development of
             these local support plans, as well as monitor their delivery in a future pandemic.




242

=== PAGE 257 ===
                                                Appendix 3: List of recommendations




Recommendation 5: Financial support to self-isolate
The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
Ireland Executive should put in place financial support schemes for individuals
complying with self-isolation rules during a pandemic.
The following principles should apply:

• There should be clear and straightforward guidance on eligibility criteria,
  application processes and payment arrangements for the support schemes.

• Information about the introduction of support schemes and how to access them
  should be communicated clearly and made available in accessible formats.

• Mechanisms should be in place to monitor the effectiveness of support
  schemes.
Plans should also be developed and maintained to enable these financial support
schemes to be scaled up rapidly during a pandemic.




                                                                                  243

=== PAGE 258 ===
      Module 7: Test, trace and isolate




      Chapter 5: Border control measures
             Recommendation 6: A strategy for international border control
             measures during a pandemic
             The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
             Ireland Executive should, in consultation with Border Force, produce a UK-wide
             strategy for the use of international border control measures during a future
             pandemic. This strategy should be informed by the experience of the Covid-19
             pandemic, including international examples of good practice.
             The strategy should include:

             • an analysis of different border control measures and the scenarios in which
               they will be effective and proportionate;

             • mechanisms to support early decision-making (including cooperation between
               the four nations of the UK) when border control measures may be required;

             • mechanisms to support the early implementation of border control measures
               when they are required;

             • plans for the enforcement of border control measures, including methods
               to support Border Force with enforcement; and

             • plans for the effective communication of border control measures to the UK
               public and international travellers.
             The strategy should ensure that the four nations are aligned on decisions about
             the measures that will be considered for deployment in different circumstances.



      Chapter 6: Communications and data
             Recommendation 7: Aligned test, trace and isolate communications
             The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
             Ireland Executive should develop aligned communications to support adherence
             to test, trace and isolate measures to control disease spread in a public health
             emergency.
             The four nations of the UK should coordinate as far as possible while considering
             any region-specific challenges or differences in approach to test, trace and isolate.
             Communications should be informed by behavioural science principles and
             tested as part of UK-wide pandemic response exercises (as recommended in the
             Inquiry’s Module 1 Report). The effectiveness of communications during a public
             health emergency should be monitored.


244

=== PAGE 259 ===
                                                     Appendix 3: List of recommendations




    Recommendation 8: Improved data collection and evaluation of test,
    trace and isolate measures
    The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
    Ireland Executive should collect robust data to monitor and evaluate test, trace
    and isolate measures introduced during a future pandemic.
    Qualitative and quantitative data relating to test, trace and isolate measures
    should be collected on public awareness, adherence and the impact of those
    measures on the public.
    The data collected should allow governments to assess differing impacts across
    groups to improve test, trace and isolate measures.
    Data collection should be aligned as far as possible across the four nations of the
    UK and should be shared within and across governments.



Chapter 7: An effective future test, trace and
isolate system
    Recommendation 9: A new UK-wide test, trace and isolate system
    The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
    Ireland Executive should work together to develop one system to find, test, trace,
    isolate and support cases of infection during a public health emergency.
    The system should be accessible to all population groups and adaptable
    to account for a range of pathogen types and the different stages of response
    to a public health emergency. It should be developed in consultation with local
    authorities, health boards and trusts, as well as experts in modelling, behavioural
    science and public health.
    This should form part of the civil emergency strategy recommended in the
    Inquiry’s Module 1 Report and the system should be tested during UK-wide
    pandemic response exercises.
